Queequeg caught one of these young saplings mimicking him behind his back. I thought the bumpkin’s hour of doom was come. Dropping his harpoon, the brawny savage caught him in his arms, and by an almost miraculous dexterity and strength, sent him high up bodily into the air; then slightly tapping his stern in mid-somerset, the fellow landed with bursting lungs upon his feet; whereupon Queequeg, turning his back upon him, lighted his tomahawk pipe and passed it to me for a puff. The Captain stormed; Queequeg twisted his tattooed face: “him bevy small-e fish-e; Queequeg no kill-e so small-e fish-e; Queequeg kill-e big whale!” Then the mainsail’s weather-sheet parted; the boom flew from side to side, sweeping the after deck; the greenhorn went overboard; all hands panic-struck. Queequeg dropped to his knees, crawled under the boom’s path, whipped hold of a rope, secured one end, and flinging the other like a lasso, caught it round the spar as it swept over his head; at the next jerk it was trapped, and all was safe. The schooner luffed; while the hands cleared away the stern boat, Queequeg, stripped to the waist, sprang from the side with a long living arc, swam through the freezing foam, then shot perpendicularly, glanced around, and dived. He rose again, one arm striking out, the other dragging a lifeless form. They were picked up; the bumpkin restored; the captain begged his pardon. From that hour I clove to Queequeg like a barnacle—yea, till poor Queequeg took his last long dive.

Was there ever such unconsciousness? He did not seem to think that he at all deserved a medal from the Humane and Magnanimous Societies. He only asked for water—fresh water—something to wipe the brine off; then, dry clothes, pipe, and mild eyes that seemed to say—“It’s a mutual, joint-stock world, in all meridians. We cannibals must help these Christians.”

After a fine run we arrived in Nantucket.

Nantucket! Take out your map and look at it. See what a real corner of the world it occupies; how it stands there, away off shore, more lonely than the Eddystone lighthouse; a mere elbow of sand; all beach, without a background. Some gamesome wights will tell you that they import Canada thistles; that people there plant toadstools before their houses, to get under the shade in summer time; that one blade of grass makes an oasis, three blades in a day’s walk a prairie; that they are so shut up, belted about, every way inclosed, surrounded, and made an utter island of by the ocean, that to their very chairs and tables small clams will sometimes be found adhering, as to the backs of sea turtles. But these extravaganzas only show that Nantucket is no Illinois.

Look now at the wondrous traditional story of how this island was settled by the red-men. In olden times an eagle swooped down upon the New England coast, and carried off an infant Indian in his talons. The parents resolved to follow in the same direction; setting out in their canoes, after a perilous passage they discovered the island, and there they found an empty ivory casket,—the poor little Indian’s skeleton.

What wonder, then, that these Nantucketers, born on a beach, should take to the sea for a livelihood! They first caught crabs and quohogs in the sand; grown bolder, they waded out with nets for mackerel; more experienced, they pushed off in boats and captured cod; and at last, launching a navy of great ships on the sea, explored this watery world; put an incessant belt of circumnavigations round it; and in all seasons and all oceans declared everlasting war with the mightiest animated mass that has survived the flood; most monstrous and most mountainous! That Himmalehan, salt-sea Mastodon, clothed with such portentousness of unconscious power, that his very panics are more to be dreaded than his most fearless and malicious assaults!

And thus have these naked Nantucketers, these sea hermits, issuing from their ant-hill in the sea, overrun and conquered the watery world like so many Alexanders; parcelling out among them the Atlantic, Pacific, and Indian oceans, as the three pirate powers did Poland. Let America add Mexico to Texas, and pile Cuba upon Canada; let the English overswarm all India, and hang out their blazing banner from the sun; two thirds of this terraqueous globe are the Nantucketer’s. For the sea is his; he owns it, as Emperors own empires; other seamen having but a right of way through it. The Nantucketer, he alone resides and riots on the sea; he alone, in Bible language, goes down to it in ships; to and fro ploughing it as his own special plantation. There is his home; there lies his business; a Noah’s flood would not interrupt it. He lives on the sea, as prairie cocks in the prairie; he hides among the waves, he climbs them as chamois hunters climb the Alps. For years he knows not the land; when he comes to it at last, it smells like another world. With the landless gull that at sunset folds her wings and sleeps between billows; so at nightfall, out of sight of land, he furls his sails and lays him to his rest, while under his very pillow rush herds of walruses and whales.

It was quite late when the little Moss came snugly to anchor; so we could attend to no business but a supper and a bed. Peter Coffin had sent us to his cousin Hosea Hussey of the Try Pots, famous for chowders; but his sea-directions—keep the yellow warehouse to starboard till you open a white church to larboard, then keep that to larboard till three points starboard, and then ask—set us beating about in the dark till we came to something there was no mistaking.

Two enormous wooden pots painted black, suspended by asses’ ears, swung from the cross-trees of an old top-mast before an old doorway; the other horns sawed, so the spar looked not a little like a gallows. Perhaps I was over sensitive, but as I gazed up to the two remaining horns—one for Queequeg, and one for me, thinks I—there came a vague misgiving. A Coffin for my Innkeeper; tombstones in the whalemen’s chapel; and here a gallows and a pair of prodigious black pots! Are these throwing out oblique hints touching Tophet?

A freckled woman in a yellow gown under a dull red lamp, briskly scolding a man in a purple woollen shirt—“Get along with ye, or I’ll be combing ye!” “There’s Mrs. Hussey,” said I. Mr. Hosea being from home, she was entirely competent; she ushered us to a table spread with the relics of a late repast, turned, and said—“Clam or Cod?”

“What’s that about Cods, ma’am?” said I, with much politeness. “Clam or Cod?” she repeated. “A clam for supper? a cold clam; is that what you mean, Mrs. Hussey? but that’s a rather cold and clammy reception in the winter time, ain’t it, Mrs. Hussey?” But she, eager to resume scolding the purple-shirted man, seemed to hear nothing but “clam,” bawled “clam for two,” and disappeared. “Queequeg,” said I, “do you think that we can make out a supper for us both on one clam?”

However, a warm savory steam belied that cheerless prospect; and when the smoking chowder came in, the mystery was delightfully explained. Oh, sweet friends! hearken to me. It was made of small juicy clams, scarcely bigger than hazel nuts, mixed with pounded ship biscuit, and salted pork cut up into little flakes; the whole enriched with butter, and plentifully seasoned with pepper and salt. Our appetites sharpened by the frosty voyage, and the chowder being surpassingly excellent, we despatched it with expedition; then, bethinking me of Mrs. Hussey’s “clam and cod,” I stepped to the kitchen door, uttered “cod” with great emphasis, and in due time a fine cod-chowder was placed before us.

We resumed business; and while plying our spoons in the bowl, thinks I to myself, I wonder now if this here has any effect on the head? What’s that stultifying saying about chowder-headed people? “But look, Queequeg, ain’t that a live eel in your bowl? Where’s your harpoon?”

Fishiest of all fishy places was the Try Pots: chowder for breakfast, and chowder for dinner, and chowder for supper, till you looked for fish-bones coming through your clothes. The area before the house was paved with clam-shells. Mrs. Hussey wore a polished necklace of codfish vertebra; Hosea Hussey had his account books bound in superior old shark-skin. There was a fishy flavor to the milk, too, which I could not at all account for, till one morning happening to take a stroll along the beach among some fishermen’s boats, I saw Hosea’s brindled cow feeding on fish remnants, and marching along the sand with each foot in a cod’s decapitated head, looking very slip-shod, I assure ye.

Supper concluded, we received a lamp and directions bedward; but as Queequeg was about to precede me upstairs, the lady reached forth her arm and demanded his harpoon; she allowed no harpoon in her chambers. “Why not?” said I; “every true whaleman sleeps with his harpoon—but why not?” “Because it’s dangerous,” says she. “Ever since young Stiggs coming from that unfort’nt v’y’ge of his, when he was gone four years and a half, with only three barrels of ile, was found dead in my first floor back, with his harpoon in his side; ever since then I allow no boarders to take sich dangerous weepons in their rooms at night.”

“But the chowder; clam or cod to-morrow for breakfast, men?” “Both,” says I; “and let’s have a couple of smoked herring by way of variety.”

In bed we concocted plans; but Queequeg, consulting Yojo—his black little god—declared that Yojo strongly enjoined the choice of ship should rest wholly with me; Yojo had pitched upon a vessel which, left to myself, I should infallibly light upon; in that vessel I must immediately ship, even for the present irrespective of Queequeg. This I misliked; I had relied on the savage’s sagacity; but he fasted with Yojo that day, and I sallied out alone among the shipping.

Three ships were up for three-years’ voyages: the Devil-dam, the Tit-bit, and the Pequod—named after the extinct tribe. I peered about the first two; then went aboard the Pequod and decided this was the very ship.

You may have seen quaint craft—square-toed luggers, Japanese junks—but never such a rare old Pequod: old-school, claw-footed; long seasoned and weather-stained; her old hull’s complexion darkened like a French grenadier’s who has alike fought in Egypt and Siberia; her venerable bows bearded; her masts—cut somewhere on the coast of Japan—stood stiffly up like the spines of the three old kings of Cologne; her ancient decks were worn and wrinkled, like the pilgrim-worshipped flag-stone in Canterbury Cathedral where Becket bled. To these antiquities were added marvellous features of her wild business. Old Captain Peleg—once chief mate, now a principal owner—had inlaid her grotesqueness with trophies: apparelled like a barbaric Ethiopian emperor, pendants of polished ivory; a cannibal of a craft, tricking herself in the chased bones of her enemies. All round, her open bulwarks were garnished like one continuous jaw, the long sharp teeth of sperm whales for pins; her running-gear travelled over sheaves of sea-ivory; scornful of a wheel, she sported a tiller carved in one mass from the long narrow lower jaw of her hereditary foe. The helmsman in a tempest felt like a Tartar clutching his steed’s jaw. A noble craft, but somehow a most melancholy! All noble things are touched with that.

Aft of the mainmast stood a strange sort of tent, or rather wigwam—of conical slabs of right-whale bone laced together, their loose hairy fibres waving to and fro like the top-knot on some old Pottowottamie Sachem’s head; a triangular opening faced towards the bows. Half concealed within sat an elderly, brown, brawny man, in blue pilot-cloth in the Quaker cut; there was a fine and almost microscopic net-work of the minutest wrinkles interlacing round his eyes, from continual sailings in many hard gales, and always looking to windward—Captain Peleg. “Supposing it be the captain of the Pequod, what dost thou want?” he demanded. I said I would ship. He damned the “marchant service,” flourished his leg—“I’ll take that leg away from thy stern if ever thou talkest the marchant service to me again”—and plied me with suspicions and jests about pirates and murderers. At last, “What takes thee a-whaling?” “To see what it is; to see the world,” I said. “Clap eye on Captain Ahab, young man, and thou wilt find he has but one leg,” he cried. “Was the other lost by a whale?” “Devoured—chewed up—crunched by the monstrousest parmacetty that ever chipped a boat!”

He sent me forward to peep over the weather-bow. The ship pointed seaward; an unlimited, monotonous prospect; “nothing but water—and a squall coming.” “Well, what dost thou think then of seeing the world? Can’t ye see the world where you stand?” But go a-whaling I must, and he would ship me.

Below, in the small cabin, sat Captain Bildad—tall, gaunt, drab to the chin, spectacles on nose—reading a ponderous Scripture. “Bildad,” cried Captain Peleg, “at it again, Bildad, eh? Ye have been studying those Scriptures, now, for the last thirty years, to my certain knowledge. How far ye got, Bildad?” As if long used to such profane talk, Bildad, without noticing it, quietly looked up, then went on spelling at his book. Like many Nantucketers, both were Quakers; but some of that sect are the most sanguinary of all whale-hunters—fighting Quakers—with a vengeance. And when these things unite in a man of greatly superior natural force, with a globular brain and a ponderous heart; who has also by the stillness and seclusion of many long night-watches in the remotest waters been led to think untraditionally and independently; receiving all nature’s sweet or savage impressions fresh from her own virgin breast, and thereby chiefly to learn a bold and nervous lofty language—that man makes one in a whole nation’s census—a mighty pageant creature, formed for noble tragedies. Nor will it detract from him, dramatically regarded, if there be a half-wilful overruling morbidness at the bottom of his nature. For all men tragically great are made so through a certain morbidness. Be sure of this, O young ambition, all mortal greatness is but disease. But, as yet we have not to do with such an one, but with quite another; and still a man, peculiar only as another phase of the Quaker. Bildad, retired from the sea and a principal owner, had the name of an incorrigible old hunks; hard-hearted in discipline though he never swore—getting unmitigated hard work out of men with the drab eye alone. His religion refused arms ashore, yet he had spilled tuns upon tuns of leviathan gore afloat. Very probably he had come to the sensible conclusion that a man’s religion is one thing, this practical world another. This world pays dividends.

“Dost thee?” said Bildad, in a hollow tone. “I dost,” said I unconsciously, he was so intense a Quaker. Peleg spread the ship’s articles. In whaling they pay no wages; all hands receive shares called lays, proportioned to duty. A green hand like me might hope for the 275th lay; a long lay, but something. While Peleg hacked at his pen, Bildad mumbled over his book—“Lay not up for yourselves treasures upon earth, where moth—” “Well, Captain Bildad,” cried Peleg, “what lay shall we give this young man?” “The seven hundred and seventy-seventh wouldn’t be too much,” said Bildad—“where moth and rust do corrupt, but lay—” “Blast your eyes, Bildad,” roared Peleg, “thou dost not want to swindle this young man! He must have more than that. I put him down for the three hundredth.” Bildad lifted his drab face: widows and orphans must be considered; too abundant rewards take bread from them—the 777th lay, Captain Peleg. Then flame met drab. “Fiery pit! fiery pit! ye insult me, man; tell me I’m bound to hell, and I’ll—yes, I’ll swallow a live goat with all his hair and horns on! Out of the cabin, ye canting, drab-coloured son of a wooden gun!” The squall blew by; “Whew!” whistled Peleg—“mend that pen, will ye, Bildad.” And so I was set down for the three hundredth lay.

I asked leave to bring a friend next day. “Fetch him,” said Peleg. Signed, I climbed on deck, but bethought me—the captain remained unseen. Often a whale-ship is fitted and crewed ere the captain arrives; yet see him one should. “Where is Captain Ahab?” I asked. Peleg frowned: Ahab kept close; not sick, not well; a queer man, so some think, but a good one. “Oh, thou’lt like him well enough. He’s a grand, ungodly, god-like man; doesn’t speak much; but when he does—listen. Ahab’s above the common; colleges and cannibals both have schooled him; his lance the keenest out of all our isle. He ain’t Captain Bildad; he ain’t Captain Peleg; he’s Ahab, boy; and Ahab of old, thou knowest, was a crowned king!” “And a very vile one,” said I; “did not the dogs lick his blood?” Peleg’s eye fixed me. “Never say that on board the Pequod. His mother, crazy and widowed, named him; fools have called it prophetic. ’Tis a lie. I know him; I sailed with him; a good man—not pious-good like Bildad, but a swearing good man—something like me, only more of him. He was never very jolly; and on the passage home he was a little out of his mind—a spell from the sharp shooting pains in his bleeding stump. Ever since he lost his leg last voyage by that accursed whale, he’s been moody—desperate moody and savage sometimes; but that will pass. Better sail with a moody good captain than a laughing bad one. And remember: he has a wife—not three-voyages wed—a sweet, resigned girl; a child, by that sweet girl. Stricken, blasted, if he be, Ahab has his humanities!”

As I walked away, I was full of thoughtfulness; what had been incidentally revealed to me of Captain Ahab, filled me with a certain wild vagueness of painfulness concerning him. And somehow, at the time, I felt a sympathy and a sorrow for him, but for I don’t know what, unless it was the cruel loss of his leg. And yet I also felt a strange awe of him; but that sort of awe, which I cannot at all describe, was not exactly awe; I do not know what it was. But I felt it; and it did not disincline me towards him. However, my thoughts were at length carried in other directions, so that for the present dark Ahab slipped my mind.

Queequeg’s Ramadan—his day of Fasting and Humiliation—was to last till nightfall. I respect all men’s obligations, never mind how comical, and could not find it in my heart to undervalue even a congregation of ants worshipping a toad-stool; or those other creatures in certain parts of our earth, who with a degree of footmanism quite unprecedented in other planets, bow down before the torso of a deceased landed proprietor merely on account of the inordinate possessions yet owned and rented in his name. We good Presbyterian Christians should be charitable in these things, and not fancy ourselves so vastly superior because of other mortals’ half-crazy conceits. There was Queequeg, now, certainly entertaining the most absurd notions about Yojo and his Ramadan;—but what of that? Queequeg thought he knew what he was about, I suppose; he seemed to be content; and there let him rest. Heaven have mercy on us all—Presbyterians and Pagans alike—for we are all somehow dreadfully cracked about the head, and sadly need mending.

Towards evening I knocked; no answer. The door was fast within. Peering the key-hole I saw—strange!—the wooden shaft of his harpoon leaning by the wall, though Mrs. Hussey had taken it overnight. Then surely he was inside. “Apoplexy!” cried the chamber-maid; down came Mrs. Hussey with mustard-pot and vinegar-cruet. “The axe! the axe!” I cried. She thrust out her countenance—“Do you talk of prying open any of my doors?” She felt in the closet under the landing—Queequeg’s harpoon was missing. “He’s killed himself! Paint me a sign: ‘no suicides permitted here, and no smoking in the parlor.’” Her key turned; the supplemental bolt held. I ran for a start; she caught at me; I dashed—crash went the door; and there sat Queequeg, cool and self-collected, squatting on his hams, holding Yojo on his head like a carved image.

All we said, we could drag out no word. He had sat so eight or ten hours, fasting. I wondered if this could possibly be a part of his Ramadan; do they fast on their hams that way in his native island? It can’t last for ever, and his Ramadan only comes once a year. I left him; supped; listened to plum-pudding tales till near eleven; returned—there he squatted still. I besought him to shake himself; no reply. I threw my bearskin over him against the cold night and lay down; Think of it; sleeping all night in the same room with a wide awake pagan on his hams in this dreary, unaccountable Ramadan! But at last I slept. With dawn’s first glimpse he rose—stiff joints—limped to me, pressed his forehead to mine: his Ramadan was over.

Now, as I before hinted, I have no objection to any person’s religion, be it what it may, so long as that person does not kill or insult any other person, because that other person don’t believe it also. But when a man’s religion becomes really frantic; when it is a positive torment to him; and, in fine, makes this earth of ours an uncomfortable inn to lodge in; then I think it high time to take that individual aside and argue the point with him. I lectured him—rise and progress of primitive religions; present sects; how Lents, Ramadans, and prolonged ham-squattings in cold rooms are stark nonsense; bad for health; useless for soul; opposed to common sense. Besides, fasting makes the body cave in; hence the spirit caves in; and all thoughts born of a fast must necessarily be half-starved. This is the reason why most dyspeptic religionists cherish such melancholy notions about their hereafters. In one word, hell is an idea first born on an undigested apple-dumpling; and since then perpetuated through the hereditary dyspepsias nurtured by Ramadans. Had he dyspepsia? “Once,” he said—after his father the king’s great feast: fifty enemies killed, cooked and eaten that evening. “No more,” I cried, shuddering; I knew the rest.

My sermon little moved him; he looked at me with a sort of condescending concern and compassion, as though he thought it a great pity that such a sensible young man should be so hopelessly lost to evangelical pagan piety. At last we dressed; and Queequeg, taking a hearty breakfast of chowders, we sallied toward the Pequod, picking our teeth with halibut bones.

As we walked down the wharf, Queequeg carrying his harpoon, Captain Peleg hailed from his whale-bone wigwam, saying he let no cannibals aboard unless they showed papers. “He must show that he’s converted,” added Captain Bildad’s hollow voice, thrusting his head from behind. “Son of darkness, art thou at present in communion with any Christian church?”

“He’s a member of the First Congregational Church,” said I. “First Congregational—Deacon Deuteronomy Coleman’s?” cried Bildad, taking out his spectacles and peering at Queequeg. “Not very long a member, I guess.” “Nor baptized right,” said Peleg, “or it would have washed some of that devil’s blue off his face.” “Young man,” said Bildad sternly, “thou art skylarking with me—explain thyself, thou young Hittite. What church dost thee mean? answer me.” Finding myself hard pushed, I replied: “I mean, sir, the same ancient Catholic Church to which you and I, and Captain Peleg there, and Queequeg here, and all of us, and every mother’s son and soul of us belong; the great and everlasting First Congregation of this whole worshipping world; only some of us cherish queer crotchets no ways touching the grand belief; in that we all join hands.” “Splice, thou mean’st splice hands,” cried Peleg, drawing nearer. “Young man, you’d better ship for a missionary, instead of a fore-mast hand; I never heard a better sermon. Deacon Deuteronomy—why Father Mapple himself couldn’t beat it, and he’s reckoned something. Come aboard, come aboard; never mind about the papers. Tell Quohog there—what’s his name?—to step along. By the great anchor, what a harpoon! Quohog, ever stand in a whale-boat’s head? ever strike a fish?”

Without a word, Queequeg leaped to a boat in the davits; bracing his left knee, he poised his iron: “Cap’ain, you see him small drop tar on water dere? you see him?—well, spose him one whale eye—well, den!” He darted—the iron hissed over Bildad’s broad brim; the glistening spot vanished. “Spos-ee him whale-e eye; why, dad whale dead.”

“Quick, Bildad,” said Peleg, retreating aghast, “get the ship’s papers. We must have Hedgehog—Quohog—in one of our boats. The ninetieth lay, and that’s more than ever was given a harpooneer yet out of Nantucket.”

Down we went; soon Queequeg was enrolled with me. “Quohog there don’t write, does he?” said Peleg. “Dost thou sign thy name or make thy mark?” Unabashed, Queequeg took the pen and copied in the proper place the queer round figure tattooed on his arm—so that, through Peleg’s obstinate mistake, it stood: Quohog. his X mark.

Meanwhile Bildad, steadfastly eyeing him, rose and drew from his drab pockets a tract—“The Latter Day Coming; or No Time to Lose”—and, grasping book and hands together, said, “Son of darkness, I must do my duty by thee; if thou still clingest to thy Pagan ways, which I sadly fear, I beseech thee, remain not for aye a Belial bondsman. Spurn the idol Bell; turn from the wrath to come; mind thine eye; steer clear of the fiery pit!” Something of the salt sea yet lingered in old Bildad’s language, heterogeneously mixed with Scriptural and domestic phrases.

“Avast there, Bildad,” cried Peleg, “avast spoiling our harpooneer. Pious harpooneers never make good voyagers—it takes the shark out of ’em; no harpooneer is worth a straw who aint pretty sharkish. There was young Nat Swaine, once the bravest boat-header; he joined the meeting, and never came to good—sheered from whales for fear of after-claps.”

“Peleg!” said Bildad, lifting eyes and hands, “thou thyself hast seen perilous times; when this same Pequod had her three masts overboard in that typhoon on Japan, did’st thou not think of Death and the Judgment then?”

“Hear him, hear him now,” cried Peleg, marching the cabin, hands deep in pockets. “Think of that! When every moment we thought the ship would sink! Death and the Judgment then? What? With all three masts making such an everlasting thundering against the side; and every sea breaking over us, fore and aft. Think of Death and the Judgment then? No! no time to think about Death then. Life was what Captain Ahab and I was thinking of; and how to save all hands—how to rig jury-masts—how to get into the nearest port; that was what I was thinking of.”

Bildad buttoned up and stalked on deck; we followed. He stood quietly overlooking the sailmakers—now and then stooping to save a patch, or an end of tarred twine.

“Shipmates, have ye shipped in that ship?” said a ragged stranger, levelling his massive forefinger at the Pequod. He was but shabbily apparelled in faded jacket and patched trowsers; a rag of a black handkerchief investing his neck. A confluent small-pox had in all directions flowed over his face, and left it like the complicated ribbed bed of a torrent, when the rushing waters have been dried up.

“Anything down there about your souls?” “Oh, perhaps you hav’n’t got any,” he said quickly. “No matter though, I know many chaps that hav’n’t got any,—good luck to ’em; and they are all the better off for it. A soul’s a sort of a fifth wheel to a wagon.”

“_He’s_ got enough, though, to make up for all deficiencies of that sort in other chaps,” abruptly said the stranger, placing a nervous emphasis upon the word _he_. “About him—Old Thunder. Ye hav’n’t seen him yet, have ye?”

“Who’s Old Thunder?”

“Captain Ahab.”

He thrust out his finger like a bayonet. “When Captain Ahab is all right, then this left arm of mine will be all right; not before. But nothing about that thing that happened to him off Cape Horn, long ago, when he lay like dead for three days and nights; nothing about that deadly skrimmage with the Spaniard afore the altar in Santa?—heard nothing about that, eh? Nothing about the silver calabash he spat into? And nothing about his losing his leg last voyage, according to the prophecy?”

“They didn’t tell much,” said I. “Only that he’s a good whale-hunter and a good captain.”

“That’s true—both true. But jump when he gives an order. Step and growl; growl and go.”

He stood pointing at the Pequod, as if in troubled reverie. “Ye’ve shipped, have ye? Well, well, what’s signed, is signed; and what’s to be, will be; and then again, perhaps it won’t be, after all. Anyhow, it’s all fixed and arranged a’ready; and some sailors or other must go with him, I suppose; as well these as any other men, God pity ’em! Morning to ye, shipmates; the ineffable heavens bless ye! I’m sorry I stopped ye.”

“If you have anything important, out with it,” said I.

“Ye are just the man for him—the likes of ye. Oh! when ye get there, tell ’em I’ve concluded not to make one of ’em.”

“Stop—your name?”

“Elijah.”

Elijah! We went on, counting him a humbug bugbear. Yet, turning a corner, I glanced back and saw him dogging us at a distance; I said nothing to Queequeg, but watched if he would turn where we turned—he did. This circumstance, coupled with his ambiguous, half-hinting, half-revealing, shrouded sort of talk, now begat in me all kinds of vague wonderments and half-apprehensions, all connected with the Pequod; and Captain Ahab; and the leg he had lost; and the Cape Horn fit; and the silver calabash; and the prediction of the squaw Tistig; and the voyage we had bound ourselves to sail; and a hundred other shadowy things. Crossing over, we retraced our steps to try him; he passed by, not seeming to notice us. This relieved me, and finally, in my heart, I pronounced him—a humbug.

A day or two passed; all was astir aboard the Pequod. Old sails were mended; new canvas and rigging came aboard. Captain Peleg seldom went ashore, roaring from his whalebone den; Bildad marked arrivals.

Word ran that chests must be on board by night; the ship did not sail for several days. Every one knows what a multitude of things—beds, sauce-pans, knives and forks, shovels and tongs, and what not—are indispensable to housekeeping. Whaling is three-years’ housekeeping upon the wide ocean; spare boats, spare spars, spare lines and harpoons—spare everythings, almost, but a spare Captain and duplicate ship.

Chief among the fetchers was Bildad’s sister—Aunt Charity. Never did any woman better deserve her name—Charity, Aunt Charity as everybody called her. And like a sister of charity did this charitable Aunt Charity bustle about hither and thither; now a jar of pickles; now quills for the mate’s log; then a roll of flannel for some rheumatic back; last of all, a long oil-ladle in one hand and a still longer lance in the other. It was a ship in which her beloved brother Bildad was concerned, and in which she herself owned a score or two of well-saved dollars.

Often I asked after Captain Ahab: “Getting better—expected aboard every day,” they said. If I had been downright honest with myself, I would have seen plainly in my heart that I did but half fancy being committed this way to so long a voyage, without once laying my eyes on the man who was to be the absolute dictator of it; but when a man suspects any wrong, it sometimes happens that if he be already involved in the matter, he insensibly strives to cover up his suspicions even from himself. And much this way it was with me. I said nothing, and tried to think nothing.

At last it was given out the ship would certainly sail next day. So, at early dawn, Queequeg and I set forth.

Grey, misty dawn; sailors ran ahead.

“Avast!” cried a voice; a hand fell on our shoulders; a figure peered between us. Elijah.

“Going aboard?”

“Yes; and what’s that to you?”

“Coming back afore breakfast?” he wondered.

“He’s cracked—come on.”

“Holloa!” cried stationary Elijah, hailing us when we had removed a few paces.

“Never mind him,” said I. “Come on.”

But he stole up to us again, and suddenly clapping his hand on my shoulder, said—“Did ye see anything looking like men going towards that ship a while ago?”

“I thought I saw four or five, but it was dim.”

“Very dim, very dim. Morning to ye.”

Once more we quitted him; but once more he came softly after us; and touching my shoulder again, said, “See if you can find ’em now, will ye?”

“Morning to ye! morning to ye! Oh! I was going to warn ye against—but never mind, never mind—it’s all one, all in the family too;—sharp frost this morning, ain’t it? Good-bye to ye. Shan’t see ye again very soon, I guess; unless it’s before the Grand Jury.”

We stepped on board. Profound quiet; cabin locked; hatches on. In the forecastle, a tattered old rigger lay face-down across two chests, in profoundest sleep. But I beat the thing down; and again marking the sleeper, jocularly hinted to Queequeg that perhaps we had best sit up with the body; telling him to establish himself accordingly. He put his hand upon the sleeper’s rear, as though feeling if it was soft enough; and then, without more ado, sat quietly down there.

“Gracious!—don’t sit there,” said I.
“Oh! perry dood seat,” said Queequeg; “my country way; won’t hurt him face.”
“Face!” said I; “call that his face?—you are heavy; it’s grinding the face of the poor. Get off, Queequeg! Look, he’ll twitch you off soon. I wonder he don’t wake.”

He shifted by the head, lit his tomahawk-pipe, and flourished its hatchet-side over the sleeper. We kept the pipe passing over him, from one to the other; and meanwhile, upon questioning him in his broken fashion, Queequeg gave me to understand that, in his land, the king, chiefs, and great people generally, were in the custom of fattening some of the lower orders for ottomans; and to furnish a house comfortably in that respect, you had only to buy up eight or ten lazy fellows, and lay them round in the piers and alcoves; upon occasion, a chief calling his attendant, and desiring him to make a settee of himself under a spreading tree, perhaps in some damp marshy place.

“What’s that for, Queequeg?”
“Perry easy, kill-e; oh! perry easy!”

The heavy vapor filled the hole; the man breathed muffledly, rolled, and sat up rubbing his eyes. “Holloa! who be ye smokers?”
“Shipped men. When does she sail?”
“She sails to-day. The Captain came aboard last night.”
“What Captain?—Ahab?”
“Who but him indeed?”

A noise on deck—“Holloa! Starbuck’s astir,” said the rigger; “good man, and a pious.” He turned to; we followed.

Clear sunrise now; the crew came aboard by twos and threes; riggers bestirred; mates engaged. Meanwhile Captain Ahab remained invisibly enshrined within his cabin.

Merry Christmas.

Towards noon, the riggers were dismissed, the Pequod hauled from the wharf; thoughtful Charity came off with her last gifts—a night-cap for Stubb and a spare Bible for the steward. Then Peleg and Bildad issued forth.

“Now, Mr. Starbuck, are you sure everything is right? Captain Ahab is all ready—just spoke to him. Call all hands—blast ’em!” cried Peleg.

“No need of profane words, friend Peleg,” said Bildad; “away, Starbuck, and do our bidding.”

How now! Here upon the very point of starting for the voyage, Captain Peleg and Captain Bildad were going it with a high hand on the quarter-deck, just as if they were to be joint-commanders at sea, as well as to all appearances in port. And, as for Captain Ahab, no sign of him was yet to be seen; only, they said he was in the cabin; and as, not yet completely recovered—so they said—and as it was not his proper business, but the pilot’s, to get the ship under weigh, therefore, Captain Ahab stayed below. And all this seemed natural enough; especially as in the merchant service many captains never show themselves on deck for a considerable time after heaving up the anchor.

Peleg did most of the roaring; Bildad, a licensed pilot, looked forward for the anchor, now and then chanting a dismal stave of psalmody to cheer the windlass hands, who roared a chorus about the girls in Booble Alley. Nevertheless, not three days previous, Bildad had told them that no profane songs would be allowed on board the Pequod, particularly in getting under weigh; and Charity, his sister, had placed a small choice copy of Watts in each seaman’s berth.

“Man the capstan! Blood and thunder!—jump!” Peleg cried, stumping along the bar, using his leg very freely. I paused a moment on my handspike, thinking of the perils we both ran in starting with such a devil for a pilot; I was comforting myself, however, with the thought that in pious Bildad might be found some salvation, spite of his seven hundred and seventy-seventh lay; when I felt a sudden sharp poke in my rear—my first kick. “Spring, thou sheep-head!—spring thy eyes out!” roared Peleg, while imperturbable Bildad led off with his psalm.

At last the anchor up, sails set—we glided out. It was a short, cold Christmas; the freezing spray cased us in ice as in polished armor. The long rows of teeth on the bulwarks glistened in the moonlight; and like the white ivory tusks of some huge elephant, vast curving icicles depended from the bows. Lank Bildad headed the first watch, and ever and anon his steady notes were heard:

“Sweet fields beyond the swelling flood,
Stand dressed in living green.
So to the Jews old Canaan stood,
While Jordan rolled between.”

Never did those sweet words sound more sweetly. Spite of this frigid winter night in the boisterous Atlantic, spite of my wet feet and wetter jacket, there was yet, it then seemed to me, many a pleasant haven in store.

We gained an offing; the pilots were needed no more; the stout sail-boat ranged alongside. Loath to depart a ship bound beyond both stormy Capes, poor old Bildad lingered long; paced the deck with anxious strides; ran down into the cabin; again came on deck, and looked to windward; looked towards the wide and endless waters; looked towards the land; and at last grasped stout Peleg’s hand, and, holding up a lantern, as if to say, “Nevertheless, friend Peleg, I can stand it.” Peleg took it more like a philosopher; yet when the lantern came too near, a tear twinkled.

“Captain Bildad—come, old shipmate, we must go. Back the main-yard! Boat ahoy!—Luck to ye, Starbuck—Mr. Stubb—Mr. Flask—good-bye and good luck to ye all—and this day three years I’ll have a hot supper smoking for ye in old Nantucket. Hurrah and away!”

“God bless ye, and have ye in His holy keeping,” murmured Bildad. “I hope ye’ll have fine weather now, so that Captain Ahab may soon be moving among ye—a pleasant sun is all he needs. Mind the cooper—don’t waste spare staves. Don’t whale it too much a’ Lord’s days; but don’t miss a fair chance either. Beware of fornication if ye touch at the islands. The sail-needles are in the green locker! Don’t keep that cheese too long down in the hold, Mr. Starbuck; it’ll spoil. Be careful with the butter—twenty cents the pound it was, and mind ye, if—”

“Come, come, Captain Bildad; away!” cried Peleg, hurrying him over.

Ship and boat diverged; a screaming gull flew overhead; the two hulls wildly rolled; we gave three heavy-hearted cheers, and blindly plunged like fate into the lone Atlantic.

On that shivering winter’s night when the Pequod thrust her vindictive bows into the malicious waves, who should stand at her helm but Bulkington! Newly landed from a four years’ voyage, he pushed unrestingly off again. The land seemed scorching to his feet. Wonderfullest things are ever the unmentionable; deep memories yield no epitaphs; this six-inch chapter is the stoneless grave of Bulkington. It fared with him as with the storm-tossed ship that miserably drives along the leeward land: the port would fain give succor—safety, comfort, hearthstone there—yet in such a gale the land is her direst jeopardy; she must fly all hospitality; one touch would make her shudder through and through. With all her might she crowds off shore; fights ’gainst the very winds that would blow her homeward; seeks all the lashed sea’s landlessness again; for refuge’s sake forlornly rushing into peril; her only friend her bitterest foe!

Know ye now, Bulkington? Glimpses do ye seem to see of that mortally intolerable truth; that all deep, earnest thinking is but the intrepid effort of the soul to keep the open independence of her sea; while the wildest winds of heaven and earth conspire to cast her on the treacherous, slavish shore? But as in landlessness alone resides highest truth, shoreless, indefinite as God—so, better is it to perish in that howling infinite, than be ingloriously dashed upon the lee, even if that were safety! For worm-like, then, oh! who would craven crawl to land! Terrors of the terrible! is all this agony so vain? Take heart, take heart, O Bulkington! Bear thee grimly, demigod! Up from the spray of thy ocean-perishing—straight up, leaps thy apotheosis!

As Queequeg and I are now fairly embarked in this business of whaling; and as this business of whaling has somehow come to be regarded among landsmen as a rather unpoetical and disreputable pursuit; therefore, I am all anxiety to convince ye, ye landsmen, of the injustice hereby done to us hunters of whales.

In the first place, it may be deemed almost superfluous to establish the fact, that among people at large, the business of whaling is not accounted on a level with what are called the liberal professions. If a stranger were introduced into any miscellaneous metropolitan society, it would but slightly advance the general opinion of his merits, were he presented to the company as a harpooneer, say; and if in emulation of the naval officers he should append the initials S.W.F. (Sperm Whale Fishery) to his visiting card, such a procedure would be deemed pre-eminently presuming and ridiculous.

Doubtless one leading reason why the world declines honoring us whalemen, is this: they think that, at best, our vocation amounts to a butchering sort of business; and that when actively engaged therein, we are surrounded by all manner of defilements. Butchers we are—true. But butchers also—of the bloodiest badge—have been all Martial Commanders whom the world delights to honor. As for the alleged uncleanliness, ye shall soon be initiated into certain facts; but even granting the charge—what are disordered slippery decks to the unspeakable carrion of the battle-field, from which so many soldiers return to drink in all ladies’ plaudits? And if peril so much enhances the soldier’s repute; let me assure ye many a veteran who has freely marched up to a battery, would quickly recoil at the apparition of the sperm whale’s vast tail fanning into eddies the air over his head. For what are the comprehensible terrors of man compared with the interlinked terrors and wonders of God!

But, though the world scouts at us whale hunters, yet does it unwittingly pay us the profoundest homage; for almost all the tapers, lamps, and candles that burn round the globe, burn—as before so many shrines—to our glory!

But look at this matter in other lights; weigh it in all sorts of scales. Why admirals of whaling fleets in De Witt’s day? Why Louis XVI fitting out ships from Dunkirk, inviting Nantucket families? Why Britain paying bounties by the million? And how comes it that we Americans outnumber all others; seven hundred vessels; eighteen thousand men; millions of dollars afloat—if there be not something puissant in whaling?

But this is not the half. I freely assert no single peaceful influence in threescore years has more operated upon the whole broad world than the high and mighty business of whaling; one way and another, it has begotten events so continuously momentous, that whaling may well be regarded as that Egyptian mother, who bore offspring themselves pregnant from her womb. Let a handful suffice. For many years past the whale-ship has been the pioneer to remotest seas; she explored uncharted archipelagoes where no Cook or Vancouver sailed; and if men-of-war now peacefully ride in once savage harbors, let them fire salutes to the whale-ship, which first interpreted between them and the savages. Trumpet your Cooks and Krusensterns; I say anonymous captains out of Nantucket were as great, and greater—in succourless empty-handedness, in heathenish sharked waters, and by the beaches of unrecorded, javelin islands, they battled with virgin wonders and terrors that your marine-backed heroes had not willingly dared. All that is made a flourish of in the old South Sea Voyages was but the lifetime commonplaces of our heroic Nantucketers.

Until the fishery rounded Cape Horn, scarce any but colonial commerce touched the opulent Spanish Pacific; it was the whaleman who broke the jealous policy of the crown, and from those whalemen at last eventuated the liberation of Peru, Chili, and Bolivia.

That great America on the other side, Australia—blunder-born by a Dutchman—other ships long shunned; the whale-ship touched there, mothering that mighty colony; yea, in its infancy feeding it with her benevolent biscuit. Polynesia confesses the same, doing commercial homage to the whale-ship that cleared the way for missionary and merchant; and if that double-bolted land, Japan, is ever to become hospitable, it is the whale-ship alone to whom the credit will be due; for already she is on the threshold.

But if, in the face of all this, you still declare that whaling has no æsthetically noble associations connected with it, then am I ready to shiver fifty lances with you there, and unhorse you with a split helmet every time.

The whale has no famous author, and whaling no famous chronicler, you will say.

_The whale no famous author, and whaling no famous chronicler?_ Who wrote the first account of our Leviathan? Who but mighty Job! And who composed the first narrative of a whaling-voyage? Who, but no less a prince than Alfred the Great, who, with his own royal pen, took down the words from Other, the Norwegian whale-hunter of those times! And who pronounced our glowing eulogy in Parliament? Who, but Edmund Burke!

True enough, but then whalemen themselves are poor devils; they have no good blood in their veins.

_No good blood in their veins?_ They have something better than royal blood there. The grandmother of Benjamin Franklin was Mary Folger of Nantucket—one of the old settlers and the ancestress of a long line of Folgers and harpooneers—all kith and kin to noble Benjamin—this day darting the barbed iron round the world.

Good again; but then all confess that somehow whaling is not respectable.

_Whaling not respectable?_ Whaling is imperial! By old English statutory law, the whale is declared “a royal fish.”

Oh, that’s only nominal! The whale himself has never figured in any grand imposing way.

_The whale never figured in any grand imposing way?_ In one of the mighty triumphs given to a Roman general upon his entering the world’s capital, the bones of a whale, brought from the Syrian coast, were the most conspicuous object in the cymballed procession.

Grant it; but, say what you will, there is no real dignity in whaling.

_No dignity in whaling?_ The dignity of our calling the very heavens attest. Cetus is a constellation in the South! No more! Drive down your hat in presence of the Czar, and take it off to Queequeg! No more! I know a man that, in his lifetime, has taken three hundred and fifty whales. I account that man more honorable than that great captain of antiquity who boasted of taking as many walled towns.

And, as for me, if, by any possibility, there be any as yet undiscovered prime thing in me; if I shall ever deserve any real repute in that small but high hushed world which I might not be unreasonably ambitious of; if hereafter I shall do anything that, upon the whole, a man might rather have done than to have left undone; if, at my death, my executors, or more properly my creditors, find any precious MSS. in my desk, then here I prospectively ascribe all the honor and the glory to whaling; for a whale-ship was my Yale College and my Harvard.

In behalf of the dignity of whaling, I would fain advance naught but substantiated facts. But after embattling his facts, an advocate who should wholly suppress a not unreasonable surmise, which might tell eloquently upon his cause—such an advocate, would he not be blameworthy?

There is a saltcellar of state, so called, and there may be a castor of state. How they use the salt, precisely—who knows? At coronation a king’s head is oiled—even as a head of salad. In truth, a mature man who uses hair-oil, unless medicinally, that man has probably got a quoggy spot in him somewhere.

Not olive, nor macassar, nor castor, nor bear’s, nor train, nor cod-liver. What then can it be but sperm oil in its unmanufactured, unpolluted state—the sweetest of all oils?

Think of that, ye loyal Britons! we whalemen supply your kings and queens with coronation stuff!

The chief mate was Starbuck, a Nantucketer and of Quaker descent; a long, earnest man, hard as twice-baked biscuit. His pure tight skin was an excellent fit; and, closely wrapped up in it, and embalmed with inner health and strength, like a revivified Egyptian, this Starbuck seemed prepared to endure for long ages to come; for be it Polar snow or torrid sun, like a patent chronometer, his interior vitality was warranted to do well in all climates. Looking into his eyes, you seemed to see there the yet lingering images of those thousand-fold perils he had calmly confronted through life.

Uncommonly conscientious and endued with a deep natural reverence, the wild watery loneliness of his life inclined him to that superstition which in some organizations springs from intelligence rather than ignorance. Far-away domestic memories—his young Cape wife and child—bent him from native ruggedness. “I will have no man in my boat,” said Starbuck, “who is not afraid of a whale.” He meant that the most reliable courage rises from a fair estimation of peril; the utterly fearless man is a far more dangerous comrade than a coward.

“Aye, aye,” said Stubb, the second mate, “Starbuck, there, is as careful a man as you’ll find anywhere in this fishery”—but we shall ere long see what that word “careful” precisely means.

Starbuck was no crusader after perils; in him courage was not a sentiment, but a thing simply useful to him, and always at hand upon all mortally practical occasions. Wherefore he had no fancy for lowering for whales after sun-down; nor for persisting in fighting a fish that too much persisted in fighting him. For, thought Starbuck, I am here in this critical ocean to kill whales for my living, and not to be killed by them for theirs; and that hundreds of men had been so killed Starbuck well knew. What doom was his own father’s? Where, in the bottomless deeps, could he find the torn limbs of his brother?

With memories like these in him, and given to a certain superstitiousness, the courage of this Starbuck which could, nevertheless, still flourish, must indeed have been extreme; yet under suitable circumstances there lay in him an element which would break out from its confinement, and burn all his courage up—that sort of bravery which, while generally abiding firm in conflict with seas, or winds, or whales, yet cannot withstand those more terrific, because more spiritual terrors, which sometimes menace you from the concentrating brow of an enraged and mighty man.

But were the coming narrative to reveal in any instance the complete abasement of poor Starbuck’s fortitude, scarce might I have the heart to write it; for it is a thing most sorrowful, nay shocking, to expose the fall of valour in the soul. Men may seem detestable as joint stock-companies and nations; knaves, fools, and murderers there may be; men may have mean and meagre faces; but man, in the ideal, is so noble and so sparkling, such a grand and glowing creature, that over any ignominious blemish in him all his fellows should run to throw their costliest robes. That immaculate manliness we feel within ourselves, so far within us, that it remains intact though all the outer character seem gone; bleeds with keenest anguish at the undraped spectacle of a valor-ruined man. Nor can piety itself, at such a shameful sight, completely stifle her upbraidings against the permitting stars.

But this august dignity I treat of, is not the dignity of kings and robes, but that abounding dignity which has no robed investiture. Thou shalt see it shining in the arm that wields a pick or drives a spike; that democratic dignity which, on all hands, radiates without end from God; Himself! The great God absolute! The centre and circumference of all democracy! His omnipresence, our divine equality!

If, then, to meanest mariners, and renegades and castaways, I shall hereafter ascribe high qualities, though dark; weave round them tragic graces; if even the most mournful, perchance the most abased, among them all, shall at times lift himself to the exalted mounts; if I shall touch that workman’s arm with some ethereal light; if I shall spread a rainbow over his disastrous set of sun; then against all mortal critics bear me out in it, thou just Spirit of Equality, which hast spread one royal mantle of humanity over all my kind! Bear me out in it, thou great democratic God! who didst not refuse to the swart convict, Bunyan, the pale, poetic pearl; Thou who didst clothe with doubly hammered leaves of finest gold, the stumped and paupered arm of old Cervantes; Thou who didst pick up Andrew Jackson from the pebbles; who didst hurl him upon a war-horse; who didst thunder him higher than a throne! Thou who, in all Thy mighty, earthly marchings, ever cullest Thy selectest champions from the kingly commons; bear me out in it, O God!

Stubb, the second mate, a Cape-Cod-man, was happy-go-lucky; neither craven nor valiant; taking perils as they came with an indifferent air; good-humored, easy, and careless, he presided over his whale-boat as if the most deadly encounter were but a dinner, and his crew all invited guests. In the very death-lock of the fight he handled his unpitying lance as a whistling tinker his hammer; humming old rigadig tunes while flank and flank with the exasperated monster. Long usage had converted the jaws of death into an easy chair for him. What he thought of death itself, there is no telling—whether he ever thought of it at all; but if he ever did chance to cast his mind that way after a comfortable dinner, like a good sailor he took it to be a sort of call of the watch to tumble aloft about something he would find out when he obeyed, and not sooner. What, perhaps, with other things, made Stubb such an easy-going, unfearing man, so cheerily trudging off with the burden of life in a world full of grave pedlars, all bowed to the ground with their packs; what helped to bring about that almost impious good-humor of his; that thing must have been his pipe. For, like his nose, his short, black little pipe was one of the regular features of his face; he kept a row of them ready loaded in a rack, and whenever he turned in, he smoked them all out in succession, lighting one from the other to the chapter’s end. When Stubb dressed, instead of first putting legs into trowsers, he put his pipe into his mouth. For every one knows that this earthly air, whether ashore or afloat, is terribly infected with the nameless miseries of the numberless mortals who have died exhaling it; and as in time of the cholera some go with a camphorated handkerchief to the mouth, so Stubb’s tobacco smoke might have operated against all mortal tribulations as a sort of disinfecting agent.

The third mate was Flask, of Tisbury in Martha’s Vineyard; short, stout, ruddy; very pugnacious concerning whales, who somehow seemed to think that the great leviathans had personally and hereditarily affronted him; and therefore it was a sort of point of honor with him, to destroy them whenever encountered. So utterly lost was he to all sense of reverence for the many marvels of their majestic bulk and mystic ways, and so dead to anything like an apprehension of any possible danger from encountering them, that in his poor opinion the wondrous whale was but a species of magnified mouse, or at least water-rat, requiring only a little circumvention and some small application of time and trouble in order to kill and boil. This ignorant, unconscious fearlessness made him a little waggish in the matter of whales; he followed the fish for the fun of it; a three years’ voyage round Cape Horn only a jolly joke. They called him King-Post on board the Pequod, like that short, square timber in Arctic whalers; made to clinch tight and last long.

These three—Starbuck, Stubb, Flask—were momentous men, headsmen of the three boats. In Ahab’s grand order of battle, they were as captains of companies; armed with long keen spears; their harpooneers as flingers of javelins; squire and Knight.

First among the harpooneers was Queequeg—Starbuck’s squire.

Next, Tashtego, pure-blooded Indian of Gay-Head, high cheek-boned, black rounding eyes—Oriental in largeness, Antarctic in glitter; the infallible arrow of his sires replaced by the unerring harpoon. He was Stubb’s squire.

Third, Daggoo, gigantic, coal-black, lion-treading; two golden hoops in his ears like ring-bolts; six feet five in his socks, erect as a giraffe; the corporeal humility of looking up at him made a white man seem a white flag suing truce of a fortress. Curious to tell, this imperial negro, Ahasuerus Daggoo, was the Squire of little Flask, who looked like a chess-man beside him.

As for the residue, at the present day not one in two before the mast in the American fishery are Americans born, though pretty nearly all the officers are. Islanders make the best whalemen: Azores, Shetlands; they were nearly all Islanders in the Pequod—Isolatoes, I call such, each living on his own separate continent; yet now, federated along one keel, an Anacharsis Clootz deputation from all the isles of the sea, and all the ends of the earth, accompanying Old Ahab to lay the world’s grievances before that bar from which not many ever come back. Black Little Pip—he never did—oh, no! Poor Alabama boy! Soon ye shall see him beating his tambourine on the grim forecastle; prelusive of the eternal time when bid strike in with angels in glory; called a coward here, hailed a hero there!

For several days after leaving Nantucket, nothing above hatches was seen of Captain Ahab. The mates regularly relieved each other; yet ever and anon they came from the cabin with sudden, peremptory orders—plain proof they but commanded vicariously; their supreme lord and dictator was there, unseen in the now sacred retreat.

My vague disquiet touching the unknown captain, heightened by Elijah’s diabolical incoherences, grew at sea to perturbation; yet the mates allayed misgivings. Running south, winter bit us less and less; and on one of those grey, gloomy mornings of the transition, when with a fair wind the ship was rushing through the water with a vindictive sort of leaping and melancholy rapidity, I mounted to the deck—reality outran apprehension: Captain Ahab stood upon his quarter-deck.

No common illness marked him. He looked like a man cut away from the stake when the fire has wasted all the limbs without consuming them; his whole high, broad form seemed made of solid bronze, shaped in an unalterable mould, like Cellini’s cast Perseus. Threading from among his grey hairs, and down one side of his scorched face and neck, disappearing in his clothing, a slender, livid, rod-like mark—like that perpendicular seam sometimes made in the straight, lofty trunk of a great tree, when the upper lightning tearingly darts down it, and, without wrenching a single twig, peels and grooves out the bark from top to bottom, leaving the tree still greenly alive, but branded. Whether that mark was born with him, or whether it was the scar left by some desperate wound, no one could say. But once an old Gay-Head Indian among the crew superstitiously asserted that not till he was full forty years old did Ahab become that way branded, and then it came upon him, not in the fury of any mortal fray, but in an elemental strife at sea. Yet this wild hint seemed inferentially negatived by what a grey Manxman insinuated; that if ever Captain Ahab should be tranquilly laid out—if that should ever come to pass—then whoever did that last office would find a birth-mark on him from crown to sole. Then, too, the barbaric white leg upon which he partly stood—the ivory fashioned from the polished jaw-bone of a sperm whale. He steadied it in an auger-hole by the mizzen shrouds; one arm held a shroud; his face fixed straight out beyond the ever-pitching prow. In that fearless forward dedication of glance was an infinity of firmest fortitude; in his face, a crucifixion; in all, the nameless regal overbearing dignity of some mighty woe. He spoke not; nor did the officers address him; yet in their minutest gestures they owned the uneasy consciousness of a troubled master-eye.

Ere long he withdrew; but thereafter he was daily visible—at his pivot-hole, upon an ivory stool, or pacing the deck. As the sky grew less gloomy he grew less recluse. For a time he seemed as unnecessary as another mast; the Pequod was but making a passage; the mates sufficed; there was little, out of himself, to employ him; and thus, for that interval, the clouds piled upon his brow. Nevertheless, the warm, warbling persuasiveness of the pleasant, holiday weather gradually charmed him a little from his mood. For, as when the red-cheeked, dancing girls, April and May, trip home to the wintry, misanthropic woods; even the barest, ruggedest, most thunder-cloven old oak will at least send forth some few green sprouts, to welcome such glad-hearted visitants; so Ahab did, in the end, a little respond to the playful allurings of that girlish air. More than once he put forth the faint blossom of a look which, in any other man, would have soon flowered out in a smile.

Ice and icebergs astern, the Pequod rolled into the bright Quito spring—the warmly cool, clear, ringing, perfumed, overflowing, redundant days, were as crystal goblets of Persian sherbet, heaped up—flaked up, with rose-water snow. The starred and stately nights seemed haughty dames in jewelled velvets, nursing at home in lonely pride, the memory of their absent conquering Earls, the golden helmeted suns! For sleeping man, ’twas hard to choose between such winsome days and such seducing nights. But all that witchery turned inward. Inward they turned upon the soul, especially when the still mild hours of eve came on; then, memory shot her crystals as the clear ice most forms of noiseless twilights. And more and more it wrought on Ahab’s texture. Old age is always wakeful; as if, the longer linked with life, the less man has to do with aught that looks like death. It was so with Ahab; only that now, of late, he seemed so much to live in the open air, that truly speaking, his visits were more to the cabin, than from the cabin to the planks. “It feels like going down into one’s tomb,”—he would mutter to himself—“for an old captain like me to be descending this narrow scuttle, to go to my grave-dug berth.”

Nightly, when quietude fell, he would emerge, gripping the iron banister, and usually abstained from patrolling the quarter-deck; for to his wearied mates, seeking repose within six inches of his ivory heel, such would have been the reverberating crack and din of that bony step, that their dreams would have been on the crunching teeth of sharks. But once the mood was on him too deep; with heavy, lumber-like pace he measured the ship from taffrail to mainmast, till Stubb came up from below with a certain deprecating humorousness, hinting at muffling the noise with a globe of tow for the ivory heel. “Am I a cannon-ball, Stubb,” said Ahab, “that thou wouldst wad me that fashion? But go thy ways; I had forgot. Below to thy nightly grave; where such as ye sleep between shrouds, to use ye to the filling one at last.—Down, dog, and kennel!” Starting at the unforeseen concluding exclamation of the so suddenly scornful old man, Stubb was speechless a moment; then said excitedly, “I am not used to be spoken to that way, sir; I do but less than half like it, sir.” “Avast!” gritted Ahab between his set teeth, and violently moving away, as if to avoid some passionate temptation. “No, sir; not yet,” said Stubb, emboldened, “I will not tamely be called a dog, sir.” “Then be called ten times a donkey, and a mule, and an ass, and begone, or I’ll clear the world of thee!” Ahab advanced upon him with such overbearing terrors that Stubb involuntarily retreated down the scuttle.

“There he muttered, “It’s very queer. Stop, Stubb; somehow, now, I don’t well know whether to go back and strike him, or—what’s that?—down here on my knees and pray for him? Yes, that was the thought coming up in me; but it would be the first time I ever did pray.” It’s queer; very queer; and he’s queer too; aye, take him fore and aft, he’s about the queerest old man Stubb ever sailed with. How he flashed at me!—his eyes like powder-pans! is he mad? Anyway there’s something on his mind, as sure as there must be something on a deck when it cracks. Didn’t that Dough-Boy tell me, of a morning he always finds the old man’s hammock clothes rumpled and tumbled, the sheets down at the foot, the coverlid almost tied into knots, and the pillow a sort of frightful hot, as though a baked brick had been on it? A hot old man! I guess he’s got what some folks ashore call a conscience; it’s a kind of Tic-Dolly-row—worse nor a toothache. Well, well; I don’t know what it is, but the Lord keep me from catching it. He’s full of riddles; I wonder what he goes into the after hold for, every night, as Dough-Boy tells me he suspects; what’s that for, I should like to know? Who’s made appointments with him in the hold? Here goes for a snooze. Think not, is my eleventh commandment; and sleep when you can, is my twelfth.” Yet even as he slung to hammock, he started: “didn’t he call me a dog?—ten times a donkey—and pile jackasses on top of that! Maybe he did kick me, and I didn’t observe it, I was so taken all aback with his brow, somehow. By the Lord, I must have been dreaming—How? how? how?—the only way’s to stash it; so here goes to hammock again; and in the morning, I’ll see how this plaguey juggling thinks over by daylight.”

When Stubb had gone, Ahab leaned over the bulwarks; then called for his ivory stool and pipe, lit it at the binnacle, and sat smoking.

In old Norse times, the thrones of the sea-loving Danish kings were fabricated, saith tradition, of the tusks of the narwhale. How could one look at Ahab then, seated on that tripod of bones, without bethinking him of the royalty it symbolized? For a Khan of the plank, and a king of the sea, and a great lord of Leviathans was Ahab.

Thick vapor blew back into his face. “How now,” he soliloquized, “this smoking no longer soothes. Oh, my pipe! hard must it go with me if thy charm be gone! Here have I been unconsciously toiling, not pleasuring—aye, and ignorantly smoking to windward all the while; to windward, and with such nervous whiffs, as if, like the dying whale, my final jets were the strongest and fullest of trouble. What business have I with this pipe? This thing that is meant for sereneness, to send up mild white vapors among mild white hairs, not among torn iron-grey locks like mine. I’ll smoke no more—”

He tossed the lighted pipe into the sea; it hissed as the ship shot by. With slouched hat, Ahab lurchingly paced the planks.

Next morning Stubb accosted Flask. “Such a queer dream, King-Post, I never had. You know the old man’s ivory leg—well, I dreamed he kicked me with it; and when I tried to kick back, upon my soul, my little man, I kicked my leg right off! And then, presto! Ahab seemed a pyramid, and I, like a blazing fool, kept kicking at it. But what was still more curious—Flask, you know how curious all dreams are—through all this rage I somehow seemed to think it was not much of an insult, that kick from Ahab. ‘Why,’ thinks I, ‘what’s the row? It’s not a real leg, only a false leg.’ And there’s a mighty difference between a living thump and a dead thump; that’s what makes a blow from the hand, Flask, fifty times more savage to bear than a blow from a cane. The living member—that makes the living insult, my little man. ‘Yes,’ thinks I, ‘it was only a playful cudgelling—in fact, only a whaleboning—not a base kick. Besides, look at it once; the end of it—the foot part—what a small sort of end it is; whereas, if a broad-footed farmer kicked me, there’s a devilish broad insult. But this insult is whittled down to a point only.’”

“Then a badger-haired old merman with a hump takes me by the shoulders, and slews me round. ‘What are you ’bout?’ says he. Slid! man, but I was frightened. Such a phiz! But, somehow, next moment I was over the fright. ‘What am I about?’ says I at last. ‘And what business is that of yours, I should like to know, Mr. Humpback? Do you want a kick?’ By the lord, Flask, I had no sooner said that, than he turned round his stern to me, bent over, and dragging up a lot of seaweed he had for a clout—what do you think I saw?—why, thunder alive, man, his stern was stuck full of marlinspikes, with the points out. Says I, on second thoughts, ‘I guess I won’t kick you, old fellow.’ ‘Wise Stubb,’ said he, ‘wise Stubb;’ and kept muttering it all the time. Seeing he wasn’t going to stop, I thought I might as well fall to kicking the pyramid again; but he roared out, ‘Stop that kicking! Look ye here; let’s argue the insult. Captain Ahab kicked ye, didn’t he?—he used his ivory leg, didn’t he? Well then, wise Stubb, what have you to complain of? You were kicked by a great man, and with a beautiful ivory leg. It’s an honor; I consider it an honor. In old England the greatest lords think it great glory to be slapped by a queen, and made garter-knights of; but, be your boast, Stubb, that ye were kicked by old Ahab, and made a wise man of. Be kicked by him; account his kicks honors; and on no account kick back; for you can’t help yourself, wise Stubb. Don’t you see that pyramid?’”

With that, he seemed to swim off into the air. Stubb woke in his hammock. “What do you think of that, Flask?” “Foolish,” said Flask. “May be; may be; but it’s made a wise man of me. D’ye see Ahab standing there, sideways looking over the stern? Well, the best thing you can do, Flask, is to let the old man alone; never speak to him, whatever he says.—Hark!”

“Mast-head, there! Look sharp, all of ye! There are whales hereabouts! If ye see a white one, split your lungs for him!”

“A white whale—did ye mark that, man?” whispered Stubb. “There’s something special in the wind. Ahab has that that’s bloody on his mind. But, mum; he comes this way.”

Cetology.

Already boldly launched, we shall soon be lost in the deep’s unshored, harbourless immensities; ere that, attend to a matter indispensable to apprehending the leviathanic allusions to follow—a systematized exhibition of the whale in his broad genera. It is a fearful thing to classify the constituents of a chaos. “No branch of Zoology is so much involved as that which is entitled Cetology,” says Scoresby (A.D. 1820). “Utter confusion exists,” adds Surgeon Beale (A.D. 1839). “Impenetrable veil,” “Unfitness,” “field strewn with thorns,” cry Cuvier, Hunter, Lesson.

Yet though of real knowledge there be little, of books there are a plenty. Many are the men, small and great, old and new, landsmen and seamen, who have at large or in little, written of the whale. Run over a few:—The Authors of the Bible; Aristotle; Pliny; Aldrovandi; Sir Thomas Browne; Gesner; Ray; Linnæus; Rondeletius; Willoughby; Green; Artedi; Sibbald; Brisson; Marten; Lacépède; Bonneterre; Desmarest; Baron Cuvier; Frederick Cuvier; John Hunter; Owen; Scoresby; Beale; Bennett; J. Ross Browne; the Author of Miriam Coffin; Olmstead; and the Rev. T. Cheever. But the usurpation of the Greenland or right-whale as monarch has been complete—poets crowned him so—through ignorance of the great sperm whale. Now hear a new proclamation. This is Charing Cross; hear ye! good people all,—the Greenland whale is deposed,—the great sperm whale now reigneth!

Only Beale and Bennett, surgeons to South-Sea whale-ships, at all put the living sperm whale before you. But as yet, the sperm whale, scientific or poetic, lives not complete in any literature. Far above all hunted whales, his is an unwritten life.

As no better man advances, I offer my own poor draught: not minute anatomy, but an outline, to be hereafter filled by abler builders. I promise nothing complete; because any human thing supposed to be complete, must for that very reason infallibly be faulty. My object here is simply to project the draught of a systematization of cetology. I am the architect, not the builder.

But it is a ponderous task; to grope down among the unspeakable foundations and ribs of the world is a fearful thing. What am I that I should essay to hook the nose of this leviathan! The awful tauntings in Job might well appal me. Will he (the leviathan) make a covenant with thee? Behold the hope of him is vain! But I have swam through libraries and sailed through oceans; I have had to do with whales with these visible hands; I am in earnest; and I will try. There are some preliminaries to settle.

First, preliminaries. It remains, in some quarters, a moot point whether a whale be a fish. Linnæus (A.D. 1776) separates whales from fish—warm bilocular heart, lungs, movable eyelids, hollow ears, mammæ, penem intrantem, and so on. I submitted all this to my friends Simeon Macey and Charley Coffin, of Nantucket, both messmates of mine in a certain voyage, and they united in the opinion that the reasons set forth were altogether insufficient. Charley profanely hinted they were humbug. But waiving argument, I take the good old fashioned ground that the whale is a fish, and call upon holy Jonah to back me. Settle then the external definition: to be short, a whale is a spouting fish with a horizontal tail. There you have him.

By this, I exclude no spouting, horizontal-tailed sea creature Nantucketers call whale; nor include any alien. I am aware that many include the Lamatins and Dugongs; but as these pig-fish are a noisy, contemptible set, mostly lurking in the mouths of rivers, and feeding on wet hay, and especially as they do not spout, I deny their credentials as whales; and have presented them with their passports to quit the Kingdom of Cetology. Now, for method: to seize whales bodily, in their entire liberal volume, and sort them bibliographically by magnitude—like books. Hence three primary BOOKS: I. THE FOLIO; II. the OCTAVO; III. the DUODECIMO. As types: Sperm Whale (Folio); Grampus (Octavo); Porpoise (Duodecimo).

BOOK I. Folio. Chapters: I. Sperm Whale; II. Right Whale; III. Fin-Back; IV. Hump-backed; V. Razor Back; VI. Sulphur Bottom.

CH. I. Sperm Whale.—Trumpa, Physeter, Anvil-Headed, Cachalot, Pottsfich, Macrocephalus: largest inhabitant of the globe, most formidable to encounter, most majestic, and most valuable—for from him alone is spermaceti obtained. Philologically absurd; when his oil was only accidentally obtained from stranded fish, spermaceti was supposed a humor of the Greenland whale; when the truth was known, the old name, enhancing value by its strange scarcity, stuck to the whale that truly yielded it.

CH. II. Right Whale.—Most venerable, first regularly hunted; yields baleen (“whalebone”) and “whale oil.” The Great Mysticetus; the Greenland Whale; variously baptised—the Whale; the Black Whale; the True Whale. Differences claimed between English Greenland and American Right are inconclusive. Elsewhere I treat him at some length, to elucidate the sperm.

CH. III. Fin-Back.—Fin-Back, Tall-Spout, Long-John: seen in almost every sea, his angular back-fin a conspicuous gnomon casting wavy hour-lines on the dialled sea. On that Ahaz-dial the shadow often goes back. Less portly than the Right; olive-coloured; solitary, shy; his lofty jet a misanthropic spear; gifted with such wondrous velocity as to defy present pursuit; this leviathan seems the banished and unconquerable Cain of his race, bearing for his mark that style upon his back. Because he has baleen, sometimes grouped among “Whalebone whales”; but be warned: baleen, hump, fin, teeth—these features are irregularly combined across kinds; on that rock all whale-naturalists have split.

CH. IV. Hump Back.—Often taken on the northern American coast; oil indifferent; baleen present; the most gamesome and light-hearted, making more gay foam than any.

CH. V. Razor Back.—Little known; I have seen him off Cape Horn; his long sharp ridge alone shown. Let him go.

CH. VI. Sulphur Bottom.—A retiring gentleman with a brimstone belly; seldom seen save remotely in southern seas; never chased—he would run away with rope-walks of line. Prodigies are told of him. Adieu!

BOOK II. Octavoes (middling magnitude): I. Grampus; II. Black Fish; III. Narwhale; IV. Thrasher; V. Killer.

CH. I. Grampus.—His loud blowing has furnished a landsman’s proverb. Though not popularly a whale, he possesses the grand distinctive features; fifteen to twenty-five feet; swims in herds; not regularly hunted; his oil considerable and pretty good for light.

CH. II. Black Fish.—Vague name; call him the Hyena Whale. Voracious; Mephistophelean grin from the up-curved inner lips. Hooked dorsal like a Roman nose. Thin blubber, yet some yield above thirty gallons; captured for cheap oil when no better game is up.

CH. III. Narwhale (Nostril whale).—Sixteen feet; horn (a tusk) five to fifteen feet, sinister-sided; what precise purpose it serves, I cannot say. A sea-unicorn; in old days its horn, antidote to poison, fetched immense prices. Sir Martin Frobisher presented such a prodigious horn to Queen Bess. Picturesque, milk-white dotted with black; fine clear oil; found in circumpolar seas.

CH. IV. Killer.—Savage Feegee fish of a grampus’s bigness; takes Folio whales by the lip, hangs like a leech till they are worried to death. Never hunted. For we are all killers, on land and on sea; Bonapartes and Sharks included.

CH. V. Thrasher.—Famous for his tail; mounts the Folio’s back and works his passage by flogging him; still less is known than of the Killer. Outlaws both.

BOOK III. Duodecimoes (smaller): I. Huzza Porpoise; II. Algerine Porpoise; III. Mealy-mouthed Porpoise. Though but four or five feet, by my definition they are whales—spouting fish with horizontal tails.

CH. I. Huzza Porpoise.—Name of my bestowal; swims in hilarious shoals, tossing like caps on the Fourth-of-July; lucky omens. If you yourself can withstand three cheers at beholding these vivacious fish, then heaven help ye; the spirit of godly gamesomeness is not in ye. One gallon of good oil; delicate jaw-fluid prized by jewellers and watchmakers. Watch his tiny spout—you shall see the great sperm himself in miniature.

CH. II. Algerine Porpoise.—A pirate; very savage; Pacific; somewhat larger than the Huzza; buckles to a shark.

CH. III. Mealy-mouthed Porpoise.—Largest porpoise; Pacific; called the Right-Whale Porpoise for his hauntings. Neater figure; no back-fin; lovely tail; but his mealy mouth—white below, black above, a bright waist dividing—makes him look meal-bag felonious. Oil like the common porpoise’s.

Beyond the Duodecimo, I do not proceed; the Porpoise is the smallest of whales. But there is a rabble—Bottle-Nose, Junk, Pudding-Headed, Cape, Leading, Cannon, Scragg, Coppered, Elephant, Iceberg, Quog, Blue, etc.—uncertain, fugitive, half-fabulous. Let future investigators mark and incorporate them by Folio, Octavo, or Duodecimo, as may be.
