Finally: this system is left unfinished, as Cologne’s cathedral with the crane upon the incomplete tower. For small erections may be finished by their first architects; grand ones, true ones, ever leave the copestone to posterity. God keep me from ever completing anything. This whole book is but a draught—nay, but the draught of a draught. Oh, Time, Strength, Cash, and Patience!

Because the whale-fleet knows a class of officers unknown elsewhere—the harpooneers—here fits a domestic peculiarity. In the old Dutch Fishery, command was divided between the captain and an officer called the Specksnyder—literally, Fat-Cutter; usage made it Chief Harpooneer. Navigation and general management were the captain’s; all whale-hunting concerns the Specksnyder’s. In the British Greenland Fishery, under the corrupted title Specksioneer, he lingers, abridged; now but senior harpooneer, one of the captain’s subalterns. Yet upon the harpooneers’ good conduct the voyage largely depends; and in the American Fishery, under certain circumstances the command of the ship’s deck may be his. Therefore the sea’s grand maxim demands he live apart from the men forward and be marked their professional superior—though familiarly their social equal.

At sea the grand distinction is this: officer aft, man forward. Hence mates quarter with the captain; and, in most American whalers, the harpooneers too take their meals in the captain’s cabin and sleep near it.

Though a Southern whaling voyage, with its perils and community of interest, may in some cases beget a less rigorous discipline; yet, never mind how much like an old Mesopotamian family these whalemen may, in some instances, live together; for all that, the punctilious externals, at least, of the quarter-deck are seldom materially relaxed, and in no instance done away. Many a Nantucket skipper parades with an elated grandeur not surpassed in any military navy; nay, extorting almost as much outward homage as if he wore the imperial purple, and not the shabbiest of pilot-cloth. And though of all men the moody captain of the Pequod was the least given to that shallowest assumption; though the only homage he ever exacted was implicit, instantaneous obedience; though he required no man to remove the shoes from his feet ere stepping upon the quarter-deck; and though there were times, owing to peculiar circumstances hereafter to be detailed, when he addressed them in unusual terms, whether of condescension or in terrorem; yet even Ahab was by no means unobservant of the paramount forms and usages of the sea.

Nor, perhaps, will it fail to be eventually perceived, that behind those forms and usages, as it were, he sometimes masked himself; incidentally making use of them for other and more private ends than they were legitimately intended to subserve. That certain sultanism of his brain, else in good degree unmanifested, through those forms became incarnate in irresistible dictatorship. For be a man’s intellectual superiority what it will, it can never assume the practical, available supremacy over other men without the aid of some sort of external arts and entrenchments—always, in themselves, more or less paltry and base. This it is, that for ever keeps God’s true princes of the Empire from the world’s hustings; and leaves the highest honors that this air can give, to those men who become famous more through their infinite inferiority to the choice hidden handful of the Divine Inert, than through their undoubted superiority over the dead level of the mass. Such large virtue lurks in these small things when extreme political superstitions invest them, that in some royal instances even to idiot imbecility they have imparted potency. But when, as with Nicholas the Czar, the ringed crown encircles an imperial brain, the plebeian herds crouch abased before the tremendous centralization. Nor, will the tragic dramatist who would depict mortal indomitableness in its fullest sweep and direct swing, ever forget a hint, incidentally so important in his art, as the one now alluded to.

But Ahab, my Captain, still moves before me in all his Nantucket grimness and shagginess; and in this episode touching Emperors and Kings, I must not conceal that I have only to do with a poor old whale-hunter like him; and, therefore, all outward majestical trappings and housings are denied me. Oh, Ahab! what shall be grand in thee, it must needs be plucked at from the skies, and dived for in the deep, and featured in the unbodied air!

Noon. Dough-Boy’s pale loaf-of-bread face lifts from the scuttle: “Dinner.” Ahab, who has just taken the sun and reckoned latitude on the medallion tablet atop his ivory leg, swings by the mizen shrouds and, with an even, unexhilarated “Dinner, Mr. Starbuck,” disappears.

When the last echo of the sultan’s step has died, Starbuck takes a few grave turns, peeps into the binnacle, then, “Dinner, Mr. Stubb,” and descends. The second Emir lounges a while, shakes the main brace, then rapidly, “Dinner, Mr. Flask,” follows. Left alone, Flask tips knowing winks, kicks off his shoes, cuts a silent hornpipe over the Grand Turk’s head, slings his cap to the mizentop; but ere he crosses the threshold below, he ships a new face; independent, hilarious Flask enters King Ahab’s presence transformed to Abjectus, or the Slave.

So works the intense artificialness of sea-usages. Officers bold enough on deck, at the captain’s table are inoffensive, deprecatory, humble—this is marvellous, sometimes most comical. Wherefore this difference? A problem? Perhaps not. To have been Belshazzar, King of Babylon; and to have been Belshazzar, not haughtily but courteously, therein certainly must have been some touch of mundane grandeur. But he who in the rightly regal and intelligent spirit presides over his own private dinner-table of invited guests, that man’s unchallenged power and dominion of individual influence for the time; that man’s royalty of state transcends Belshazzar’s, for Belshazzar was not the greatest. Who has but once dined his friends, has tasted what it is to be Cæsar. It is a witchery of social czarship which there is no withstanding. Now, if to this consideration you superadd the official supremacy of a ship-master, then, by inference, you will derive the cause of that peculiarity of sea-life just mentioned.

Over his ivory-inlaid board Ahab presided like a mute, maned sea-lion on a white coral beach, surrounded by warlike but still deferential cubs. Each waited to be served; their intent eyes fastened on the old man’s knife as he carved; silence, solemn as Frankfort’s coronation banquet. Ahab forbade not talk; only he was dumb. What a relief it was to choking Stubb, when a rat made a sudden racket in the hold below. When, between knife and fork, he motioned Starbuck’s plate, the mate received his meat as though receiving alms; and cut it tenderly; and a little started if, perchance, the knife grazed against the plate; and chewed it noiselessly; and swallowed it, not without circumspection. Poor little Flask—the youngest son—had the shinbones. To help himself had seemed larceny. Least of all did he presume to butter. Whether the owners denied him for fear of blotching his sunny cheek, or butter in these marketless waters stood at a premium—however it was, Flask, alas! was a butterless man!

Another thing. He was last down and first up. Starbuck and Stubb had the start, and the privilege of lingering. If Stubb, with small appetite, showed symptoms of concluding, Flask must bestir himself or get scarce three mouthfuls; for it is against holy usage that Stubb precede Flask to the deck. So Flask owned that since promotion he had never known aught but hunger. Peace and satisfaction, thought Flask, have for ever departed from my stomach. I am an officer; but, how I wish I could fish a bit of old-fashioned beef in the forecastle, as when before the mast. There’s the fruits of promotion now; there’s the vanity of glory: there’s the insanity of life! Besides, if any sailor had a grudge against him, ample vengeance was a peep through the sky-light: Flask sitting dumfoundered before awful Ahab.

Ahab and his three mates formed the first table. After their inverted departure, the pallid steward restored some hurried order; then the three harpooneers were bidden—the residuary legatees—turning the cabin into a servants’ hall.

In strange contrast to the hardly tolerable constraint and nameless invisible domineerings of the captain’s table, was the entire care-free license, the almost frantic democracy of those inferior fellows the harpooneers. While mates feared the hinges of their jaws, the harpooneers chewed with a report; they dined like lords, filled like Indian ships. Queequeg and Tashtego’s appetites were portentous; if not nimble with great barons of salt-junk, Dough-Boy felt Tashtego’s harpoon-wise fork prick him forward. Once Daggoo, in sudden humor, snatched him up bodily, thrust his head into a trencher, while Tashtego, knife in hand, traced the circle preliminary to scalping. Dough-Boy was a nervous little fellow; the standing spectacle of black terrific Ahab, and these periodical visitations, made his life one continual lip-quiver. Commonly, having furnished all, he escaped to his pantry and fearfully peeped through the blinds till the tempest passed.

It was a sight: Queequeg’s filed teeth opposite Tashtego’s; Daggoo, seated on the floor (a bench would have lifted his hearse-plumed head into the carlines), at every motion making the low frame shake like a ship bearing an African elephant. Yet the great negro was abstemious, dainty even—small mouthfuls maintaining so broad and superb a person; doubtless, this noble savage fed strong and drank deep of the abounding element of air; and through his dilated nostrils snuffed in the sublime life of the worlds. Not by beef or bread are giants made. But Queequeg’s mortal, barbaric smack of the lip—ugly enough—made Dough-Boy glance at his own lean arms for teeth-marks. When Tashtego sang out for him to produce himself that his bones might be picked, the crockery rattled with Dough-Boy’s palsy. Nor did the whetstones help—the harpooneers sharpened their knives at dinner; that grating sound did not tranquillize him. Alas! hard fares the white waiter who waits upon cannibals. Not a napkin should he carry on his arm, but a buckler.

Though these barbarians dined in the cabin and nominally lived there, they were scarcely in it save at meals and before sleep, passing through to their own quarters. In this, Ahab was no exception to American whale-captains who deem the cabin theirs by right. In real truth, mates and harpooneers lived out of the cabin more than in; for in the cabin was no companionship; socially, Ahab was inaccessible. He lived in the world as the last of the Grisly Bears in settled Missouri. And as, when Spring and Summer depart, that wild Logan, burying in a tree’s hollow, lives out the winter there, sucking his own paws; so, in his inclement, howling old age, Ahab’s soul, shut in the caved trunk of his body, fed upon the sullen paws of its gloom.

It was in the more pleasant weather, that in due rotation with the other seamen my first mast-head came round. In most American whalemen the mast-heads are manned almost as the anchor trips; and if, after years, a ship draws nigh home with anything empty in her, then her mast-heads are kept manned to the last—hope not struck till the spires of port are under the skysail-poles.

As the business is an ancient one, expatiate we a little. The earliest standers of mast-heads I take to have been the old Egyptians; for though their progenitors, the builders of Babel, must doubtless, by their tower, have intended to rear the loftiest mast-head in all Asia, or Africa either; yet, as that great stone mast of theirs may be said to have gone by the board, in the dread gale of God’s wrath; therefore, we cannot give these Babel builders priority over the Egyptians. Their stair-like pyramids furnished those long-legged astronomers a mounting place to “sing out” for new stars, even as our look-outs sing for sails or whales. In Saint Stylites, the famous Christian hermit of old times, who built him a lofty stone pillar in the desert and spent the whole latter portion of his life on its summit, hoisting his food from the ground with a tackle; in him we have a remarkable instance of a dauntless stander-of-mast-heads; who was not to be driven from his place by fogs or frosts, rain, hail, or sleet; but valiantly facing everything out to the last, literally died at his post. Of modern standers-of-mast-heads we have a lifeless set—Napoleon aloft on the Vendôme column; great Washington on his main-mast in Baltimore; Nelson in Trafalgar Square; but neither great Washington, nor Napoleon, nor Nelson, will answer a single hail from below, however madly invoked to befriend by their counsels the distracted decks upon which they gaze.

Nor is it unwarrantable to couple shore-standers with sea-standers; Obed Macy tells how Nantucketers, ere regular whale-ships, nailed cleats up coast spars for look-outs; New Zealand Bay-men, too, cried whales from beach-spires. But turn we to the one proper mast-head, that of a whaleship at sea. The three mast-heads are kept manned from sun-rise to sun-set; the seamen taking their two-hour turns as at the helm. In the serene tropics it is delightful: there you stand, a hundred feet above the silent decks, striding along the deep as if the masts were gigantic stilts; while beneath you and between your legs, as it were, swim the hugest monsters of the sea, even as ships once sailed between the boots of the famous Colossus at old Rhodes. The tranced ship indolently rolls; the drowsy trade winds blow; a sublime uneventfulness invests you; your dinners are snugly stowed in casks, your bill of fare immutable.

Yet months of your voyage are spent up there, and the place is destitute of cosy inhabitiveness. Your perch is the head of the t’ gallant-mast, upon two thin cross-trees—cosy as a bull’s horns. To be sure, in cold weather you may carry your house aloft in a watch-coat; but properly speaking the thickest watch-coat is no more of a house than the unclad body; for as the soul is glued inside of its fleshy tabernacle, and cannot freely move about in it, nor even move out of it, without running great risk of perishing (like an ignorant pilgrim crossing the snowy Alps in winter); so a watch-coat is not so much of a house as it is a mere envelope, or additional skin encasing you.

Much to be deplored, too, that our southern mast-heads lack those enviable crow’s-nests of the Greenlandmen. Captain Sleet, in his fireside narrative, describes his Sleet’s crow’s-nest—seat, locker, racks for trumpet and glass—nay, even a rifle to pop stray narwhales. He enlarges learnedly upon “binnacle deviations” and “azimuth compass observations;” but for all his azimuths, he knew that case-bottle nicely tucked within reach was a faithful comforter. Brave, honest, learned Captain; only why ignore that bottle?

But if we South fishers lack such snuggeries, our seas are serenity itself. I used to lounge up the rigging, rest a moment in the top, then throw a lazy leg over the top-sail yard for a preliminary survey, and at last mount to my destination.

Let me make a clean breast: I kept but sorry guard. With the problem of the universe revolving in me, how could I—being left to myself at that thought-engendering altitude—strictly obey, “Keep your weather eye open, and sing out every time”?

And let me in this place movingly admonish you, ye ship-owners of Nantucket! Beware of enlisting in your vigilant fisheries any lad with lean brow and hollow eye; given to unseasonable meditativeness; and who offers to ship with the Phædon instead of Bowditch in his head. Beware of such an one, I say; your whales must be seen before they can be killed; and this sunken-eyed young Platonist will tow you ten wakes round the world, and never make you one pint of sperm the richer. Nor are these monitions at all unneeded. For nowadays, the whale-fishery furnishes an asylum for many romantic, melancholy, and absent-minded young men, disgusted with the carking cares of earth, and seeking sentiment in tar and blubber. Childe Harold not unfrequently perches himself upon the mast-head of some luckless disappointed whale-ship, and in moody phrase ejaculates:—

“Roll on, thou deep and dark blue ocean, roll! Ten thousand
blubber-hunters sweep over thee in vain.”

Very often do the captains of such ships take those absent-minded young philosophers to task, upbraiding them with not feeling sufficient “interest” in the voyage; half-hinting that they are so hopelessly lost to all honorable ambition, as that in their secret souls they would rather not see whales than otherwise. But all in vain; those young Platonists have a notion that their vision is imperfect; they are short-sighted; what use, then, to strain the visual nerve? They have left their opera-glasses at home. “Why, thou monkey,” said a harpooneer to one of these lads, “we’ve been cruising now hard upon three years, and thou hast not raised a whale yet. Whales are scarce as hen’s teeth whenever thou art up here.” Perhaps they were; or perhaps there might have been shoals of them in the far horizon; but lulled into such an opium-like listlessness of vacant, unconscious reverie is this absent-minded youth by the blending cadence of waves with thoughts, that at last he loses his identity; takes the mystic ocean at his feet for the visible image of that deep, blue, bottomless soul, pervading mankind and nature; and every strange, half-seen, gliding, beautiful thing that eludes him; every dimly-discovered, uprising fin of some undiscernible form, seems to him the embodiment of those elusive thoughts that only people the soul by continually flitting through it. In this enchanted mood, thy spirit ebbs away to whence it came; becomes diffused through time and space; like Cranmer’s sprinkled Pantheistic ashes, forming at last a part of every shore the round globe over.

There is no life in thee, now, except that rocking life imparted by a gently rolling ship; by her, borrowed from the sea; by the sea, from the inscrutable tides of God. But while this sleep, this dream is on ye, move your foot or hand an inch; slip your hold at all; and your identity comes back in horror. Over Descartian vortices you hover; and perhaps at mid-day, in the fairest weather, with one half-throttled shriek you drop through that transparent air into the summer sea, no more to rise for ever. Heed it well, ye Pantheists!

It was not long after the affair of the pipe, that Ahab, as was his wont, paced the quarter-deck. Soon his steady, ivory stride was heard, as to and fro he paced his old rounds, upon planks so familiar to his tread, that they were all over dented, like geological stones, with the peculiar mark of his walk. Did you fixedly gaze, too, upon that ribbed and dented brow; there also, you would see still stranger foot-prints—the foot-prints of his one unsleeping, ever-pacing thought. “D’ye mark him, Flask?” whispered Stubb; “the chick that’s in him pecks the shell. ’Twill soon be out.” Near sunset he halted by the bulwarks, fixed his bone leg in the auger-hole, grasped a shroud, and ordered Starbuck to send everybody aft. “Mast-heads, there! come down!”

The ship’s company gathered. He paced; then suddenly: “What do ye do when ye see a whale, men?” “Sing out for him!” “And next?” “Lower away, and after him!” “And what tune is it ye pull to?” “A dead whale or a stove boat!” At every shout his countenance strangely and fiercely glowed.

Half-revolving in his pivot-hole, with one hand reaching high up a shroud, he held aloft a bright coin: “D’ye see this Spanish ounce of gold?—it is a sixteen dollar piece, men. D’ye see it? Mr. Starbuck, hand me yon top-maul.” While the mate was getting the hammer, Ahab, without speaking, was slowly rubbing the gold piece against the skirts of his jacket, as if to heighten its lustre, and without using any words was meanwhile lowly humming to himself, producing a sound so strangely muffled and inarticulate that it seemed the mechanical humming of the wheels of his vitality in him. Receiving the top-maul from Starbuck, he advanced towards the main-mast with the hammer uplifted in one hand, exhibiting the gold with the other, and with a high raised voice exclaiming: “Whosoever of ye raises me a white-headed whale with a wrinkled brow and a crooked jaw; whosoever of ye raises me that white-headed whale, with three holes punctured in his starboard fluke—look ye, whosoever of ye raises me that same white whale, he shall have this gold ounce, my boys!” He nailed the coin to the main-mast. “Skin your eyes for him, men; look sharp for white water; if ye see but a bubble, sing out.”

Tashtego, Daggoo, and Queequeg started at the traits. “Captain Ahab,” said Tashtego, “that white whale must be the same that some call Moby Dick.” “Moby Dick?” shouted Ahab. “Does he fan-tail a little curious, sir?” said the Gay-Header. “And has he a curious spout, very bushy?” said Daggoo. “And he have good many iron in him hide, too, all twiske-tee be-twisk,” cried Queequeg—“like him—him—” “Corkscrew!” cried Ahab. “Aye, Queequeg, the harpoons lie all twisted and wrenched in him; aye, Daggoo, his spout is a big one, like a whole shock of wheat, and white as a pile of our Nantucket wool after the great annual sheep-shearing; aye, Tashtego, and he fan-tails like a split jib in a squall. Death and devils! men, it is Moby Dick ye have seen—Moby Dick—Moby Dick!”

“Captain Ahab,” said Starbuck at last, “I have heard of Moby Dick—but it was not Moby Dick that took off thy leg?” “Who told thee that?—Aye, Starbuck; aye, my hearties all round; it was Moby Dick that dismasted me; Moby Dick that brought me to this dead stump I stand on now. Aye, aye,” he shouted with a terrific, loud, animal sob, like that of a heart-stricken moose; “Aye, aye! it was that accursed white whale that razeed me; made a poor pegging lubber of me for ever and a day!” Then tossing both arms, with measureless imprecations he shouted out: “Aye, aye! and I’ll chase him round Good Hope, and round the Horn, and round the Norway Maelstrom, and round perdition’s flames before I give him up. And this is what ye have shipped for, men! to chase that white whale on both sides of land, and over all sides of earth, till he spouts black blood and rolls fin out. What say ye, men—will ye splice hands on it, now?” “Aye, aye!” shouted harpooneers and seamen: “A sharp eye for the white whale; a sharp lance for Moby Dick!”

“God bless ye,” he half sobbed and half shouted. “Steward! go draw the great measure of grog. But what’s this long face about, Mr. Starbuck; wilt thou not chase the white whale? art not game for Moby Dick?” “I am game for his crooked jaw, and for the jaws of Death too, if it fairly comes in the way of the business we follow; but I came here to hunt whales, not my commander’s vengeance. How many barrels will thy vengeance yield thee? it will not fetch thee much in our Nantucket market.” “Nantucket market! Hoot!—come closer, Starbuck; thou requirest a little lower layer. If money’s to be the measurer, man, then let me tell thee, that my vengeance will fetch a great premium here!” He smote his chest. “He smites his chest,” whispered Stubb, “what’s that for? methinks it rings most vast, but hollow.” “Vengeance on a dumb brute!” cried Starbuck; “that simply smote thee from blindest instinct! Madness! To be enraged with a dumb thing, Captain Ahab, seems blasphemous.”

“Hark ye yet again—the little lower layer. All visible objects, man, are but as pasteboard masks. But in each event—in the living act, the undoubted deed—there, some unknown but still reasoning thing puts forth the mouldings of its features from behind the unreasoning mask. If man will strike, strike through the mask! How can the prisoner reach outside except by thrusting through the wall? To me, the white whale is that wall, shoved near to me. Sometimes I think there’s naught beyond. But ’tis enough. He tasks me; he heaps me; I see in him outrageous strength, with an inscrutable malice sinewing it. That inscrutable thing is chiefly what I hate; and be the white whale agent, or be the white whale principal, I will wreak that hate upon him. Talk not to me of blasphemy, man; I’d strike the sun if it insulted me. For could the sun do that, then could I do the other; since there is ever a sort of fair play herein, jealousy presiding over all creations. But not my master, man, is even that fair play. Who’s over me? Truth hath no confines. Take off thine eye! more intolerable than fiends’ glarings is a doltish stare! So, so; thou reddenest and palest; my heat has melted thee to anger-glow. But look ye, Starbuck, what is said in heat, that thing unsays itself. There are men from whom warm words are small indignity. I meant not to incense thee. Let it go. Look! see yonder Turkish cheeks of spotted tawn—living, breathing pictures painted by the sun. The Pagan leopards—the unrecking and unworshipping things, that live; and seek, and give no reasons for the torrid life they feel! The crew, man, the crew! Are they not one and all with Ahab, in this matter of the whale? See Stubb! he laughs! See yonder Chilian! he snorts to think of it. Stand up amid the general hurricane, thy one tost sapling cannot, Starbuck! And what is it? Reckon it. ’Tis but to help strike a fin; no wondrous feat for Starbuck. What is it more? From this one poor hunt, then, the best lance out of all Nantucket, surely he will not hang back, when every foremast-hand has clutched a whetstone? Ah! constrainings seize thee; I see! the billow lifts thee! Speak, but speak!—Aye, aye! thy silence, then, that voices thee. (Aside) Something shot from my dilated nostrils, he has inhaled it in his lungs. Starbuck now is mine; cannot oppose me now, without rebellion.”

“God keep me!—keep us all!” murmured Starbuck, lowly. But in his joy at the enchanted, tacit acquiescence of the mate, Ahab did not hear his foreboding invocation; nor yet the low laugh from the hold; nor yet the presaging vibrations of the winds in the cordage; nor yet the hollow flap of the sails against the masts, as for a moment their hearts sank in. For again Starbuck’s downcast eyes lighted up with the stubbornness of life; the subterranean laugh died away; the winds blew on; the sails filled out; the ship heaved and rolled as before. Ah, ye admonitions and warnings! why stay ye not when ye come? But rather are ye predictions than warnings, ye shadows! For with little external to constrain us, the innermost necessities in our being, these still drive us on.

“The measure! the measure!” cried Ahab.

He took the brimming pewter and ordered the harpooneers to produce their weapons. Ranging them before him near the capstan, with their harpoons in their hands, while his three mates stood at his side with their lances, and the rest of the ship’s company formed a circle, he stood for an instant searchingly eyeing every man. But those wild eyes met his, as the bloodshot eyes of the prairie wolves meet the eye of their leader, ere he rushes on at their head in the trail of the bison. “Drink and pass!” he cried, handing the heavy charged flagon to the nearest seaman. “The crew alone now drink. Round with it, round! Short draughts—long swallows, men; ’tis hot as Satan’s hoof. So, so; it goes round excellently. It spiralizes in ye; forks out at the serpent-snapping eye. Well done; almost drained. That way it went, this way it comes. Hand it me—here’s a hollow! Men, ye seem the years; so brimming life is gulped and gone. Steward, refill!

“Advance, ye mates! Cross your lances full before me. Well done! Let me touch the axis.” So saying, with extended arm, he grasped the three level, radiating lances at their crossed centre; suddenly and nervously twitching them; as though, by some nameless, interior volition, he would fain have shocked into them the same fiery emotion accumulated within the Leyden jar of his own magnetic life. The three mates quailed before his strong, sustained, and mystic aspect. Stubb and Flask looked sideways from him; the honest eye of Starbuck fell downright. “In vain!” cried Ahab; “but, maybe, ’tis well. For did ye three but once take the full-forced shock, then mine own electric thing, that had perhaps expired from out me. Perchance, too, it would have dropped ye dead. Perchance ye need it not. Down lances! And now, ye mates, I do appoint ye three cupbearers to my three pagan kinsmen there—yon three most honorable gentlemen and noblemen, my valiant harpooneers. Disdain the task? What, when the great Pope washes the feet of beggars, using his tiara for ewer? Oh, my sweet cardinals! your own condescension, that shall bend ye to it. Cut your seizings and draw the poles, ye harpooneers!”

Silently obeying, the three harpooneers now stood with the detached iron part of their harpoons, some three feet long, held, barbs up, before him. “Stab me not with that keen steel! Cant them; cant them over! know ye not the goblet end? Turn up the socket! So, so; now, ye cup-bearers, advance. The irons! take them; hold them while I fill!” Forthwith, slowly going from one officer to the other, he brimmed the harpoon sockets with the fiery waters from the pewter. “Now, three to three, ye stand. Commend the murderous chalices! Bestow them, ye who are now made parties to this indissoluble league. Ha! Starbuck! but the deed is done! Yon ratifying sun now waits to sit upon it. Drink, ye harpooneers! drink and swear, ye men that man the deathful whaleboat’s bow—Death to Moby Dick! God hunt us all, if we do not hunt Moby Dick to his death!”

The long, barbed steel goblets were lifted; and to cries and maledictions against the white whale, the spirits were simultaneously quaffed down with a hiss. Once more the pewter went; then, waving his free hand to them, they dispersed; and Ahab retired within his cabin.

By the stern windows Ahab sat alone, gazing out. “I leave a white and turbid wake; pale waters, paler cheeks. Yonder, by ever-brimming goblet’s rim, the warm waves blush like wine. The gold brow plumbs the blue. The diver sun—slow dived from noon—goes down; my soul mounts up! she wearies with her endless hill. Is, then, the crown too heavy that I wear? this Iron Crown of Lombardy. ’Tis iron—that I know—not gold. ’Tis split, too—that I feel; the jagged edge galls me so, my brain seems to beat against the solid metal; aye, steel skull, mine; the sort that needs no helmet in the most brain-battering fight!”

“Dry heat upon my brow? Oh! time was, when as the sunrise nobly spurred me, so the sunset soothed. No more. This lovely light, it lights not me; all loveliness is anguish to me, since I can ne’er enjoy. Gifted with the high perception, I lack the low, enjoying power; damned, most subtly and most malignantly! damned in the midst of Paradise! Good night—good night! (_waving his hand, he moves from the window_.)”

“’Twas not so hard a task. I thought to find one stubborn, at the least; but my one cogged circle fits into all their various wheels, and they revolve. Oh, hard! that to fire others, the match itself must needs be wasting! What I’ve dared, I’ve willed; and what I’ve willed, I’ll do! They think me mad—Starbuck does; but I’m demoniac, I am madness maddened! The prophecy was that I should be dismembered; and—Aye! I lost this leg. I now prophesy that I will dismember my dismemberer.”

“I laugh and hoot at ye, ye cricket-players, ye pugilists, ye deaf Burkes and blinded Bendigoes! No, ye’ve knocked me down, and I am up again; but ye have run and hidden. Come forth from behind your cotton bags! I have no long gun to reach ye. Come, Ahab’s compliments to ye; come and see if ye can swerve me. Swerve me? ye cannot swerve me, else ye swerve yourselves! The path to my fixed purpose is laid with iron rails, whereon my soul is grooved to run. Over unsounded gorges, through the rifled hearts of mountains, under torrents’ beds, unerringly I rush! Naught’s an obstacle, naught’s an angle to the iron way!”

My soul is more than matched; she’s overmanned; and by a madman! Insufferable sting, that sanity should ground arms on such a field! But he drilled deep down, and blasted all my reason out of me! I see his impious end; but feel that I must help him to it. Will I, nill I, the ineffable thing has tied me to him; tows me with a cable I have no knife to cut. Horrible old man! He would be a democrat to all above; look, how he lords it over all below! Oh! I see my miserable office,—to obey, rebelling; and worse yet, to hate with touch of pity! For in his eyes I read some lurid woe would shrivel me up, had I it. Yet is there hope. Time and tide flow wide. The hated whale has the round watery world; His heaven-insulting purpose, God may wedge aside. But my whole clock’s run down; my heart the all-controlling weight, I have no key to lift again.

Oh, God! to sail with such a heathen crew—small touch of human mothers in them—whelped by the sharkish sea. The white whale is their demigorgon. Hark! the infernal orgies! that revelry is forward! mark the unfaltering silence aft! Methinks it pictures life. Foremost through the sparkling sea shoots on the gay, embattled, bantering bow, but only to drag dark Ahab after it, where he broods within his sternward cabin, builded over the dead water of the wake, and further on, hunted by its wolfish gurglings. The long howl thrills me through! Peace! ye revellers, and set the watch! Oh, life! ’tis in an hour like this, with soul beat down to knowledge—Oh, life! ’tis now that I do feel the latent horror in thee! but ’tis not me! that horror’s out of me! and with the soft feeling of the human in me, yet will I try to fight ye, ye grim, phantom futures! Stand by me, hold me, bind me, O ye blessed influences!

Fore-top; Stubb, mending a brace. “Ha! ha! ha!—I’ve been thinking over it, and that ha, ha’s the final consequence; because a laugh’s the wisest, easiest answer to all that’s queer; and come what will, one comfort’s left—it’s all predestinated. I heard not all his talk with Starbuck; but be sure the old Mogul has fixed him, too. Well, Stubb—wise Stubb—what of it? Here’s a carcase. Be it what it will, I’ll go to it laughing. Such a waggish leering as lurks in all your horribles! I feel funny. Fa, la! lirra, skirra! What’s my juicy little pear at home doing now? Crying its eyes out?—Giving a party to the last arrived harpooneers, I dare say, gay as a frigate’s pennant, and so am I—fa, la! lirra, skirra! Oh—”

“We’ll drink to-night with hearts as light,
To love, as gay and fleeting
As bubbles that swim, on the beaker’s brim,
And break on the lips while meeting.”

“A brave stave that—who calls? Mr. Starbuck? Aye, aye, sir—just through with this job—coming.”

Midnight, Forecastle.

Foresail rises and discovers the watch, standing, lounging, leaning, and singing in chorus:

“Farewell and adieu to you, Spanish ladies!
Farewell and adieu to you, ladies of Spain!”

A Nantucket sailor: “Oh, boys, don’t be sentimental; it’s bad for the digestion! Take a tonic, follow me!”

“Our captain stood upon the deck,
A spy-glass in his hand,
… So, be cheery, my lads! may your hearts never fail!
While the bold harpooner is striking the whale!”

“Eight bells there, forward!” pipes the mate from aft. The bell-boy, Pip—black, slight, and sleepy—is bullied up.

Dutch Sailor: “Grand snoozing to-night, maty; fat night for that. We sing; they sleep—aye, lie down there, like ground-tier butts. Tell ’em it’s the resurrection; they must kiss their last, and come to judgment. That’s the way—that’s it; thy throat ain’t spoiled with eating Amsterdam butter.”

French Sailor: “Hist, boys! let’s have a jig or two before we ride to anchor in Blanket Bay. Pip! little Pip! hurrah with your tambourine!”
Pip (sulky): “Don’t know where it is.”
French Sailor: “Beat thy belly, then, and wag thy ears. Legs! legs!”

“Go it, Pip! Bang it, bell-boy! Make fire-flies!” cries an Azore; “there goes another jingler dropped off; I pound it so,” says Pip. Tashtego, quiet, smokes.

Old Manx Sailor: “I wonder whether those jolly lads bethink them of what they are dancing over. I’ll dance over your grave, I will—that’s the bitterest threat of your night-women, that beat head-winds round corners. O Christ! to think of the green navies and the green-skulled crews! Well, well; belike the whole world’s a ball; dance on, lads, you’re young; I was once.”

They cluster; the wind rises. “By Brahma!” cries a Lascar; “the sky-born Ganges turns to wind!”

Maltese Sailor (reclining and shaking his cap): “It’s the waves—the snow’s caps turn to jig it now. They’ll shake their tassels soon. Now would all the waves were women, then I’d go drown, and chassee with them evermore! There’s naught so sweet on earth—heaven may not match it!—as those swift glances of warm, wild bosoms in the dance.”

The Sicilian reclines. A Tahitian starts from his mat, hailing holy nakedness and Pirohitee’s torrents—then, “The blast! the blast! Up, spine, and meet it!”

“Stand by for reefing, hearties!” says a Portuguese. “Crack, old ship!” says the Dane.

“Our captain has his birthmark,” mutters the Manxman; “and there’s another in the sky—lurid-like, all else pitch black.”
“What of that?” growls Daggoo; “who’s afraid of black’s afraid of me!”
A Spanish sailor advances: “Aye, harpooneer, thy race is the undeniable dark side of mankind—devilish dark at that. No offence.”
Daggoo (grimly). “None.”

Knives glitter—“A row! a row!” Tashtego puffs: “Gods and men—both brawlers! Humph!” Old Manx Sailor: “Ready formed. There! the ringed horizon. In that ring Cain struck Abel. Sweet work, right work! No? Why then, God, mad’st thou the ring?”

“Hands by the halyards! in top-gallant sails! Stand by to reef topsails!” comes the cry from the quarter-deck. They scatter.

Pip cowers under the windlass: “Jollies? Lord help such jollies! Crish, crash! there goes the jib-stay! Blang-whang! Duck lower, Pip—here comes the royal yard! Worse than the whirled woods the last day of the year! Fine prospects to ’em; they’re on the road to heaven. Hold on hard! Jimmini, what a squall! But those chaps there are worse yet—they are your white squalls, they. White squalls? white whale—shirr! shirr! Here have I heard all their chat just now, and the white whale—shirr! shirr!—but spoken of once! and only this evening—it makes me jingle all over like my tambourine—that anaconda of an old man swore ’em in to hunt him! Oh, thou big white God aloft there somewhere in yon darkness, have mercy on this small black boy down here; preserve him from all men that have no bowels to feel fear!”

I, Ishmael, was one of that crew; my shouts went up with the rest; my oath was welded with theirs; and stronger I shouted, and more I hammered and clinched my oath, because of the dread in my soul. A wild, mystical, sympathetical feeling was in me; Ahab’s quenchless feud seemed mine. With greedy ears I learned the history of that murderous monster against whom we had sworn.

For some time past, though at intervals only, the unaccompanied, secluded White Whale had haunted those uncivilized seas mostly frequented by Sperm Whale fishermen. Yet not all knew of his existence; few had knowingly seen him; fewer still had actually given him battle. The causes were many: the wide sprinkling of cruisers over the watery circumference; solitary latitudes; year-long voyages without a news-telling sail; irregular sailings from home; and the fishery’s inordinate distances—all these long obstructed the spreading of specific tidings concerning Moby Dick. Of late the fishery had been marked by ferocity and cunning in many monsters; and those who ignorantly gave him battle ascribed their terrors to the general perils of the fishery more than to any individual cause.

At first, those who, hearing of the White Whale, by chance caught sight of him, lowered as boldly as for any whale; but calamities—no mere sprains or amputations, but fatal to the last degree—ensued; repeated repulses piled his terrors upon him, till they shook the fortitude of many brave hunters. Wild rumors, too, grew like fungi from the smitten trunk of fact. Sea-life breeds them; whalemen—superstitious beyond other sailors, and most conversant with appalling marvels—nourish them. Ever gathering volume in their world-circling transit, hints and half-formed foetal suggestions of supernatural agency invested Moby Dick with terrors unborrowed from the visible; so that few, who had at least heard of him by rumor, were willing to encounter his jaw.

Practical influences worked likewise. The Sperm Whale’s pre-eminent tremendousness, fearfully distinct from other leviathans, has never died out among whalemen. Right-whalemen, brave enough in northern warfare, shrank from this apparition of the South. Naturalists had helped awe along; yet the fishery’s realities amend such reports.

Thus overawed, men recalled the early days when it was hard to induce practised Right-whalemen to embark against the Sperm Whale; to attempt it, they protested, was to be torn into a quick eternity. Nevertheless, some there were who would give chase; and a greater number who, hearing vaguely, without specific calamities or superstitions, would not flee if battle were offered.

One conceit linked at last with the White Whale in credulous minds was that Moby Dick was ubiquitous; encountered in opposite latitudes at the same instant. Nor was the conceit wholly without plausibility: the secrets of currents are hidden, and the hidden ways of the Sperm Whale beneath the surface remain, in great part, unaccountable. Scoresby records whales taken far north in the Pacific with Greenland irons in them; from which some inferred that to the whale the Nor’ West Passage was never a problem. Forced into familiarity with such prodigies, and knowing the White Whale had escaped after repeated intrepid assaults, men went further: declaring him not only ubiquitous but immortal (for immortality is but ubiquity in time); that though groves of spears were planted in his flanks, he would still swim; that if ever he spouted thick blood, it were a ghastly deception, and his unsullied jet would again be seen hundreds of leagues away.

But strip him of superstitions, there was enough in his earthly make and incontestable character to strike imagination. It was not so much his bulk as his peculiar snow-white wrinkled forehead, and high pyramidical white hump—tokens whereby, in limitless seas, he revealed his identity afar. The rest of him was streaked, spotted, marbled with that shrouded hue; gliding at noon through a dark blue sea, he left a milky-way wake of creamy foam spangled with golden gleamings.

Nor his hue, nor his deformed lower jaw, so much invested him with terror, as his unexampled intelligent malignity; and, beyond all, those treacherous retreats when, feigning flight, he suddenly turned, bearing down, staving boats, or driving them in consternation back to their ship.

Fatalities had attended his chase. Though disasters are not uncommon, yet, in his case, such seemed the infernal aforethought that every death was not wholly laid to an unintelligent agent. Judge, then, to what pitches of fury desperate hunters were impelled, when, amid chips of chewed boats and sinking limbs of torn comrades, they swam out of the white curds of his direful wrath into the serene, exasperating sunlight, that smiled on, as if at a birth or a bridal.

Then one captain—Ahab—seizing a line-knife from his broken prow, dashed at the whale, as a duellist at his foe, blindly seeking with a six-inch blade to reach the fathom-deep life. Sweeping his sickle-shaped lower jaw, Moby Dick reaped away Ahab’s leg as a mower a blade of grass. No turbaned Turk, no hired Venetian or Malay, could have smote him with more seeming malice. Small reason to doubt that ever since, Ahab cherished a wild vindictiveness; and in his frantic morbidness he came to identify with the whale not only all bodily woes, but all intellectual and spiritual exasperations. The White Whale swam before him as the monomaniac incarnation of those malicious agencies which deep men feel eating in them. That intangible malignity which has been from the beginning; to whose dominion even the modern Christians ascribe one-half of the worlds; which the ancient Ophites of the east reverenced in their statue devil;—Ahab did not fall down and worship it like them; but deliriously transferring its idea to the abhorred white whale, he pitted himself, all mutilated, against it. All that most maddens and torments; all that stirs up the lees of things; all truth with malice in it; all that cracks the sinews and cakes the brain; all the subtle demonisms of life and thought; all evil, to crazy Ahab, were visibly personified, and made practically assailable in Moby Dick. He piled upon that white hump the sum of his race’s rage from Adam down; then, as if his chest were a mortar, burst his hot heart’s shell upon it.

His monomania did not, perhaps, rise at the instant of dismemberment. Then he but loosed a sudden corporal animosity; felt the bodily laceration and nothing more. But forced homeward, for long months Ahab and anguish lay together in one hammock, rounding in mid-winter the howling Patagonian Cape; then his torn body and gashed soul bled into one and made him mad. At intervals in the passage he was a raving lunatic; they laced him fast as he sailed, raving in his hammock. In more sufferable latitudes, under mild stun’sails, he seemed restored; issued calm orders; mates thanked God the madness had gone; yet in his hidden self he raved on. Human madness is oft a cunning, feline thing; fleeing, it but transmutes into subtler form. Ahab’s full lunacy subsided not, but deepeningly contracted; like the unabated Hudson, when that noble Northman flows narrowly, but unfathomably through the Highland gorge. None of his great natural intellect perished; his living agent became a living instrument; his special lunacy stormed his general sanity and turned its cannon on its own mad mark; so that, far from losing strength, to that one end he possessed a thousand-fold more potency than he had sanely brought to bear on any reasonable object.

This is much; yet Ahab’s larger, darker, deeper part remains unhinted. But vain to popularize profundities, and all truth is profound. Winding far down from within the very heart of this spiked Hotel de Cluny where we here stand—however grand and wonderful, now quit it;—and take your way, ye nobler, sadder souls, to those vast Roman halls of Thermes; where far beneath the fantastic towers of man’s upper earth, his root of grandeur, his whole awful essence sits in bearded state; an antique buried beneath antiquities, and throned on torsoes! So with a broken throne, the great gods mock that captive king; so like a Caryatid, he patient sits, upholding on his frozen brow the piled entablatures of ages. Wind ye down there, ye prouder, sadder souls! question that proud, sad king! A family likeness! aye, he did beget ye, ye young exiled royalties; and from your grim sire only will the old State-secret come.

He knew, in his heart, something of this: “All my means are sane, my motive and my object mad.” He dissembled to mankind, and in some sort still did; but that dissembling was subject to his perceptibility, not his will. So well did he succeed that, when he stepped ashore on his ivory leg, no Nantucketer thought him other than naturally grieved; his sea-delirium ascribed to grief; his moodiness to the quick of that casualty. Far from distrusting his fitness, the prudent isle deemed him, for those very reasons, edged for the bloody hunt. Gnawed within and scorched without by the fangs of an incurable idea, such an one seemed the very man to dart iron at the most appalling of brutes, or, failing that, to cheer and howl on his underlings to the attack. Be it as it may, certain it is, that with his mad secret bolted within, Ahab purposely sailed with the one, only, all-engrossing object of hunting the White Whale. Had one shore-friend half dreamed what lurked in him, they had wrenched the ship from such a fiendish man. They were bent on profits; he on an audacious, immitigable, supernatural revenge.

Here, then, was this grey-headed, ungodly old man, chasing with curses a Job’s whale round the world, at the head of a crew of mongrel renegades, castaways, and cannibals—morally enfeebled also, by the incompetence of mere unaided virtue or right-mindedness in Starbuck, the invulnerable jollity of indifference and recklessness in Stubb, and the pervading mediocrity in Flask. Such a crew, so officered, seemed specially picked by some infernal fatality to help him to his revenge. How they so aboundingly responded—by what evil magic their souls were possessed, till at times his hate seemed theirs; what the White Whale was to them, or how, in some dim, unsuspected way, he might have seemed to their unconscious understandings the gliding demon of the seas of life—all this were to dive deeper than Ishmael can go. The subterranean miner that works in us all—who can tell whither his shaft leads by the shifting, muffled sound of his pick? Who does not feel the irresistible arm drag? What skiff in tow of a seventy-four can stand still? For one, I gave myself up to the abandonment of the time and tide; yet, while all a-rush to encounter the whale, could see naught in that brute but the deadliest ill.

What the white whale was to Ahab has been hinted; what, at times, he was to me remains unsaid. Aside from those more obvious considerations, there was another thought, or rather vague, nameless horror concerning him, which at times by its intensity completely overpowered all the rest; and yet so mystical and well nigh ineffable was it, that I almost despair of putting it in a comprehensible form. It was the whiteness of the whale that above all things appalled me. But how can I hope to explain myself here; and yet, in some dim, random way, explain myself I must, else all these chapters might be naught. Though in many natural objects, whiteness refiningly enhances beauty—as in marbles, japonicas, and pearls; and though various nations have recognised a certain royal pre-eminence in this hue, the grand old kings of Pegu placing the title Lord of the White Elephants above all their other ascriptions, and the modern kings of Siam unfurling the same; and though the Hanoverian flag bears a snow-white charger, and the great Austrian Empire, Cæsarian, heir to overlording Rome, has for the imperial colour the same imperial hue; and though, besides all this, whiteness has been made significant of gladness—the Roman white stone marking a joyful day—and of many touching, noble things—the innocence of brides, the benignity of age, the ermine of the Judge, and the daily state of kings and queens drawn by milk-white steeds; and though in the higher mysteries it is the Persian white forked flame, Great Jove incarnate in a snow-white bull, and to the noble Iroquois the midwinter sacrifice of the sacred White Dog; and though directly from the Latin for white all Christian priests derive the name of the alb or tunic, and among the holy pomps of the Romish faith white is specially employed in the celebration of the Passion; and though in the Vision of St. John white robes are given to the redeemed, and the four-and-twenty elders stand clothed before the great white throne, and the Holy One that sitteth there white like wool; and though, finally, this pre-eminence in it applies to the human race itself, giving the white man ideal mastership over every dusky tribe; yet for all these accumulated associations, there lurks an elusive something in the innermost idea of this hue which strikes more of panic to the soul than that redness which affrights in blood.

Divorced from kindly associations and coupled with what is terrible, whiteness heightens terror to the furthest bounds. Witness the white bear and the white shark—their ghastly whiteness imparting an abhorrent mildness to their dumb gloating. Not the heraldic tiger so staggers courage as the white-shrouded ghostliness of those pale marauders. The French, alluding to the white, silent stillness of death, call that shark Requin—from Requiem.

Bethink thee of the albatross: in its wondrous bodily whiteness chiefly lurks the spell. Not Coleridge first threw that spell; but God’s great, unflattering laureate, Nature. I remember the first I ever saw—regal, unspotted, hooked Roman bill; at intervals it arched forth its vast archangel wings as if to embrace some holy ark; wondrous flutterings shook it; though bodily unharmed it uttered cries. Through its inexpressible, strange eyes, methought I peeped to secrets which took hold of God. As Abraham before the angels, I bowed myself; the white thing was so white, its wings so wide, and in those for ever exiled waters, I had lost the miserable warping memories of traditions and of towns. “Goney!” cried a sailor; the truth was in the bird’s whiteness.

So, too, the White Steed of the Prairies—a most imperial and archangelical apparition of that unfallen, western world—whose spiritual whiteness clothed him with divineness that enforced a nameless terror. And yet there are other whitenesses that lose strange glory and acquire a repelling chill: the Albino, invested in all-pervading pallor, more hideous than deformity; the White Squall; the White Hoods of Ghent masked in their snowy faction; the marble pallor of the dead, the shroud’s hue, and ghosts rising in a milk-white fog; yea, even Death, riding his pallid horse.

Why to the man of untutored ideality, who happens to be but loosely acquainted with the peculiar character of the day, does the bare mention of Whitsuntide marshal in the fancy such long, dreary, speechless processions of slow-pacing pilgrims, down-cast and hooded with new-fallen snow? Name alone can summon a spectralness: the White Tower; the White Mountains—whence, in peculiar moods, a gigantic ghostliness steals over the soul—while the thought of Virginia’s Blue Ridge is full of a soft, dewy, distant dreaminess; the White Sea exerts such a spectralness over the fancy, while that of the Yellow Sea lulls us with mortal thoughts of long lacquered mild afternoons on the waves; and “the tall pale man” of the Hartz.

Nor is it earthquakes and stampedoes alone that make tearless Lima the strangest, saddest city: for Lima has taken the white veil; and there is a higher horror in this whiteness of her woe. Old as Pizarro, this whiteness keeps her ruins for ever new; admits not the cheerful greenness of complete decay; spreads over her broken ramparts the rigid pallor of an apoplexy that fixes its own distortions.

To the common apprehension this phenomenon is not prime in terror; yet often the very thing that appals is the whiteness. A mariner, called by night to view his ship sailing through a midnight sea of milky whiteness, feels a silent dread, as if shoals of combed white bears swam around him; the lead assures soundings, but heart and helm go down till blue water is under him again. The backwoodsman views with indifference a prairie sheeted with snow; but not so the sailor in Antarctic scenes, where boundless churchyards grin with icy monuments and splintered crosses.

“White-lead chapter,” sayest thou? “White flag hung out from a craven soul?” Tell me, then, why a strong colt, smelling only the musk of a buffalo robe, starts and snorts with bursting eyes though he never knew the goring bisons of Oregon. No: but here thou beholdest even in a dumb brute, the instinct of the knowledge of the demonism in the world. So to me the milky sea, the frost-festoons, the windrowed snows—are the shaken robe to the frightened colt.

Though neither knows where lie the nameless things of which the mystic sign gives forth such hints; yet with me, as with the colt, somewhere those things must exist. Though in many of its aspects this visible world seems formed in love, the invisible spheres were formed in fright.

But not yet the incantation is solved. Why should that which is emblem of the Christian’s Deity be also intensifier of the appalling? Is it that by its indefiniteness it shadows forth the heartless voids and immensities of the universe, and thus stabs us from behind with the thought of annihilation, when beholding the white depths of the milky way? Or that whiteness is not so much a colour as the visible absence of colour; and at the same time the concrete of all colours; is it for these reasons that there is such a dumb blankness, full of meaning, in a wide landscape of snows—a colourless, all-colour of atheism from which we shrink? And when we remember the philosophers who tell us all other earthly hues—every stately or lovely emblazoning—the sweet tinges of sunset skies and woods; yea, and the gilded velvets of butterflies, and the butterfly cheeks of young girls; all these are but subtile deceits, not actually inherent in substances, but only laid on from without; so that all deified Nature absolutely paints like the harlot, whose allurements cover nothing but the charnel-house within; and when we consider further that the mystical cosmetic which produces every one of her hues—the great principle of light—for ever remains white or colorless in itself, and if operating without medium upon matter, would touch all objects, even tulips and roses, with its own blank tinge—pondering all this, the palsied universe lies before us a leper; and like travellers in Lapland refusing coloured glasses, the infidel gazes himself blind at the monumental white shroud that wraps all the prospect round him. And of all these things the Albino whale was the symbol. Wonder ye then at the fiery hunt?

“Hist! did you hear that noise, Cabaco?” Middle-watch; fair moon; the men, in a cordon from butt to scuttle-butt, passed the buckets on the hallowed quarter-deck, careful not to speak or rustle. It was in the midst of this repose, that Archy, one of the cordon, whose post was near the after-hatches, whispered to his neighbor, a Cholo, the words above.

“There it is again—under the hatches—don’t you hear it—a cough—it sounded like a cough.”

“Cough be damned! Pass along that return bucket.”

“There again—there it is!—it sounds like two or three sleepers turning over, now!”

“Caramba! it’s the three soaked biscuits ye ate for supper turning over inside of ye—look to the bucket!”

“I’ve sharp ears.”

“Aye, you’re the chap that heard the old Quakeress’s knitting-needles fifty miles from Nantucket—the chap.”

“Grin away; Hark ye, Cabaco, there is somebody down in the after-hold that has not yet been seen on deck; and I suspect our old Mogul knows something of it too. I heard Stubb tell Flask, one morning watch, that there was something of that sort in the wind.”

“Tish! the bucket!”

Follow Ahab to his cabin after the oath-bound night, and you would see him draw forth wrinkled yellow sea-charts, spread them on the screwed-down table, and trace courses across blank spaces. Piles of old log-books lay at his hand—seasons and places of former captures collated—and the heavy pewter lamp suspended in chains over his head, continually rocked with the motion of the ship, and for ever threw shifting gleams and shadows of lines upon his wrinkled brow, till it almost seemed that while he himself was marking out lines and courses on the wrinkled charts, some invisible pencil was also tracing lines and courses upon the deeply marked chart of his forehead.

This was his nightly work. With all four oceans before him, he threaded a maze of currents and eddies toward that monomaniac thought. To the unacquainted, such a quest might seem absurd—one solitary creature in unhooped oceans. Not so to Ahab, who knew the sets of tides and the drifts of the sperm whale’s food, the seasons and latitudes; from these he derived surmises approaching certainties, the timeliest day to be upon this or that ground. So assured is the whale’s periodicalness that some would chart his migrations like swallows.

Moreover, in passages between grounds, sperm whales, guided by some infallible instinct—say, rather, secret intelligence from the Deity—mostly swim in veins, ocean-lines straight as surveyors’ parallels, a few miles broad; continuing their way along a given ocean-line with such undeviating exactitude, that no ship ever sailed her course, by any chart, with one tithe of such marvellous precision. Within that magic zone, at set seasons, they may with confidence be looked for. Hence Ahab could hope for encounters on the grounds, and even as he crossed between them.

Yet herds shift; solitaries shift; and Moby Dick’s known resorts were but ocean-inns, not abodes. The crowning datum was the Season-on-the-Line—years of periodic lingerings where most deadly encounters had occurred, where the waves were storied with his deeds, and where the monomaniac old man found the motive to his vengeance. But he would not rest his all upon one flattering fact; nor lull his vow’s sleeplessness with any postponement.

The Pequod sailed at the season’s very beginning; no doubling Cape Horn and reaching the equatorial Pacific in time. He must wait the ensuing year; and perhaps he chose it so, to spend the interval in hunt, should the White Whale turn up in Persian Gulf, Bengal Bay, China Seas, or any waters haunted by his race. Monsoons or Trades might blow him into the zig-zag of the Pequod’s wake.

“Mad,” say you, to recognize a single whale again? No. Moby Dick’s snow-white brow and hump were unmistakable. And have I not tallied the whale, Ahab would mutter to himself, as after poring over his charts till long after midnight he would throw himself back in reveries—tallied him, and shall he escape? His broad fins are bored, and scalloped out like a lost sheep’s ear! Then his mad mind would run in a breathless race till faintness came, and on deck he would seek to recover his strength. Ah, God! what trances of torments does that man endure who is consumed with one unachieved revengeful desire. He sleeps with clenched hands; and wakes with his own bloody nails in his palms.

At times intolerable dreams resumed the day’s intense thoughts, carried them on amid a clashing of phrensies, and whirled them round and round and round in his blazing brain, till the very throbbing of his life-spot became insufferable anguish; and when, as was sometimes the case, these spiritual throes heaved his being from its base, a chasm seemed opening in him, from which forked flames and lightnings shot up, and accursed fiends beckoned him to leap down among them; when this hell in himself yawned beneath him, a wild cry would be heard through the ship; and with glaring eyes Ahab would burst from his state room, as though escaping from a bed that was on fire. Yet these, instead of symptoms of some latent weakness or fright at his own resolve, were tokens of its intensity. For the Ahab that had gone to his hammock—the scheming, unappeasedly steadfast hunter of the white whale—was not the agent that so caused him to burst from it in horror again. The latter was the eternal, living principle or soul in him; and in sleep, being for the time dissociated from the characterizing mind that at other times employed it, it sought escape from the scorching contiguity of the frantic thing, of which, for the time, it was no longer an integral. But as the mind does not exist unless leagued with the soul, therefore it must have been that, in Ahab’s case, yielding up all his thoughts and fancies to his one supreme purpose; that purpose, by its own sheer inveteracy of will, forced itself against gods and devils into a kind of self-assumed, independent being of its own. Nay, could grimly live and burn, while the common vitality to which it was conjoined, fled horror-stricken from the unbidden and unfathered birth. Therefore the tormented spirit that glared out of bodily eyes, when what seemed Ahab rushed from his room, was for the time but a vacated thing, a formless somnambulistic being, a ray of living light, to be sure, but without an object to colour, and therefore a blankness in itself. God help thee, old man, thy thoughts have created a creature in thee; and he whose intense thinking thus makes him a Prometheus; a vulture feeds upon that heart for ever; that vulture the very creature he creates.

So far as what there may be of a narrative in this book; the foregoing matter requires familiar enlargement, to take away incredulity touching the natural verity of the main points. I shall not proceed methodically, but be content to produce the desired impression by separate citations of items, practically known to me as a whaleman.

First: I have personally known three instances in which a harpooned whale escaped, and, after an interval—even three years—was again struck by the same hand and slain; when the two irons, each marked with the same private cypher, were taken from the body. In the instance where three years intervened between the two darts, the man who flung them had in the interval voyaged to Africa, gone inland with a discovery party, and wandered for nearly two years amid serpents, savages, tigers, poisonous miasmas—perils incident to roaming unknown regions. Meanwhile, the whale he had struck must also have been on its travels; no doubt it had thrice circumnavigated the globe, brushing with its flanks all the coasts of Africa; but to no purpose. This man and this whale again came together, and the one vanquished the other. In two of the three I saw the whales struck, and afterwards the two irons with their respective marks taken from the dead fish. In the three-year instance, I was in the boat both times, first and last, and the last time distinctly recognised a huge mole under the whale’s eye which I had observed there three years previous.

Secondly: In the fishery there have been whales popularly cognisable at distant times and places, not solely for bodily peculiarity (for that the try-works soon end), but for their terrible prestige; most fishermen were content to recognise such a one by merely touching their tarpaulins when he lounged by on the sea, without seeking closer acquaintance. Like some poor devils ashore that happen to know an irascible great man, they make distant unobtrusive salutations to him in the street, lest if they pursued the acquaintance further, they might receive a summary thump for their presumption. Was it not so, O Timor Tom! thou famed leviathan, scarred like an iceberg, haunting the Oriental straits? Was it not so, O New Zealand Jack! thou terror of all cruisers near the Tattoo Land? Was it not so, O Morquan! King of Japan, whose lofty jet at times seemed a snow-white cross? Was it not so, O Don Miguel! thou Chilian whale, marked like an old tortoise with mystic hieroglyphics upon the back! Some of these, having wrought havoc, were gone in quest of, systematically hunted, chased, and killed by valiant captains, as Captain Butler hunted Annawon through Narragansett woods.

I do not know where I can find a better place than just here to make mention of one or two other things, which to me seem important, as in printed form establishing in all respects the reasonableness of the whole story of the White Whale, more especially the catastrophe; for this is one of those disheartening instances where truth requires full as much bolstering as error. So ignorant are most landsmen of some of the plainest and most palpable wonders of the world, that without some hints touching the plain facts they might scout at Moby Dick as a monstrous fable, or still worse and more detestable, a hideous and intolerable allegory.

Though most men have some vague ideas of the general perils of the grand fishery, they have nothing like a fixed, vivid conception of those perils and the frequency with which they recur. Not one disaster in fifty finds record. Do you suppose that that poor fellow there, who this moment perhaps caught by the whale-line off the coast of New Guinea, is being carried down to the bottom of the sea by the sounding leviathan—do you suppose that that poor fellow’s name will appear in the newspaper obituary you will read to-morrow at your breakfast? No: because the mails are very irregular between here and New Guinea. In fact, did you ever hear what might be called regular news direct or indirect from New Guinea? Yet I tell you that upon one particular voyage which I made to the Pacific, among many others we spoke thirty different ships, every one of which had had a death by a whale, some of them more than one, and three that had each lost a boat’s crew. For God’s sake, be economical with your lamps and candles! not a gallon you burn but at least one drop of man’s blood was spilled for it.

People ashore admit a whale’s enormousness, but when narrating some specific example of this two-fold enormousness, they have significantly complimented me upon my facetiousness; when, I declare upon my soul, I had no more idea of being facetious than Moses, when he wrote the history of the plagues of Egypt. I rest on independent testimony that the sperm whale can, with aforethought, stave in, utterly destroy, and sink a large ship—and has done it.

In 1820 the ship Essex, Captain Pollard, of Nantucket, lowered for a shoal of sperm whales. A very large one, escaping from the boats, bore directly down upon the ship; dashing his forehead against her hull, he so stove her in that in less than ten minutes she settled and fell over; not a plank survives. Owen Chace, the chief mate, wrote a plain narrative: “It was anything but chance which directed his operations; he made two several attacks upon the ship ahead, thereby combining the speed of the two objects for the shock; his aspect was most horrible, indicative of resentment and fury.” I have seen Owen Chace, who was chief mate of the Essex at the time of the tragedy; I have read his plain and faithful narrative; I have conversed with his son; and all this within a few miles of the scene of the catastrophe.

In 1807 the ship Union, also of Nantucket, was totally lost off the Azores by a like onset. And Commodore J——, sceptical over a whaling-captains’ dinner in Oahu that any whale could make his stout sloop-of-war leak so much as a thimbleful, was presently met at sea by a portly sperm whale who fetched him such a thwack that, with all pumps going, he ran for the nearest port to heave down. I am not superstitious; but I consider the Commodore’s interview providential. Was not Saul of Tarsus converted from unbelief by a similar fright? I tell you, the sperm whale will stand no nonsense.

Take Langsdorff: “An uncommon large whale, the body larger than the ship itself, lay almost at the surface; the ship, in full sail, was almost upon him; it was impossible to prevent striking. The monster, setting up its back, raised the ship three feet at least out of the water... we thought we had struck a rock; but the creature sailed off solemnly.” The Captain D’Wolf here alluded to—a New Englander, my uncle—substantiates every word.

Lionel Wafer, Dampier’s chum, tells of a terrible shock at sea at four in the morning; guns leaped; men were shaken from hammocks; no ground found with the lead. He imputed it to an earthquake; but I should not wonder if an unseen whale vertically bumped the hull.

As for strength and malice: lines made fast to a running whale have towed a ship in a calm as a horse walks off with a cart; more than once a struck sperm whale, rallying, has pursued boats back to the ship; and upon attack he often opens his mouth and retains it so for minutes, eloquent indication of his character.

But I must be content with only one more and a concluding illustration; a remarkable and most significant one, by which you will not fail to see, that not only is the most marvellous event in this book corroborated by plain facts of the present day, but that these marvels (like all marvels) are mere repetitions of the ages; so that for the millionth time we say amen with Solomon—Verily there is nothing new under the sun.

In the sixth Christian century lived Procopius of Constantinople, in the days of Justinian and Belisarius; by the best authorities he has been considered a most trustworthy and unexaggerating historian, save in one or two particulars not at all affecting what follows. In his history he mentions that, during his prefecture, a great sea-monster was captured in the Propontis (Sea of Marmora) after destroying vessels at intervals for more than fifty years—a fact set down in substantial history. Of what precise species he does not say; but as the creature stove ships, he must have been a whale; and I am strongly inclined to think a sperm whale. For though the Mediterranean and its connexions are no habitual resort of the sperm whale, modern instances prove him there at times; a vessel of war readily passes the Dardanelles—by the same route a sperm whale might pass out of the Mediterranean into the Propontis. In the Propontis, as far as I can learn, none of that peculiar substance called brit—the aliment of the right whale—is to be found; but I have every reason to believe that the food of the sperm whale—squid or cuttle-fish—lurks at the bottom of that sea. If, then, you properly put these statements together, and reason upon them a bit, you will clearly perceive that, according to all human reasoning, Procopius’s sea-monster, that for half a century stove the ships of a Roman Emperor, must in all probability have been a sperm whale; and so, the reasonableness of the whole story of the White Whale, catastrophe and all, stands affirmed.

Though consumed with the hot fire of his purpose, Ahab might be far too wedded by nature and long habit to a fiery whaleman’s ways wholly to abandon the collateral prosecution of the voyage. It would be refining too much to say his vindictiveness towards the White Whale extended to all sperm whales, multiplying chances by multiplying slain; but other considerations swayed him.

To accomplish his object Ahab must use tools; and of all tools used in the shadow of the moon, men are most apt to get out of order. He knew that however magnetic his ascendency over Starbuck, it did not cover the complete spiritual man any more than corporeal superiority involves intellectual mastership. Starbuck’s body and coerced will were Ahab’s while the magnet lay at his brain; yet in his soul he abhorred the quest and would frustrate it. It might be a long interval ere the White Whale was seen; during it Starbuck would relapse into open rebellion unless common prudential influences bore on him. Not only that, but the subtle insanity of Ahab respecting Moby Dick was noways more significantly manifested than in his superlative sense and shrewdness in foreseeing that, for the present, the hunt should in some way be stripped of that strange imaginative impiousness which naturally invested it; that the full terror of the voyage must be kept withdrawn into the obscure background (for few men’s courage is proof against protracted meditation unrelieved by action); that when they stood their long night watches, his officers and men must have some nearer things to think of than Moby Dick. For however eagerly they had hailed the quest, all sailors of all sorts are more or less capricious and unreliable—they live in the varying outer weather, and they inhale its fickleness—and so temporary interests and employments must intervene and hold them healthily suspended for the final dash.

Nor was Ahab unmindful of another thing. In times of strong emotion mankind disdain all base considerations; but such times are evanescent. The permanent constitutional condition of the manufactured man, thought Ahab, is sordidness. Grant that the White Whale breeds a certain generous knight-errantism in them; still, while for love they give chase, they must have food for their more common appetites. Even the high lifted and chivalric Crusaders were not content to traverse two thousand miles without burglaries and pious perquisites by the way. Had they been held strictly to one romantic end, too many had turned in disgust. I will not strip these men, thought Ahab, of all hopes of cash—aye, cash. They may scorn cash now; but let some months go by, and no perspective promise of it to them, and then this same quiescent cash all at once mutinying in them, this same cash would soon cashier Ahab.

He had, too, by prematurely revealing the private purpose of the Pequod’s voyage, laid himself open to the charge of usurpation; with impunity the crew might refuse obedience, even wrest command. His only protection lay in predominating brain, heart, and hand, backed by a heedful, calculating attention to every minute atmospheric influence affecting his men.

For all these reasons he saw he must still, in a good degree, continue true to the nominal purpose: observe usages, and even evince passionate interest in the general pursuit. His voice now often hailed the mast-heads; let no porpoise go unreported. Vigilance was not long without reward.

It was a cloudy, sultry afternoon; the seamen lazily lounged about the decks, or vacantly gazed into the lead-coloured waters. Queequeg and I were weaving a sword-mat for our boat’s lashing. So subdued and preluding was the scene, and such an incantation of reverie lurked in the air, that each silent sailor seemed resolved into his own invisible self.

I, the attendant, passed the woof of marline between the warp with my hand for shuttle; Queequeg, standing sideways, ever and anon slid his heavy oaken sword between the threads, and, idly looking off, carelessly drove home every yarn: so strange a dreaminess did there then reign all over the ship and all over the sea, only broken by the intermitting dull sound of the sword, that it seemed as if this were the Loom of Time, and I myself were a shuttle mechanically weaving and weaving away at the Fates. There lay the fixed threads of the warp, subject to but one ever-returning vibration. This warp seemed necessity; and here, thought I, with my own hand I ply my own shuttle and weave my own destiny into these unalterable threads. Meantime, Queequeg’s impulsive, indifferent sword—sometimes hitting slantingly or strongly, sometimes weakly—shaped the fabric; this easy, indifferent sword must be chance—aye, chance, free will, and necessity—nowise incompatible—all interweavingly working together. The straight warp of necessity, not to be swerved from its ultimate course—its every alternating vibration, indeed, only tending to that; free will still free to ply her shuttle between given threads; and chance, though restrained in its play within the right lines of necessity, and sideways in its motions directed by free will, though thus prescribed to by both, chance by turns rules either, and has the last featuring blow at events.

Thus we were weaving and weaving away when a sound so strange, long, and musically wild and unearthly came that the ball of free will dropped from my hand, and I stood gazing up at the clouds whence that voice dropped like a wing. High in the cross-trees that mad Gay-Header, Tashtego, leaned forward, hand stretched like a wand; at brief sudden intervals he continued his cries.

As he stood hovering over you half suspended in air, so wildly and eagerly peering towards the horizon, you would have thought him some prophet or seer beholding the shadows of Fate, and by those wild cries announcing their coming.

“There she blows! there! there! there! she blows! she blows!”

“Where-away?”

“On the lee-beam, about two miles off! a school of them!”

Instantly all was commotion.
