The Sperm Whale blows as a clock ticks, with the same undeviating and reliable uniformity—and thereby whalemen distinguish him. “There go flukes!” cried Tashtego, and the whales disappeared. “Time! time!” called Ahab. Dough-Boy ran, glanced at the watch, and reported the minute. The ship kept away; cranes were thrust out; line tubs fixed; the mainyard was backed, and the three boats swung over the sea like samphire baskets over high cliffs; outside the bulwarks their eager crews with one hand clung to the rail, while one foot was expectantly poised on the gunwale. So look the long line of man-of-war’s men about to throw themselves on board an enemy’s ship.

But at that instant a sudden exclamation drew every eye from the whale. Dark Ahab stood surrounded by five dusky phantoms, fresh-formed out of air.

The phantoms—so they then seemed—flitted noiseless on the starboard side, casting loose the tackles of the captain’s spare boat. Tall and swart by its bows stood one with a single white tooth wickedly protruding from steel-like lips; a funereal Chinese jacket and wide black trowsers clothed him; but, strangest of all, a glistening white plaited turban crowned the ebonness—his own living hair coiled round and round. Less swart, his companions were tiger-yellow men of the Manillas—of that vivid complexion peculiar to some of the aboriginal natives there;—a race notorious for a certain diabolism of subtilty, and by some honest white mariners supposed to be the paid spies and secret confidential agents on the water of the devil, their lord, whose counting-room they suppose to be elsewhere.

“All ready there, Fedallah?” cried Ahab.

“Ready,” hissed the white-turbaned old man.

“Lower away then; d’ye hear?—Lower away there, I say!”

With the sheaves whirring, three boats wallowed into the sea; the sailors, goat-like, leaped to them from the rolling hull. Hardly had they shot clear when a fourth keel rounded under the stern—Ahab’s—rowed by the five strangers. He stood erect, hailing, “Spread yourselves!” But, with eyes riveted upon Fedallah and his crew, the other boats stared.

“Captain Ahab?—” said Starbuck.

“Spread yourselves,” cried Ahab; “give way, all four boats. Thou, Flask, pull out more to leeward!”

“Aye, aye, sir,” cheerily cried little King-Post, sweeping his great steering-oar. “Lay back! There!—there again! There she blows right ahead, boys!” “Never heed yonder yellow boys, Archy.”

“Oh, I don’t mind ’em, sir,” said Archy; “I knew it all before now. Didn’t I hear ’em in the hold? And didn’t I tell Cabaco here of it? They are stowaways, Mr. Flask.”

“What think ye of those yellow boys, sir?” bawled Stubb towards Starbuck, whose face, flinty-set, never turned.

“Smuggled aboard,” came the grave quick whisper; “but never mind, Mr. Stubb—(seethe her, my lads!)—duty and profit, hand in hand!”

“Aye, aye; I thought as much,” soliloquized Stubb. “The White Whale’s at the bottom of it. Well then—give way, men! It ain’t the White Whale to-day!”

To soothe his uneasy crew he preached in that queer mingled fun-and-fury which was his: “Pull, pull, my fine hearts-alive; pull, my children; pull, my little ones! Why don’t you break your backbones, my boys? What is’t you stare at? Those chaps yonder? Tut!—they’re only five more hands to help us—the more the merrier. So, so; there you are now—long and strong! That’s the stroke for a thousand pounds; that’s the stroke to sweep the stakes! Hurrah for the gold cup of sperm oil, my heroes! Easy—easy; don’t be in a hurry. Why don’t you snap your oars, you rascals? Bite something, you dogs! Stop snoring, ye sleepers, and pull. Pull, will ye? pull, can’t ye? pull, won’t ye? Why in the name of gudgeons and ginger-cakes don’t ye pull?—pull and break something! pull, and start your eyes out! Here!”—and out whipped his knife—“every mother’s son of ye draw his knife, and pull with the blade between his teeth. That’s it—that’s it, my steel-bits. Start her—start her, my silver-spoons! Start her, marling-spikes!”

Stubb’s exordium is given here at large, for it shows his religion of rowing. He would say the most terrific things to his crew, in a tone so strangely compounded of fun and fury, and the fury seemed so calculated merely as a spice to the fun, that no oarsman could hear such queer invocations without pulling for dear life, and yet pulling for the mere joke of the thing. Besides, he all the time looked so easy and indolent himself, so loungingly managed his steering-oar, and so broadly gaped—open-mouthed at times—that the mere sight of such a yawning commander, by sheer force of contrast, acted like a charm upon the crew. Then again, Stubb was one of those odd sort of humorists, whose jollity is sometimes so curiously ambiguous, as to put all inferiors on their guard in the matter of obeying them.

Meanwhile, ranging most to windward, Ahab’s boat kept foremost; a potent crew pulled him—those tiger-yellow creatures of his rose and fell like five trip-hammers; Fedallah, stripped to the waist, bent at the harpooneer oar; and Ahab, one arm thrown back like a fencer’s, steered as in a thousand lowerings ere the White Whale had torn him. All at once his outstretched arm signaled; the five oars peaked; boat and crew sat motionless. The whales had irregularly settled; from his closer ken, Ahab had seen it.

“Every man look out along his oars!” cried Starbuck. “Thou, Queequeg, stand up!”

The savage sprang to the triangular bow-box and gazed intently. Astern, Starbuck himself, cool and adroit, balanced on the stern platform, silently eyeing the vast blue eye of the sea. Near by, little King-Post, not content with the loggerhead’s palm-breadth, mounted Daggoo’s shoulders; the gigantic negro rolled his noble form to the sea’s rhythm, while flaxen Flask, on his back, looked a snow-flake on basalt. Though vivacious little Flask stamped with impatience, not one added heave did he thereby give to the negro’s lordly chest; so have I seen Passion and Vanity stamping the living magnanimous earth, but the earth did not alter her tides and her seasons for that.

Stubb, for his part, would solace the lull with his pipe; but even as he struck fire on his palm, Tashtego, star-staring to windward, dropped like light to his thwart and cried, “Down, down all, and give way!—there they are!”

To a landsman nothing were visible then but a troubled bit of greenish-white water and thin puffs of vapor blowing to leeward like scud; beneath that wavering air, and half beneath a thin sheet of sea, the whales swam—their spoutings their forerunning couriers.

All four boats drove for the dancing whiteness. “Pull, pull, my good boys,” whispered Starbuck with his keen needle-eyes dead ahead—his silence cut now and then by a whisper harsh with command or soft with entreaty. Not so Flask: he raved and stamped on his hat, flinging it seaward, roaring to be beached on their black backs. “Look at that chap now,” drawled Stubb between his unlighted pipe. “He’s got fits—that Flask has. Merrily, babes—pull; but take it easy!” At times his vivid cries made one or two snatch a fearful look over the shoulder; but this was against all rule; for the oarsmen must put out their eyes, and ram a skewer through their necks; usage pronouncing that they must have no organs but ears, and no limbs but arms, in these critical moments.

But what words Ahab poured into his tiger crew are best omitted here; for you live under the blessed light of the evangelical land. Only the infidel sharks in the audacious seas may give ear to such words, when, with tornado brow, and eyes of red murder, and foam-glued lips, Ahab leaped after his prey.

The sight was full of awe—the omnipotent swells shouldering our low gunwales; the tipped knife-edge pauses and sled-like slides; cries of headsmen and harpooneers; the shuddering gasps of oarsmen; while the ivory Pequod bore down with outstretched sails like a wild hen after her screaming brood. Not the raw recruit, marching from the bosom of his wife into the fever heat of his first battle; not the dead man’s ghost encountering the first unknown phantom in the other world;—neither of these can feel stranger and stronger emotions than that man does, who for the first time finds himself pulling into the charmed, churned circle of the hunted sperm whale.

The dun cloud-shadows deepening made the white waters telling clear. Our sail drew; with the rising wind we rushed so mad that the lee oars scarce could keep from tearing out. Mist swallowed ship and boats. “Give way,” whispered Starbuck, hauling his sheet; “time to kill a fish ere the squall! There’s white water—close! Spring!”

Cries on either beam told the other boats were fast. Hardly heard, when Starbuck’s lightning whisper: “Stand up!” and Queequeg sprang, iron poised. None of us oarsmen, backs turned to peril, saw the life-and-death thing, but reading the mate’s face we felt the instant. “That’s his hump. There—there—give it to him!” A short rushing; the iron leapt. Then, in one welded commotion—an astern push; a forward shock as on a ledge; sail collapsing; a scalding jet; an earthquake underfoot; and we, half-suffocated, were tossed helter-skelter into the white curdling cream. Squall, whale, and harpoon all blended; the whale, but grazed, escaped.

Swamped, yet nearly whole, the boat floated. We swam her round, crossed oars for thwarts, and tumbled in, sitting knee-deep in sea; the craft seemed a coral boat grown from the bottom. The wind howled; bucklers of waves clashed; the squall roared and crackled like a white prairie-fire. Vain to hail the others. Night thickened with rack and scud; no Pequod. Baling was forbid by the rising sea; oars were life-preservers now. Starbuck, after many failures, got the lantern lit; set it on a waif-pole and gave it Queequeg—the standard-bearer of our forlorn hope. There he sat, holding up that imbecile candle in the heart of that almighty forlornness; the sign and symbol of a man without faith, hopelessly holding up hope in the midst of despair.

Dawn, and still the mist spread. The empty lantern lay crushed. Suddenly Queequeg sprang, hand to ear. We too heard faint creakings of ropes. Nearer; the fog parted to a huge vague form. Affrighted, we leaped for our lives as the ship loomed right upon us. Our abandoned boat gaped beneath her bows like a chip at a cataract’s base; the vast hull rolled over it; it weltered astern; we swam for it, were dashed against, and at last were taken up and landed aboard. Ere the squall’s close the other boats had cut loose and come in. The ship had given us up, but cruised still, if haply she might light on some token of our perishing—an oar or a lance pole.

There are certain queer times and occasions in this strange mixed affair we call life when a man takes this whole universe for a vast practical joke, though the wit thereof he but dimly discerns, and more than suspects that the joke is at nobody’s expense but his own. However, nothing dispirits, and nothing seems worth while disputing. He bolts down all events, all creeds, and beliefs, and persuasions, all hard things visible and invisible, never mind how knobby; as an ostrich of potent digestion gobbles down bullets and gun flints. And as for small difficulties and worryings, prospects of sudden disaster, peril of life and limb; all these, and death itself, seem to him only sly, good-natured hits, and jolly punches in the side bestowed by the unseen and unaccountable old joker. That odd sort of wayward mood I am speaking of, comes over a man only in some time of extreme tribulation; it comes in the very midst of his earnestness, so that what just before might have seemed to him a thing most momentous, now seems but a part of the general joke. There is nothing like the perils of whaling to breed this free and easy sort of genial, desperado philosophy; and with it I now regarded this whole voyage of the Pequod, and the great White Whale its object.

“Queequeg,” said I, shaking off the water, “does this often happen?” He, as soaked as I, gave me to understand that it did. “Mr. Stubb,” said I to that worthy, smoking in the rain, “our prudent Starbuck—was that his discretion, going plump on a whale with sail set in a foggy squall?” “Certain. I’ve lowered for whales from a leaking ship in a gale off Cape Horn.” “Mr. Flask,” said I, turning to little King-Post, “is it law for an oarsman to break his back pulling himself back-foremost into death’s jaws?” “Can’t you twist that smaller?” said Flask. “Yes, that’s the law. I should like to see a boat’s crew backing water up to a whale face foremost. Ha, ha! the whale would give them squint for squint, mind that!”

So, considering capsizings common; that at the critical instant of going-on I must resign my life to a steersman stamping the craft to scuttles; that our late disaster was chiefly from Starbuck’s driving on to his whale in a squall—and he most heedful of men; that I belonged to his boat; and in what a devil’s chase I was implicated touching the White Whale—I thought I might as well go below and make a rough draft of my will. “Queequeg,” said I, “come along; you shall be my lawyer, executor, and legatee.” It may seem strange that of all men sailors should be tinkering at their last wills and testaments, but there are no people in the world more fond of that diversion. This was the fourth time in my nautical life that I had done the same thing. After the ceremony was concluded upon the present occasion, I felt all the easier; a stone was rolled away from my heart. Besides, all the days I should now live would be as good as the days that Lazarus lived after his resurrection; a supplementary clean gain of so many months or weeks as the case might be. I survived myself; my death and burial were locked up in my chest. I looked round me tranquilly and contentedly, like a quiet ghost with a clean conscience sitting inside the bars of a snug family vault. Now then, thought I, unconsciously rolling up the sleeves of my frock, here goes for a cool, collected dive at death and destruction, and the devil fetch the hindmost.

“Who would have thought it, Flask!” cried Stubb; “if I had but one leg you would not catch me in a boat, unless maybe to stop the plug-hole with my timber toe. Oh! he’s a wonderful old man!” Flask made nothing of it: Ahab had a knee and good part of the other; if off at the hip, that were disabling. “I don’t know that, my little man; I never yet saw him kneel.”

Among whale-wise people it has often been argued whether, considering the paramount importance of his life to the success of the voyage, it is right for a whaling captain to jeopardize that life in the active perils of the chase. So Tamerlane’s soldiers argued whether that invaluable life should be carried into the thickest of the fight. But with Ahab, the question assumed a modified aspect: in a calling where every moment comprises a peril, was it wise for any maimed man to enter a whale-boat? In general, the joint-owners of the Pequod must have plainly thought not. Ahab well knew his friends at home might think little of his lowering in comparatively harmless vicissitudes of the chase, to be near and give orders; but to have a boat apportioned to him as regular headsman, and—above all—to be supplied with five extra men for that same boat—no such conceits had the owners. He therefore solicited no crew, hinted nothing. Nevertheless he took private measures. After the whaleboats were fitted, men had spied him bestirring himself—making thole-pins with his own hands for what all thought a spare boat; shaping small wooden skewers to pin the bow-groove over the running line; ordering an extra sheathing to the bottom—as if to better bear the pointed pressure of his ivory limb; and, most of all, exactly shaping the thigh-board—the clumsy cleat to brace the knee. Oft he stood there, knee fixed in the semicircle, gouging and straightening with the chisel. Folks deemed all this but heedfulness for the ultimate chase of Moby Dick, whom he had vowed to hunt in person; no one dreamed a separate boat’s crew was assigned to that boat.

With the subordinate phantoms—those yellow men—what wonder remained soon waned; for in a whaler wonders soon wane. Besides, now and then such unaccountable odds and ends of strange nations come up from the unknown nooks and ash-holes of the earth to man these floating outlaws of whalers; and the ships themselves often pick up such queer castaway creatures found tossing about the open sea on planks, bits of wreck, oars, whaleboats, canoes, blown-off Japanese junks, and what not; that Beelzebub himself might climb up the side and step down into the cabin to chat with the captain, and it would not create any unsubduable excitement in the forecastle.

But the hair-turbaned Fedallah remained a muffled mystery to the last. Whence he came; by what unaccountable tie he was linked to Ahab’s fortunes; nay, what half-hinted influence—Heaven knows, it might have been authority—he held over him; none knew. But one cannot sustain an indifferent air concerning Fedallah. He was such a creature as civilized, domestic people in the temperate zone only see in their dreams, and that but dimly; but the like of whom now and then glide among the unchanging Asiatic communities, especially the Oriental isles to the east of the continent—those insulated, immemorial, unalterable countries, which even in these modern days still preserve much of the ghostly aboriginalness of earth’s primal generations, when the memory of the first man was a distinct recollection, and all men his descendants, unknowing whence he came, eyed each other as real phantoms, and asked of the sun and the moon why they were created and to what end; when though, according to Genesis, the angels indeed consorted with the daughters of men, the devils also, add the uncanonical Rabbins, indulged in mundane amours.

Under easy sail we swept four cruising-grounds—the Azores; the Cape de Verdes; the Plate off the Rio de la Plata; and the Carrol Ground southerly from St. Helena—when, one serene and moonlight night, when all the waves rolled by like scrolls of silver; and, by their soft, suffusing seethings, made what seemed a silvery silence, not a solitude; on such a silent night a silvery jet was seen far in advance of the white bubbles at the bow. Lit by the moon, it looked celestial; seemed some plumed and glittering god uprising from the sea. Fedallah first saw it; for on such nights he stood mast-head look-out with day’s precision—his turban and the moon, companions in one sky. Silent for many nights, he at last let fall that unearthly cry—“There she blows!” Had the trump of judgment blown, they could not have quivered more; yet still they felt no terror; rather pleasure. Every man started as if a winged spirit hailed us; though unwonted the hour, so delirious was the cry that almost every soul desired a lowering.

Ahab, pacing with side-lunging strides, commanded t’gallants, royals, stunsails—every rag—to be set. With mast-heads manned, the piled craft rolled before the wind; the upbearing taffrail-breeze made the hovering deck feel like air, as though two powers warred in her—one to mount to heaven, one to drive yawingly on. Had you watched Ahab’s face, you had seen two things at strife there too; his live leg made lively echoes; his dead limb struck coffin-taps. On life and death this old man walked. But though we shot swiftly, the silvery jet was no more seen that night. Each swore he saw it once, but never twice.

Some days later, again at the same silent hour, again it was announced, again it vanished; so night after night it served us—mysteriously jetted into moon or starlight, disappearing for a day or three, and each time still further in our van—ever alluring us on. With the superstition of their race, and the Pequod’s preternaturalness, some swore that wherever seen—that unnearable spout was cast by one self-same whale; and that whale, Moby Dick. For a time a peculiar dread reigned, as if treacherously beckoning us on that he might turn and rend us in remotest savage seas.

The blue blandness of the weather sharpened this awe; for days we voyaged through seas so lonesomely mild that all space, in repugnance to our vengeful errand, seemed vacating itself of life before our urn-like prow. But turning eastward, the Cape winds howled; the ivory-tusked Pequod bowed to the blast and gored the dark waves till, like showers of silver chips, the foam flew over the bulwarks; the vacuity of life fled, but gave place to sights more dismal. Strange forms darted at our bows; sea-ravens thickened our wake and, spite of our hootings, perched on the stays as if we were some drifting, uninhabited craft. And heaved and heaved, still unrestingly heaved the black sea, as if its vast tides were a conscience; and the great mundane soul were in anguish and remorse for the long sin and suffering it had bred.

Cape of Good Hope, do they call ye? Rather Cape Tormentoso, as of yore; for long allured by the perfidious silences that before had attended us, we found ourselves launched into this tormented sea, where guilty beings transformed into those fowls and these fish, seemed condemned to swim on everlastingly without any haven in store, or beat that black air without any horizon. But calm, snow-white, and unvarying, the solitary jet at times would be descried—its feathered fountain to the sky; still beckoning.

In such blackness Ahab took almost continual command of the drenched and dangerous deck; gloomily reserved; seldom addressing his mates. When all is secured aloft, captains and crews become practical fatalists; and there, with ivory leg in its hole and one hand on a shroud, for hours he gazed dead to windward, sleet half congealing his eyelashes. The crew, driven from the forecastle by bursting seas, swung wordless in bowlines along the waist; by night the same muteness; wordless Ahab stood up to the blast. Once Starbuck, going below, saw him in his floor-screwed chair, hat and coat still dripping; a chart of tides and currents unrolled; his lantern swinging from his clenched hand; his head thrown back, closed eyes pointed towards the tell-tale’s needle. Terrible old man! thought Starbuck with a shudder; sleeping in this gale, still thou steadfastly eyest thy purpose.

South-east of the Cape, off the Crozetts, a sail loomed—the Goney, or Albatross. From the fore-mast-head, I beheld a whaler long absent from home—as if the waves had been fullers, this craft was bleached like the skeleton of a stranded walrus; her sides channeled with rust; spars and rigging furred like hoar-frost; only her lower sails set; long-bearded look-outs, bepatched by four years’ cruising, swayed in iron hoops, so near we might almost have leaped from one mast-head to the other; yet, those forlorn-looking fishermen, mildly eyeing us as they passed, said not one word to our own look-outs.

“Ship ahoy! Have ye seen the White Whale?” hailed our quarter-deck. But as the strange captain put his trumpet to his mouth, it fell into the sea; the wind rising, he strove in vain to make himself heard. Our men, in silent ways, marked the omen at the first mention of the name to another ship. Ahab paused; he had almost lowered to board her, but the threatening wind forbade. Taking the windward, he seized his trumpet and, knowing her for a Nantucketer bound home, roared: “Ahoy there! This is the Pequod, bound round the world! Tell them to address all future letters to the Pacific ocean! and this time three years, if I am not at home, tell them to address them to ——”

The wakes crossed; instantly shoals of small harmless fish, that for days had swum by our side, darted with shuddering fins and ranged themselves by the stranger. Yet, to any monomaniac man, the veriest trifles capriciously carry meanings. “Swim away from me, do ye?” murmured Ahab, gazing down. There seemed but little in the words, but the tone conveyed more of deep helpless sadness than the insane old man had ever before evinced. Then, in his old lion voice, turning to the steersman, he cried: “Up helm! Keep her off round the world!”

Round the world! There is much in that sound to inspire proud feelings; but whereto does all that circumnavigation conduct? Only through numberless perils to the very point whence we started, where those that we left behind secure, were all the time before us. Were this world an endless plain, and by sailing eastward we could for ever reach new distances, and discover sights more sweet and strange than any Cyclades or Islands of King Solomon, then there were promise; but chasing far mysteries or that demon phantom that swims before all human hearts, over this round globe they either lead us in barren mazes or midway leave us whelmed.

The wind and sea then betokened storms—Ahab’s ostensible reason for not boarding the Goney; but, as subsequent conduct showed, had he, by hailing, got a negative to his one absorbing question, he would scarce have cared to consort five minutes with any stranger captain. All this were ill estimated without something said of the peculiar usages of whalers meeting in foreign seas, especially on a common cruising-ground.

If two strangers crossing the Pine Barrens or Salisbury Plain cannot avoid a salute and bit of news, how much more natural, upon the illimitable Barrens and Plains of the sea, that two whalers descrying each other off lone Fanning’s or far King’s Mills should come into close sociable contact—especially if owned in one seaport, with letters and papers to swap, and the latest whaling intelligence to exchange. Nor would difference of country much alter this; though English whalers, being few, meet seldom; and when they do, there’s apt to be a shyness—your Englishman reserved; your Yankee, he allows that sort of thing to none but himself. The English sometimes affect a metropolitan superiority over the long lean Nantucketer—sea-peasant, they think; wherein that superiority lies, hard saying, seeing the Yankees in one day kill more whales than all the English in ten years; but this foible the islander little minds—knowing he hath a few of his own.

So of all ships separately sailing the sea, whalers have most reason to be sociable—and they are so. Merchantmen oft pass like a brace of dandies in Broadway, mutually cutting each other on the high seas; and all the time indulging, perhaps, in finical criticism upon each other’s rig. Men-of-War duck ensigns in such a string of silly bowings and scrapings there’s little hearty good-will. Slave-ships hurry past; Pirates, meeting, hail “How many skulls?”—as whalers hail “How many barrels?”—and steer apart, not loving to behold each other’s villanous likeness.

But look at the godly, honest, unostentatious, hospitable, sociable, free-and-easy whaler! What does the whaler do when she meets another in any decent weather? She has a “Gam,” a thing so unknown to other ships they never heard the name; and if they did, they grin about “spouters” and “blubber-boilers.” Why it is that all Merchant-seamen, and also all Pirates and Man-of-War’s men, and Slave-ship sailors, cherish such a scornful feeling towards Whale-ships, is hard to answer. Because, in the case of pirates, say, I should like to know whether that profession of theirs has any peculiar glory about it. It sometimes ends in uncommon elevation, indeed; but only at the gallows. Hence, I conclude, that in boasting himself to be high lifted above a whaleman, in that assertion the pirate has no solid basis to stand on. But what is a Gam? You might wear out your index-finger running up and down the columns of dictionaries, and never find the word. Dr. Johnson never attained to that erudition; Noah Webster’s ark does not hold it. Nevertheless, this same expressive word has now for many years been in constant use among some fifteen thousand true born Yankees. Certainly, it needs a definition, and should be incorporated into the Lexicon. With that view, let me learnedly define it.

GAM. NOUN—_A social meeting of two_ (_or more_) _Whaleships, generally on a cruising-ground; when, after exchanging hails, they exchange visits by boats’ crews: the two captains remaining, for the time, on board of one ship, and the two chief mates on the other._

There is another item about Gamming. In a pirate, Man-of-War, or slave-ship, the captain, rowed in his boat, sits in cushioned stern-sheets and often steers with a pretty milliner’s tiller. The whale-boat has no such sofa, no tiller at all. High times indeed, if whaling captains were wheeled about the water on castors like gouty aldermen. As a complete crew must leave the ship, and the boat-steerer is of the number, that subordinate is steersman; the captain, having no place to sit, is pulled off all standing like a pine tree—conscious of all eyes levelled on him from the two ships’ sides; alive to the importance of sustaining his dignity by maintaining his legs. No easy matter: behind him the immense projecting steering-oar hits him in the small of the back; the after-oar raps his knees in front; he is wedged before and behind, and can only expand sideways by settling on his stretched legs; a sudden pitch will near topple him, for length of foundation is naught without breadth. And it would never do, in the world’s riveted gaze, to be seen steadying himself by catching hold with his hands; token of entire buoyant self-command, he carries them in his trowsers’ pockets—perhaps, being very large, heavy hands, for ballast. Nevertheless, there are instances, well authenticated, when, in an uncommonly critical squall, the straddling captain has seized the nearest oarsman’s hair, and held on there like grim death.

The Town-Ho’s Story.

(As told at the Golden Inn.)

The Cape of Good Hope, and all the watery region round about there, is like a noted four corners of a great highway, where you meet more travellers than in any other part. Not long after speaking the Goney, we encountered another homeward-bound whaleman, the Town-Ho, manned almost wholly by Polynesians. In the short gam that ensued she gave us strong news of Moby Dick. To some the general interest in the White Whale was wildly heightened by a circumstance of the Town-Ho’s story, which seemed obscurely to involve with the whale a wondrous, inverted visitation of one of those so called judgments of God. This secret part, with its particular accompaniments, never reached Ahab or his mates; the captain of the Town-Ho himself knew it not. It was the private property of three confederate white seamen of that ship; one of whom, it seems, communicated it to Tashtego with Romish injunctions of secrecy; but the following night Tashtego rambled in his sleep, and revealed so much that, when he was wakened, he could not well withhold the rest. Such the strange delicacy of those Pequod seamen who came to the full knowledge of it, that it never transpired abaft the main-mast. Interweaving, in its proper place, this darker thread with the story publicly narrated on the ship, I now proceed to put the whole on lasting record.

For my humor’s sake, I shall preserve the style in which I once narrated it at Lima, to a lounging circle of my Spanish friends, one saint’s eve, smoking upon the thick-gilt tiled piazza of the Golden Inn. Of those fine cavaliers, the young Dons, Pedro and Sebastian, were on the closer terms with me; hence the interluding questions they occasionally put, and which are duly answered at the time.

“Some two years prior to my first learning the events which I am about rehearsing to you, gentlemen, the Town-Ho, Sperm Whaler of Nantucket, was cruising in your Pacific here, not very many days’ sail eastward from the eaves of this good Golden Inn—somewhere to the northward of the Line. One morning, upon handling the pumps, according to daily usage, it was observed that she made more water in her hold than common. They supposed a sword-fish had stabbed her. But the captain, having some unusual reason for believing that rare good luck awaited him in those latitudes; and therefore being very averse to quit them; and the leak not being then considered at all dangerous, though, indeed, they could not find it; the ship still continued her cruisings, the mariners working at the pumps at wide and easy intervals; but no good luck came; the leak sensibly increased; so, taking alarm, the captain made all sail for the nearest harbor among the islands, to heave out and repair.

“Though no small passage lay before her, yet, if the commonest chance favored, he feared not foundering; his pumps were of the best, and six-and-thirty men could keep her free. In truth, she had all but certainly arrived in safety at her port, had it not been for the brutal overbearing of Radney, the mate, a Vineyarder, and the bitterly provoked vengeance of Steelkilt, a Lakeman and desperado from Buffalo.”

“Lakeman!—Buffalo! Pray, what is a Lakeman, and where is Buffalo?” said Don Sebastian.

“On the eastern shore of our Lake Erie, Don; but—I crave your courtesy—may be, you shall soon hear further of all that. Now, gentlemen, in square-sail brigs and three-masted ships, well-nigh as large and stout as any that ever sailed out of your old Callao to far Manilla; this Lakeman, in the land-locked heart of our America, had yet been nurtured by all those agrarian freebooting impressions popularly connected with the open ocean. For in their interflowing aggregate, those grand fresh-water seas of ours,—Erie, and Ontario, and Huron, and Superior, and Michigan,—possess an ocean-like expansiveness, with many of the ocean’s noblest traits; with many of its rimmed varieties of races and of climes. They contain round archipelagoes of romantic isles, even as the Polynesian waters do; in large part, are shored by two great contrasting nations, as the Atlantic is; they furnish long maritime approaches to our numerous territorial colonies from the East, dotted all round their banks; here and there are frowned upon by batteries, and by the goat-like craggy guns of lofty Mackinaw; they have heard the fleet thunderings of naval victories; at intervals, they yield their beaches to wild barbarians, whose red painted faces flash from out their peltry wigwams; for leagues and leagues are flanked by ancient and unentered forests, where the gaunt pines stand like serried lines of kings in Gothic genealogies; those same woods harboring wild Afric beasts of prey, and silken creatures whose exported furs give robes to Tartar Emperors; they mirror the paved capitals of Buffalo and Cleveland, as well as Winnebago villages; they float alike the full-rigged merchant ship, the armed cruiser of the State, the steamer, and the beech canoe; they are swept by Borean and dismasting blasts as direful as any that lash the salted wave; they know what shipwrecks are, for out of sight of land, however inland, they have drowned full many a midnight ship with all its shrieking crew. Thus, gentlemen, though an inlander, Steelkilt was wild-ocean born, and wild-ocean nurtured; as much of an audacious mariner as any. And for Radney, though in his infancy he may have laid him down on the lone Nantucket beach, to nurse at his maternal sea; though in after life he had long followed our austere Atlantic and your contemplative Pacific; yet was he quite as vengeful and full of social quarrel as the backwoods seaman, fresh from the latitudes of buck-horn handled Bowie-knives. Yet was this Nantucketer a man with some good-hearted traits; and this Lakeman, a mariner, who though a sort of devil indeed, might yet by inflexible firmness, only tempered by that common decency of human recognition which is the meanest slave’s right; thus treated, this Steelkilt had long been retained harmless and docile. At all events, he had proved so thus far; but Radney was doomed and made mad, and Steelkilt—but, gentlemen, you shall hear.

“Canallers!” cried Don Pedro, when, anon, I named the two comrades Steelkilt presently shook from aloft. “We have seen many whale-ships, but never heard of your Canallers. Pardon: who and what are they?”

“Canallers, Don, are the boatmen belonging to our grand Erie Canal. You must have heard of it.”

“Nay, Senor; hereabouts in this dull, warm, most lazy, and hereditary land, we know but little of your vigorous North.”

“Aye? Well then, Don, refill my cup. Your chicha’s very fine; and ere proceeding further I will tell ye what our Canallers are; for such information may throw side-light upon my story.

“For three hundred and sixty miles, gentlemen, through the entire breadth of the state of New York; through numerous populous cities and most thriving villages; through long, dismal, uninhabited swamps, and affluent, cultivated fields, unrivalled for fertility; by billiard-room and bar-room; through the holy-of-holies of great forests; on Roman arches over Indian rivers; through sun and shade; by happy hearts or broken; through all the wide contrasting scenery of those noble Mohawk counties; and especially, by rows of snow-white chapels, whose spires stand almost like milestones, flows one continual stream of Venetianly corrupt and often lawless life. There’s your true Ashantee, gentlemen; there howl your pagans; where you ever find them, next door to you; under the long-flung shadow, and the snug patronising lee of churches. For by some curious fatality, as it is often noted of your metropolitan freebooters that they ever encamp around the halls of justice, so sinners, gentlemen, most abound in holiest vicinities.”

“I see! I see!” impetuously exclaimed Don Pedro, spilling his chicha upon his silvery ruffles. “No need to travel! The world’s one Lima. I had thought, now, that at your temperate North the generations were cold and holy as the hills.—But the story.”

It was when the leak gained so as to require an hour or more at the pumps every day, that Radney betrayed solicitude; being a part owner, said some. That evening, as they wrought at the brakes, the clear water from the pumps rippled like a mountain spring across the deck.

“Now, as you well know, it is not seldom the case in this conventional world of ours—watery or otherwise; that when a person placed in command over his fellow-men finds one of them to be very significantly his superior in general pride of manhood, straightway against that man he conceives an unconquerable dislike and bitterness; and if he have a chance he will pull down and pulverize that subaltern’s tower, and make a little heap of dust of it. Be this conceit of mine as it may, gentlemen, at all events Steelkilt was a tall and noble animal with a head like a Roman, and a flowing golden beard like the tasseled housings of your last viceroy’s snorting charger; and a brain, and a heart, and a soul in him, gentlemen, which had made Steelkilt Charlemagne, had he been born son to Charlemagne’s father. But Radney, the mate, was ugly as a mule; yet as hardy, as stubborn, as malicious. He did not love Steelkilt, and Steelkilt knew it.

“Espying the mate drawing near as he was toiling at the pump with the rest, the Lakeman affected not to notice him, but unawed, went on with his gay banterings.

“‘Aye, aye, my merry lads, it’s a lively leak this; hold a cannikin, one of ye, and let’s have a taste. By the Lord, it’s worth bottling! I tell ye what, men, old Rad’s investment must go for it! he had best cut away his part of the hull and tow it home. The fact is, boys, that sword-fish only began the job; he’s come back again with a gang of ship-carpenters, saw-fish, and file-fish, and what not; and the whole posse of ’em are now hard at work cutting and slashing at the bottom; making improvements, I suppose. If old Rad were here now, I’d tell him to jump overboard and scatter ’em. They’re playing the devil with his estate, I can tell him. But he’s a simple old soul,—Rad, and a beauty too. Boys, they say the rest of his property is invested in looking-glasses. I wonder if he’d give a poor devil like me the model of his nose.’”

“‘Damn your eyes! what’s that pump stopping for?’ roared Radney, pretending not to hear. ‘Thunder away at it!’”

“‘Aye, aye, sir,’ said Steelkilt, merry as a cricket. ‘Lively, boys, lively, now!’ And with that the pump clanged like fifty fire-engines; the men tossed their hats off to it, and ere long that peculiar gasping of the lungs was heard which denotes the fullest tension of life’s utmost energies.

“Quitting the pump at last, Steelkilt went forward, panting, and sat on the windlass, his face fiery, his eyes bloodshot. Now what cozening fiend it was, gentlemen, that possessed Radney to meddle with such a man in that corporeally exasperated state, I know not; but so it happened. Then, intolerably striding along the deck, the mate commanded him to get a broom and sweep down, and also a shovel to remove certain piggish offensiveness.

“Sweeping at sea is boy’s work; and Steelkilt, captain of a pump-gang, was by usage exempt. He answered in his ordinary tone, broken only by exhaustion, that sweeping was not his business, and he would not do it; pointing to three lads, the customary sweepers. With an oath, Radney reiterated his command, advancing with an uplifted cooper’s club-hammer.

“But as he sat still for a moment, and as he steadfastly looked into the mate’s malignant eye and perceived the stacks of powder-casks heaped up in him and the slow-match silently burning along towards them; as he instinctively saw all this, that strange forbearance most felt by really valiant men—this nameless phantom feeling, gentlemen—stole over Steelkilt.

“For all his ire, Steelkilt’s strange forbearance held him rooted. The hammer shook within an inch of his face.

“Steelkilt rose, slowly retreating round the windlass, followed by the mate. ‘Mr. Radney, I will not obey you. Take that hammer away, or look to yourself.’

“The predestinated man came closer; Steelkilt, stabbing him with his eye, hissed that if the hammer but grazed his cheek he would murder him. Immediately the hammer touched the cheek; the next instant the lower jaw of the mate was stove in his head; he fell on the hatch spouting blood like a whale.

“Ere the cry could go aft, Steelkilt was shaking a backstay to where two of his comrades stood their mast-heads—both Canallers. Down they slid like baleful comets. The three junior mates and four harpooneers crowded the Lakeman to the deck; but Steelkilt and his desperadoes broke through, gained the forecastle, hastily slewed three or four large casks in line with the windlass, and entrenched themselves behind the barricade.

“‘Come out of that, ye pirates!’ roared the captain, menacing with a pistol in each hand. Steelkilt leaped on the casks and strode, defying the worst, but declared distinctly that his death would be the signal for a murderous mutiny. Fearing lest this might be true, the captain desisted a little, yet commanded them to turn to.

“‘Will you promise not to touch us, if we do?’ demanded Steelkilt.

“‘Turn to!—I make no promise. Do you want to sink the ship? Turn to!’

“‘Sink the ship?’ cried Steelkilt. ‘Aye, let her sink. Not a man of us turns to, unless you swear not to raise a rope-yarn against us.’ ‘What say ye, men?’ A fierce cheer was their response.

“The Lakeman now patrolled the barricade, all the while keeping his eye on the Captain, and jerking out such sentences as these:—‘It’s not our fault; we didn’t want it; I told him to take his hammer away; it was boy’s business; he might have known me before this; I told him not to prick the buffalo; I believe I have broken a finger here against his cursed jaw; ain’t those mincing knives down in the forecastle there, men? look to those handspikes, my hearties. Captain, by God, look to yourself; say the word; don’t be a fool; forget it all; we are ready to turn to; treat us decently, and we’re your men; but we won’t be flogged.’”

“‘Turn to! I make no promises, turn to, I say!’

“‘Look ye, now,’ cried the Lakeman, flinging out his arm towards him, ‘there are a few of us here (and I am one of them) who have shipped for the cruise, d’ye see; now as you well know, sir, we can claim our discharge as soon as the anchor is down; so we don’t want a row; it’s not our interest; we want to be peaceable; we are ready to work, but we won’t be flogged.’

“‘Turn to!’ roared the Captain.

“Steelkilt glanced round him a moment, and then said:—‘I tell you what it is now, Captain, rather than kill ye, and be hung for such a shabby rascal, we won’t lift a hand against ye unless ye attack us; but till you say the word about not flogging us, we don’t do a hand’s turn.’

“‘Down into the forecastle then, down with ye, I’ll keep ye there till ye’re sick of it. Down ye go.’

“‘Shall we?’ cried the ringleader to his men. Most of them were against it; but at length, in obedience to Steelkilt, they preceded him down into their dark den, growlingly disappearing, like bears into a cave.

“The captain leaped the barricade; the slide of the scuttle was drawn; the heavy brass padlock clicked upon ten in number, leaving on deck some twenty neutral.

“All night a wide-awake watch was kept; the clanking pumps dismally resounded. Twice a day for three days the key turned; water and a couple handfuls of biscuit were lowered; yells refused the summons. On the fourth morning four burst up ready to turn to; on the fifth, three others. Only three were left.

“Enraged by the defection of seven, maddened by long entombment, Steelkilt proposed to the two Canallers to burst out at the next call, armed with the keen crescentic mincing knives, and run amuck from bowsprit to taffrail; if possible, seize the ship. They swore to any mad thing but surrender; each insisted on being first up the ladder; the leader reserved that priority. Then the foul play came out: each, in his soul, had lit on the same treachery—to be foremost in breaking out, in order to be first to surrender, and so purchase pardon. They bound the sleeping Lakeman with cords; gagged him; shrieked for the captain; and, shoving him up bound, claimed the honor of securing one ripe for murder. Collared, they were dragged like dead cattle; seized up into the mizzen rigging side by side, like quarters of meat, and there they hung till morning.

“At sunrise, the captain summoned all hands; he would flog the rebels round, he said, but, considering their timely surrender, let them go with a reprimand. ‘But as for you, ye carrion rogues,’ turning to the three men in the rigging—‘for you, I mean to mince ye up for the try-pots;’ and, seizing a rope, he applied it with all his might to the backs of the two traitors, till they lifelessly hung their heads sideways, as the crucified thieves are drawn. ‘There is still rope enough left for you, my fine bantam,’ he cried to Steelkilt. ‘Take that gag from his mouth.’

“The exhausted mutineer painfully twisted his head: ‘What I say is this—and mind it well—if you flog me, I murder you!’

“‘Say ye so? then see how ye frighten me,’ said the captain, drawing off.

“‘Best not,’ hissed the Lakeman.

“‘But I must’—and the rope was raised—when Steelkilt hissed something inaudible to all but the captain. To the amazement of all, the captain started back, paced, threw down his rope, and said, ‘I won’t do it—let him go—cut him down!’

“But a pale man with a bandaged head arrested the mates—Radney, the chief mate. Mumbling that he was willing and able to do what the captain dared not, he snatched the rope and advanced.

“‘You are a coward!’ hissed the Lakeman.

“‘So I am, but take that.’ He made good his word, spite of the threat—whatever it had been. The three were cut down; all hands turned to; and sullenly the iron pumps clanged as before.

“After dark, the two trembling traitors besieged the cabin door, durst not consort with the crew, and were put down in the run for salvation. No further mutiny appeared. Mainly at Steelkilt’s instigation, the men resolved to obey all orders, and, when the ship reached port, desert in a body; and to end the voyage speedily, they agreed not to sing out for whales. The Town-Ho still maintained her mast-heads; the captain would lower that moment as on the first day; and Radney, with his bandaged mouth, would change berth for boat.

“Meanwhile, Steelkilt kept his own counsel concerning private revenge. Radney had an unseamanlike way of sitting on the bulwarks at night, dozing between the boat and ship with the sea below. Steelkilt plaited, at his leisure, a close netting for an iron ball. ‘What are you making there?’ said a shipmate. ‘Like a lanyard for your bag.’ ‘Yes, rather oddish; but it will answer.’ Lacking twine, he went to Radney: he asked quietly for some to mend his hammock; it was given; and twine and lanyard were seen no more. In the fore-ordaining soul of Steelkilt the mate lay already stark and stretched, his forehead crushed in.

“But a fool saved the would-be murderer. Just between daybreak and sunrise of the second day, when washing down decks, a stupid Teneriffe man, drawing water in the main-chains, shouted all at once, ‘There she rolls! there she rolls!’ Jesu, what a whale! It was Moby Dick.

“‘Moby Dick!’ cried Don Sebastian. ‘St. Dominic! Sir sailor, do whales have christenings? Whom call you Moby Dick?’

“‘A very white, and famous, and most deadly immortal monster, Don;—but that were too long now.’

“All was a phrensy. ‘The White Whale!—the White Whale!’ was the cry. The dogged crew eyed askance the vast milky mass that, lit by a horizontal sun, shifted and glistened like a living opal in the blue morning sea. A strange fatality pervades these events. The mutineer was bowsman to the mate; when fast, it was his duty to sit next him and haul line at command. The mate’s boat got the start; none howled more fiercely than Steelkilt as he strained at his oar. Their harpooneer got fast; spear in hand, Radney sprang to the bow—ever a furious man in a boat—crying, through bandage, to beach him on the whale’s topmost back. His bowsman hauled him up and up through blinding foam that blent two whitenesses together, till suddenly the boat struck like against a sunken ledge, and, keeling, spilled out the standing mate. The boat righted and was dashed aside by the swell; Radney was tossed into the sea on the other flank. For an instant, dim through spray, he strove to remove himself from the eye of Moby Dick. But the whale rushed round in a sudden maelstrom; seized the swimmer between his jaws; rearing high, plunged headlong, and went down.

“At the first tap of the boat’s bottom, the Lakeman had slackened the line to drop astern from the whirlpool; calmly looking on, he thought his own thoughts. A sudden, terrific, downward jerk quickly brought his knife to the line: he cut it; the whale was free. At some distance Moby Dick rose again, with tatters of Radney’s red woollen shirt caught in the teeth that had destroyed him. All four boats gave chase; the whale eluded them and wholly disappeared.

“In good time the Town-Ho reached her port—a savage, solitary place. Headed by the Lakeman, all but five or six of the foremastmen deserted among the palms, eventually seizing a large double war-canoe and setting sail for some other harbor. The ship’s company being reduced to a handful, the captain called upon Islanders to assist in heaving down to stop the leak; but ceaseless vigilance over their dangerous allies and extreme hard work so weakened the whites that, the vessel ready again, he durst not put off in so heavy a craft. He anchored as far off shore as possible; ran out two cannon; warned the Islanders not to approach; then, with one man, set sail in his best whale-boat for Tahiti, five hundred miles, to procure men.

“On the fourth day he descried a large canoe; he steered away; but it bore down. The voice of Steelkilt hailed him to heave to, or be run under. The captain presented a pistol. With one foot on each prow of the yoked canoes, the Lakeman laughed him to scorn; if the pistol so much as clicked, he would bury him in bubbles and foam.

“‘What do you want of me?’ cried the captain.

“‘Where are you bound? and for what are you bound?’ demanded Steelkilt; ‘no lies.’

“‘I am bound to Tahiti for more men.’

“‘Very good. Let me board you a moment—I come in peace.’ He swam to the boat; climbed the gunwale; stood face to face. ‘Cross your arms, sir; throw back your head. Now, repeat after me: As soon as Steelkilt leaves me, I swear to beach this boat on yonder island, and remain there six days. If I do not, may lightnings strike me!’

“‘A pretty scholar,’ laughed the Lakeman. ‘Adios, Senor!’ He swam back; watched the boat fairly beached and drawn to the cocoa-nut roots; then made sail again, and in due time arrived at Tahiti, his own place of destination. There luck befriended him: two ships about to sail for France wanted precisely his number; they embarked, and so for ever got the start of their former captain. Some ten days after the French sailed, the whale-boat arrived; the captain enlisted certain more civilized Tahitians; chartered a small native schooner; returned; found all right; and again resumed his cruisings.

“Where Steelkilt now is, none know; but upon Nantucket, the widow of Radney still turns to the sea which refuses to give up its dead; still in dreams sees the awful white whale that destroyed him.

“‘Are you through?’ said Don Sebastian, quietly.

“‘I am, Don.’

“‘Then I entreat you, tell me if to the best of your own convictions, this your story is in substance really true? It is so passing wonderful! Did you get it from an unquestionable source?’

“‘Is there a copy of the Holy Evangelists in the Golden Inn, gentlemen?’

“‘Nay,’ said Don Sebastian; ‘but I know a worthy priest near by, who will quickly procure one for me. I go for it; but are you well advised? this may grow too serious.’

“‘Will you be so good as to bring the priest also, Don?’

“‘Though there are no Auto-da-Fés in Lima now,’ said one of the company to another; ‘I fear our sailor friend runs risk of the archiepiscopacy. Let us withdraw more out of the moonlight. I see no need of this.’

“‘Excuse me for running after you, Don Sebastian; but may I also beg that you will be particular in procuring the largest sized Evangelists you can.’

“‘This is the priest; he brings you the Evangelists,’ said Don Sebastian, gravely, returning with a tall and solemn figure.

“‘Let me remove my hat. Now, venerable priest, further into the light, and hold the Holy Book before me that I may touch it.

“‘So help me Heaven, and on my honor the story I have told ye, gentlemen, is in substance and its great items, true. I know it to be true; it happened on this ball; I trod the ship; I knew the crew; I have seen and talked with Steelkilt since the death of Radney.’”

Ere long I shall paint to you, as well as one may without canvas, something like the true form of the whale when his absolute body is moored alongside, and you may fairly step upon him. But first, let us advert to those curious imaginary portraits which even to-day confidently challenge the landsman’s faith. It is time to set the world right by proving such pictures all wrong.

From the primal Hindoo, Egyptian, and Grecian sculptures, down to the Christian painters, license has prevailed. In the famous cavern-pagoda of Elephanta, the Matse Avatar—half man, half whale—gives a tail more like an anaconda’s taper than the broad palms of the true flukes. Guido’s Perseus and Hogarth’s “Perseus Descending” do no better: a howdah on its back, and a tusked mouth taken for Traitors’ Gate. The book-binder’s dolphin-whale, winding like a vine-stalk round an anchor—picturesque but fabulous—lingers from old devices.

Go to old voyagers and “scientific” delineators. In Harris’s plates from a Dutch Spitzbergen voyage (1671), white bears run over raft-like whales; elsewhere, the prodigious blunder of perpendicular flukes. Captain Colnett’s “Physeter,” “drawn by scale” from one hoisted on deck, has an eye which, by its own scale applied to a full-grown sperm whale, would make that eye a bow-window five feet long—ah, my gallant captain, why did ye not give us Jonah looking out of it! Nor are the most conscientious compilations for the young and tender free from the same heinousness. Even Goldsmith’s Animated Nature (1807) gives a whale much like an amputated sow, and a “narwhale” that is a hippogriff palmed for genuine upon schoolboys. Lacépède errs; even Scoresby denies his Mysticetus any counterpart in nature. But the cap-sheaf was reserved for Frederick Cuvier (1836), who gives what he calls a picture of the Sperm Whale—not a Sperm Whale, but a squash; perhaps, like Desmarest, from a Chinese drawing—and what lively lads with the pencil those Chinese are, many queer cups and saucers inform us.

As for the sign-painters’ whales seen in the streets hanging over oil-shops, what shall be said of them? They are generally Richard III. whales, with dromedary humps, and very savage; breakfasting on three or four sailor tarts, that is whaleboats full of mariners: their deformities floundering in seas of blood and blue paint. Yet are these manifold mistakes not so surprising. Most drawings are from the stranded fish: as a wrecked ship with broken back ill-images her proud hull at sea, so the stranded whale falsifies his living swells and undulations. Though elephants have stood for their full-lengths, the living Leviathan has never yet fairly floated himself for his portrait; afloat the vast bulk of him is out of sight, like a launched line-of-battle ship; and out of that element it is a thing eternally impossible for mortal man to hoist him bodily into the air, so as to preserve all his mighty swells and undulations. And, not to speak of the highly presumable difference of contour between a young sucking whale and a full-grown Platonian Leviathan; yet, even in the case of one of those young sucking whales hoisted to a ship’s deck, such is then the outlandish, eel-like, limbered, varying shape of him, that his precise expression the devil himself could not catch.

Nor does the skeleton yield his contour. Though Jeremy Bentham’s skeleton, hung for candelabra in an executor’s library, correctly conveys the idea of a burly-browed utilitarian old gentleman; yet nothing of this kind could be inferred from any leviathan’s articulated bones. As the great Hunter says, the skeleton bears the same relation to the fully invested animal as the insect to the chrysalis that roundly envelopes it. Mark, too, the side fin: its bones almost exactly answer to the human hand, minus only the thumb—four fingers—yet all forever mittened in flesh. “However recklessly the whale may sometimes serve us,” said humorous Stubb, “he can never be truly said to handle us without mittens.”

So conclude: the great Leviathan must remain unpainted to the last. One portrait may hit nearer than another; none can hit with any considerable exactness. The only tolerable idea of his living contour is by going a-whaling yourself; but in so doing, you run no small risk of being eternally stove and sunk. Wherefore, be not too fastidious in your curiosity touching this Leviathan.

Waiving monstrous stories, take the outlines. Of the Sperm Whale, Colnett’s and Frederick Cuvier’s are poor; Huggins’s better; by great odds Beale’s are best. His “boats attacking Sperm Whales,” though it may stir parlor scepticism, is admirably correct and life-like in general effect. Some of J. Ross Browne’s sperm drawings are pretty in contour, ill-engraved. Of the Right Whale, Scoresby’s best outlines are drawn too small to convey a desirable impression, and he has but one scene—lamentably few, since only good scenes give any truthful idea of the living whale as seen by his hunters.

By far the finest, though in some details not the most correct, are two large French engravings after Garnery—attacks on Sperm and Right Whale. In the first, a noble Sperm Whale, just risen from the profundities, bears high upon his back the wreck of a stoven boat, its prow balancing upon the spine; in that prow, for one single incomputable flash, stands an oarsman, half shrouded by the incensed boiling spout, leaping as from a precipice. The half-emptied line-tub and spilled harpoon-poles bob; swimmers’ heads scatter in affright, while in the black stormy distance the ship is bearing down upon the scene. In the second, a boat draws alongside a barnacled Right Whale rolling his weedy bulk like a mossy rock-slide from Patagonian cliffs; his jets are erect, full, and black like soot; so that from so abounding a smoke in the chimney, you would think there must be a brave supper cooking in the great bowels below. Sea fowls are pecking at the small crabs, shell-fish, and other sea candies and maccaroni, which the Right Whale sometimes carries on his pestilent back. And all the while the thick-lipped leviathan rushes through the deep, leaving tons of tumultuous white curds in his wake, rocking the slight boat like a skiff nigh the paddle-wheels of an ocean steamer. Thus, the foreground is all raging commotion; but behind, in admirable artistic contrast, is the glassy level of a sea becalmed, the drooping unstarched sails of the powerless ship, and the inert mass of a dead whale, a conquered fortress, with the flag of capture lazily hanging from the whale-pole inserted into his spout-hole.

Who Garnery was I know not; but my life for it he had practical converse with his subject, or else was marvellously tutored. The French are the lads for painting action. Go and gaze upon all the paintings of Europe, and where will you find such a gallery of living and breathing commotion on canvas, as in that triumphal hall at Versailles; where the beholder fights his way, pell-mell, through the consecutive great battles of France; where every sword flashes like the Northern Lights, and armed kings and Emperors dash by like a charge of crowned centaurs? Not wholly unworthy of a place there are these sea battle-pieces. The natural aptitude of the French for seizing the picturesqueness of things seems peculiarly evinced in what paintings and engravings they have of their whaling scenes. With not one tenth of England’s experience in the fishery, nor the thousandth part of that of the Americans, they have nevertheless furnished both nations with the only finished sketches at all capable of conveying the real spirit of the whale hunt. For the most part, the English and American draughtsmen are content with mechanical outlines—the vacant profile of the whale; which, so far as picturesqueness of effect is concerned, is about tantamount to sketching the profile of a pyramid. Even Scoresby, the justly renowned Right whaleman, after giving us a stiff full length of the Greenland whale, and three or four delicate miniatures of narwhales and porpoises, treats us to a series of classical engravings of boat hooks, chopping knives, and grapnels; and with the microscopic diligence of a Leuwenhoeck submits to the inspection of a shivering world ninety-six fac-similes of magnified Arctic snow crystals. I mean no disparagement to the excellent voyager (I honor him as a veteran), but in so important a matter it was certainly an oversight not to have procured for every crystal a sworn affidavit taken before a Greenland Justice of the Peace.

Add H. Durand’s two: a quiet noon among Pacific isles—a French whaler anchored, lazily taking water; the loosened sails and palm leaves droop in the breezeless air; the hardy fishermen under oriental repose. The other—hove-to on the open sea with a Right Whale alongside; cutting-in; a boat pushing off, mast just stepped, harpoons and lances levelled; the little craft rearing like a horse; the smoke of the torments of the boiling whale going up like the smoke over a village of smithies; to windward, a black cloud with earnest of squalls quickens the seamen.

On Tower-hill a kedger holds up a painted board of the tragic scene in which he lost his leg—three whales, three boats; one boat, presumed to contain the missing leg in all its integrity, crunched by jaws. Ten years he has shown that picture and that stump to an incredulous world; his time of justification is come; but never a stump-speech—only ruefully contemplates his own amputation.

Throughout the Pacific and in Nantucket, New Bedford, and Sag Harbor, you will come across lively sketches graven by the fishermen themselves on Sperm Whale-teeth and ladies’ busks of Right Whale-bone—skrimshander. Some of them have little boxes of dentistical-looking implements, specially intended for the skrimshandering business. But, in general, they toil with their jack-knives alone; and, with that almost omnipotent tool of the sailor, they will turn you out anything you please, in the way of a mariner’s fancy.

Long exile from Christendom and civilization inevitably restores a man to that condition in which God placed him, _i.e._ what is called savagery. Your true whale-hunter is as much a savage as an Iroquois. I myself am a savage, owning no allegiance but to the King of the Cannibals; and ready at any moment to rebel against him. Now, one of the peculiar characteristics of the savage in his domestic hours, is his wonderful patience of industry. An ancient Hawaiian war-club or spear-paddle, in its full multiplicity and elaboration of carving, is as great a trophy of human perseverance as a Latin lexicon. For, with but a bit of broken sea-shell or a shark’s tooth, that miraculous intricacy of wooden net-work has been achieved; and it has cost steady years of steady application. As with the Hawaiian savage, so with the white sailor-savage. With the same marvellous patience, and with the same single shark’s tooth of his one poor jack-knife, he will carve you a bit of bone sculpture, not quite as workmanlike, but as close packed in its maziness of design, as the Greek savage, Achilles’s shield; and full of barbaric spirit and suggestiveness, as the prints of that fine old Dutch savage, Albert Durer.

Wooden whales from South Sea war-wood hang in forecastles; brass whales for knockers thump at country doors. When the porter is sleepy, the anvil-headed whale would be best. But these knocking whales are seldom remarkable as faithful essays. On spires of old-fashioned churches sheet-iron whales weather-cock; but they are so elevated, and besides that are to all intents and purposes so labelled with “Hands off!” you cannot examine them closely enough to decide upon their merit.

In bony, ribby regions of earth, at the base of broken cliffs, you will discover Leviathan petrified in grass; in mountainous lands, along amphitheatrical ridges, the profiles of whales; but you must be a thorough whaleman, and note the latitude and longitude of your first stand-point, else such chance-like sights will elude you.

Nor when expandingly lifted by your subject, can you fail to trace out great whales in the starry heavens, with boats pursuing them; as when Eastern nations saw armies locked in battle among the clouds. Thus at the North have I chased Leviathan round and round the Pole with the revolutions of the bright points that first defined him to me; and beneath Antarctic skies I have boarded the Argo-Navis, and joined the chase against starry Cetus.

With a frigate’s anchors for bridle-bitts and fasces of harpoons for spurs, would I could mount that whale and leap the topmost skies!

Steering north-eastward from the Crozetts, we fell in with vast meadows of brit—the minute, yellow substance upon which the Right Whale largely feeds—so that we seemed to be sailing through boundless fields of ripe and golden wheat.

Numbers of Right Whales, secure from a Sperm Whaler like the Pequod, with open jaws sluggishly swam through the brit, which, adhering to the fringing fibres of that wondrous Venetian blind in their mouths, was in that manner separated from the water that escaped at the lip.

As morning mowers, who side by side slowly and seethingly advance their scythes through the long wet grass of marshy meads; even so these monsters swam, making a strange, grassy, cutting sound; and leaving behind them endless swaths of blue upon the yellow sea.

But it was only the sound they made as they parted the brit which at all reminded one of mowers. Seen from the mast-heads, especially when they paused, their vast black forms looked more like lifeless masses of rock; and as in the great hunting countries of India, the stranger at a distance will sometimes pass on the plains recumbent elephants without knowing them to be such, taking them for bare, blackened elevations of the soil; even so, often, with him who first beholds these leviathans. And even when recognised at last, their immense magnitude renders it very hard really to believe that such bulky masses of overgrowth can possibly be instinct, in all parts, with the same sort of life that lives in a dog or a horse.

Indeed, in other respects, you can hardly regard any creatures of the deep with the same feelings that you do those of the shore. For though some old naturalists have maintained that all creatures of the land are of their kind in the sea; and though taking a broad general view of the thing, this may very well be; yet coming to specialties, where, for example, does the ocean furnish any fish that in disposition answers to the sagacious kindness of the dog? The accursed shark alone can in any generic respect be said to bear comparative analogy to him.

But though, to landsmen in general, the native inhabitants of the seas have ever been regarded with emotions unspeakably unsocial and repelling; though we know the sea to be an everlasting terra incognita, so that Columbus sailed over numberless unknown worlds to discover his one superficial western one; though, by vast odds, the most terrific of all mortal disasters have immemorially and indiscriminately befallen tens and hundreds of thousands of those who have gone upon the waters; though but a moment’s consideration will teach, that however baby man may brag of his science and skill, and however much, in a flattering future, that science and skill may augment; yet for ever and for ever, to the crack of doom, the sea will insult and murder him, and pulverize the stateliest, stiffest frigate he can make; nevertheless, by the continual repetition of these very impressions, man has lost that sense of the full awfulness of the sea which aboriginally belongs to it.

The first boat we read of, floated on an ocean that with Portuguese vengeance had whelmed a whole world without leaving so much as a widow. Yea, foolish mortals, Noah’s flood is not yet subsided; two thirds of the fair world it yet covers.

Wherein differ the sea and the land, that a miracle upon one is not a miracle upon the other? Preternatural terrors rested upon the Hebrews when under the feet of Korah the live ground opened and swallowed them up for ever; yet not a modern sun sets, but in precisely the same manner the live sea swallows ships and crews.

But not only is the sea such a foe to man who is an alien to it; it is also a fiend to its own off-spring; worse than the Persian host who murdered his own guests; sparing not the creatures which itself hath spawned. Like a savage tigress that tossing in the jungle overlays her own cubs, so the sea dashes even the mightiest whales against the rocks, and leaves them there side by side with the split wrecks of ships. No mercy, no power but its own controls it. Panting and snorting like a mad battle steed that has lost its rider, the masterless ocean overruns the globe.

Consider the subtleness of the sea; how its most dreaded creatures glide under water, unapparent, and treacherously hidden beneath the loveliest tints of azure; consider also the devilish brilliance and beauty of many of its most remorseless tribes; consider, once more, the universal cannibalism of the sea.

Consider all this; and then turn to this green, gentle, and most docile earth; consider them both; and do you not find a strange analogy to something in yourself? For as this appalling ocean surrounds the verdant land, so in the soul of man there lies one insular Tahiti, full of peace and joy, but encompassed by all the horrors of the half known life. God keep thee! Push not off from that isle, thou canst never return!

Slowly wading through the meadows of brit, the Pequod held on towards Java; and still, at wide intervals in the silvery night, the lonely, alluring jet would be seen.

But one transparent blue morning, when a stillness almost preternatural spread over the sea, however unattended with any stagnant calm; when the long burnished sun-glade on the waters seemed a golden finger laid across them, enjoining some secrecy; when the slippered waves whispered as they ran on; in this profound hush of the visible sphere a strange spectre was seen by Daggoo from the main-mast-head. In the distance, a great white mass lazily rose, disentangling itself from the azure, and at last gleamed before our prow like a snow-slide, new slid from the hills; subsiding; then once more arising and silently gleaming. It seemed not a whale; and yet is this Moby Dick? thought Daggoo. Again it went down; and on reappearing once more, with a stiletto-like cry that startled every man from his nod, Daggoo yelled out—“There! there again! there she breaches! right ahead! The White Whale, the White Whale!”

Upon this, the seamen rushed to the yard-arms, as bees to the boughs. Bare-headed in the sultry sun, Ahab stood on the bowsprit, one hand far behind, casting his eager glance where Daggoo’s motionless arm pointed. Whether the flitting attendance of the one still and solitary jet had gradually worked upon Ahab, so that he was now prepared to connect the ideas of mildness and repose with the first sight of the particular whale he pursued; however this was, or whether his eagerness betrayed him; whichever way it might have been, no sooner did he distinctly perceive the white mass, than with a quick intensity he instantly gave orders for lowering.

The four boats were soon on the water; Ahab’s in advance, and all swiftly pulling towards their prey. It went down; and, with oars suspended, we waited; lo! in the same spot it slowly rose. Almost forgetting for the moment all thoughts of Moby Dick, we now gazed at the most wondrous phenomenon which the secret seas have hitherto revealed to mankind. A vast pulpy mass, of a glancing cream-colour, lay floating on the water, innumerable long arms radiating from its centre, and curling and twisting like a nest of anacondas, as if blindly to clutch at any hapless object within reach. No perceptible face or front did it have; no conceivable token of either sensation or instinct; but undulated there on the billows, an unearthly, formless, chance-like apparition of life.

As with a low sucking sound it slowly disappeared again, Starbuck, still gazing at the agitated waters where it had sunk, with a wild voice exclaimed—“Almost rather had I seen Moby Dick and fought him, than to have seen thee, thou white ghost!”

“What was it, Sir?” said Flask.

“The great live squid, which, they say, few whale-ships ever beheld, and returned to their ports to tell of it.”

But Ahab said nothing; turning his boat, he sailed back to the vessel; the rest as silently following.

Whatever superstitions the sperm whalemen in general have connected with the sight of this object, certain it is, that a glimpse of it being so very unusual, that circumstance has gone far to invest it with portentousness. So rarely is it beheld, that though one and all declare it the largest animated thing in the ocean, very few have any but the most vague ideas concerning its true nature and form; notwithstanding, they believe it to furnish to the sperm whale his only food. For at times, when closely pursued, he will disgorge what are supposed to be the detached arms of the squid—some of them exceeding twenty and thirty feet; they fancy that the monster to which these arms belonged ordinarily clings by them to the bed of the ocean; and that the sperm whale, unlike other species, is supplied with teeth in order to attack and tear it. There seems some ground to imagine that the great Kraken of Bishop Pontoppodan may ultimately resolve itself into Squid; but much abatement is necessary with respect to the incredible bulk he assigns it. By some naturalists who have vaguely heard rumors of the mysterious creature here spoken of, it is included among the class of cuttle-fish—the Anak of the tribe.

For the better understanding of whaling scenes, speak now of the magical, sometimes horrible whale-line. Once of the best hemp, slightly vapored with tar, it is now in the American fishery almost entirely of Manilla—though not so durable as hemp, stronger, and far more soft and elastic; and I will add (since there is an æsthetics in all things), is much more handsome and becoming to the boat, than hemp. Hemp, dusky as an Indian; Manilla, a golden-haired Circassian.

Only two-thirds of an inch thick, its one and fifty yarns each suspend one hundred and twenty pounds; the whole rope bears nearly three tons. In length, the common sperm line measures a little over two hundred fathoms. Towards the stern it is spirally coiled in the tub as a round, cheese-shaped mass of densely bedded sheaves; the least kink, in running out, would infallibly take somebody’s arm, leg, or entire body off; hence the utmost precaution in stowing. In English boats, two smaller tubs hold one continuous line; the American single tub, three feet in diameter, makes a prodigious freight for a craft with a half-inch bottom—like critical ice which bears a distributed but not a concentrated weight. With the painted canvas cover clapped on, the boat looks as if pulling off with a great wedding-cake for the whales.

Both ends of the line are exposed. The lower end, eye-spliced, hangs disengaged over the tub’s edge—first, to bend on an additional line from a neighboring boat should the whale sound beyond the first supply; second, for common safety’s sake, lest the doomed boat be dragged down after him. Before lowering, the upper end runs aft round the loggerhead, forward the boat’s length, jogs every oarsman’s wrist, passes between the men to the leaded chocks in the prow, pinned from slipping, hangs in a slight festoon over the bows, returns within, and, after a box-coil, is attached to the short-warp and thence to the harpoon.

Thus the whale-line folds the whole boat in its complicated coils, twisting and writhing round it in almost every direction. To the timid landsman’s eye, the crew seem Indian jugglers, festooned with deadliest snakes. Nor can any son of mortal woman, for the first time, seat himself amid those hempen intricacies, and, while straining his utmost at the oar, bethink him that at any unknown instant the harpoon may be darted, and all these horrible contortions be put in play like ringed lightnings; he cannot be thus circumstanced without a shudder that makes the very marrow in his bones to quiver like a shaken jelly. Yet habit—strange thing! what cannot habit accomplish?—Gayer sallies, more merry mirth, better jokes, and brighter repartees, you never heard over your mahogany, than over the half-inch white cedar; and, like the six burghers of Calais, the six men pull into the jaws of death with a halter round every neck.

Hence those repeated disasters of men taken out by the line. When it darts out, to be seated is like being among the whizzings of a steam-engine in full play; for the boat rocks like a cradle, pitching you without warning; and only by a buoyant simultaneousness of volition and action can you escape being made a Mazeppa of, and run away with where the all-seeing sun could never pierce you out.
