More awful than the storm is the profound calm that precedes it—for, indeed, the calm is but the wrapper and envelope of the storm; and contains it in itself, as the seemingly harmless rifle holds the fatal powder, and the ball, and the explosion—so the graceful repose of the line before play carries more of true terror than any other aspect. But why say more? All men live enveloped in whale-lines. All are born with halters round their necks; but it is only when caught in the swift, sudden turn of death, that mortals realize the silent, subtle, ever-present perils of life. And if you be a philosopher, though seated in the whale-boat, you would not at heart feel one whit more terror than before your evening fire with a poker, and not a harpoon, by your side.

If to Starbuck the Squid was portent, to Queequeg it was omen. “When you see him ’quid,” said the savage, honing his harpoon in the hoisted bow, “then you quick see him ’parm whale.”

The next day was still and sultry; the crew, spell-bound by vacancy, drowsed. It was my turn at the foremast-head; with my shoulders leaning against the slackened royal shrouds, to and fro I idly swayed in what seemed an enchanted air. No resolution could withstand it; in that dreamy mood, losing all consciousness, at last my soul went out of my body; though my body still continued to sway as a pendulum will, long after the power which first moved it is withdrawn. Ere forgetfulness altogether came over me, I had noticed that the seamen at the main and mizzen-mast-heads were already drowsy. So that at last all three of us lifelessly swung from the spars, and for every swing that we made there was a nod from below from the slumbering helmsman. The waves, too, nodded their indolent crests; and across the wide trance of the sea, east nodded to west, and the sun over all. Suddenly bubbles seemed bursting beneath my closed eyes; like vices my hands grasped the shrouds; some invisible, gracious agency preserved me; with a shock I came back to life. And lo! close under our lee, not forty fathoms off, a gigantic Sperm Whale rolled like the capsized hull of a frigate, his broad glossy back glistening like a mirror; lazily undulating, and ever and anon tranquilly spouting his vapory jet, like a portly burgher smoking his pipe of a warm afternoon. But that pipe, poor whale, was thy last. As if struck by some enchanter’s wand, the sleepy ship and every sleeper started; voices from all parts, with the three notes from aloft, hailed his regular spout.

“Clear away the boats! Luff!” cried Ahab, dashing the helm down ere the helmsman could touch it. The whale turned majestically to leeward with steady tranquillity, making few ripples. Thinking him not yet alarmed, Ahab ordered no oar to be used, no word but whispers. So, seated like Ontario Indians, we silently paddled. Presently, the monster perpendicularly flitted his tail forty feet and sank. “There go flukes!” cried all. Stubb struck his match, lit his pipe—the respite of a sounding. When he rose again, Stubb’s boat had the vantage; paddles were dropped; oars came loudly into play. Still puffing, Stubb cheered on.

A mighty change had come over the fish—“head out,” obliquely elevating that buoyant, sharp-cutting head. “Start her, start her, my men! Don’t hurry—take plenty of time—but start her; start her like thunder-claps, that’s all,” spluttered Stubb through smoke. “Start her, now; give ’em the long and strong stroke, Tashtego. Start her, Tash, my boy—start her, all; but keep cool—cucumbers is the word—easy, easy—only start her like grim death and grinning devils, and raise the buried dead perpendicular out of their graves, boys—that’s all. Start her!” “Woo-hoo! Wa-hee!” screamed the Gay-Header in reply, raising some old war-whoop to the skies; as every oarsman in the strained boat involuntarily bounced forward with the one tremendous leading stroke which the eager Indian gave. But his wild screams were answered by others quite as wild. “Kee-hee! Kee-hee!” yelled Daggoo, straining forwards and backwards on his seat, like a pacing tiger in his cage. “Ka-la! Koo-loo!” howled Queequeg, as if smacking his lips over a mouthful of Grenadier’s steak.

“Stand up, Tashtego!—give it to him!” The harpoon flew. “Stern all!” Back-water! Something went hot and hissing along every wrist—the magical line. Stubb had caught two extra turns round the loggerhead; a hempen blue smoke jetted up and mingled with his pipe. The blistering line passed through both his hands—like holding an enemy’s sharp two-edged sword by the blade, while he strives to wrest it free. “Wet the line! wet the line!” he cried. The tub-oarsman dashed sea-water with his hat; more turns; the line held; the boat flew like a shark all fins. Stubb and Tashtego changed places—stem for stern. From the vibrating line, tighter than a harpstring, you’d have thought the craft had two keels—one cleaving water, the other air; a cascade at the bows; the tall Gay-Header crouched low to bring down his centre of gravity.

“Haul in—haul in!” Hands pulled the boat up even while towed on; ranging by his flank, Stubb, knee in the clumsy cleat, darted dart after dart; at the word, the boat alternately sterned from the horrible wallow, then ranged for another fling. The red tide poured from all sides like brooks down a hill; he rolled not in brine but in blood, which bubbled and seethed for furlongs. The slanting sun on that crimson pond sent back its reflection into every face, so all glowed like red men. Jet after jet of white smoke shot from his spiracle; vehement puff after puff from Stubb’s mouth; at each dart he hauled in upon his crooked lance, straightened it by blows against the gunwale, and sent it again.

“Pull up—pull up!—close to!” Reaching far over, Stubb slowly churned his long sharp lance into the fish, carefully churning, as if feeling after a gold watch swallowed, fearful of breaking it ere hooking it out. That gold watch was the innermost life. Struck—he started into his “flurry,” horribly wallowing in blood, overwrapping himself in mad, boiling spray; the imperilled craft, dropping astern, struggled blindly from that frenzied twilight to clear air. Abating, he rolled out again; surging; spasmodically dilating and contracting his spout-hole with sharp, cracking respirations. At last, gush after gush of clotted red gore, purple lees of wine, shot into the frighted air and ran down his motionless flanks. His heart had burst. “He’s dead, Mr. Stubb,” said Daggoo. “Yes; both pipes smoked out!” Stubb scattered his dead ashes over the water, and thoughtfully eyed the vast corpse he had made.

According to usage, the whale-boat pushes off with the headsman as temporary steersman, and the harpooneer pulling the foremost oar. It needs a strong, nervous arm to strike the first iron; often, in a long dart. Yet, however prolonged the chase, the harpooneer is expected to pull to the uttermost and set an example of superhuman activity—by incredible rowing and loud, intrepid cries. What it is to keep shouting at the top of one’s compass while all the other muscles are strained—none know but those who have tried it. For one, I cannot bawl very heartily and work very recklessly at one and the same time. In this straining, bawling state, with his back to the fish, he hears, “Stand up, and give it to him!”—drops and secures his oar, turns half round, snatches the iron from the crotch, and with what little strength remains, essays to pitch it. No wonder, out of fifty fair chances, not five are successful; no wonder harpooneers are cursed and disrated; no wonder some burst blood-vessels; no wonder some ships are four years with four barrels; for it is the harpooneer that makes the voyage, and if you take the breath out of his body, how find it there when most wanted?

At the second critical instant, when the whale starts to run, headsman and harpooneer likewise start to running fore and aft, changing places to the jeopardy of all. Now, I care not who maintains the contrary, but all this is both foolish and unnecessary. The headsman should stay in the bows from first to last; he should both dart the harpoon and the lance; no rowing should be expected of him except where obvious. Long experience convinces me that more failures come of the harpooneer’s exhaustion than of the whale’s speed. To insure the greatest efficiency in the dart, the harpooneers of this world must start to their feet from out of idleness, and not from out of toil.

Out of the trunk, the branches grow; out of them, the twigs. So, in productive subjects, grow the chapters.

The crotch—a notched stick, two feet long—stands in the starboard gunwale by the bow, a rest for the harpoon’s wooden end, its naked, barbed point projecting from the prow. Thereby the weapon lies to hand; the hurler snatches it up as readily as a backwoodsman swings his rifle from the wall. Two harpoons—the first and second irons—lie there, each on its own cord, to be darted in instant succession; if one draw, the other holds.

But the whale’s instantaneous, convulsive run often forbids the second; yet, already bent to the running line, it must be tossed overboard, else most terrible jeopardy to all hands. But this critical act is not always unattended with the saddest and most fatal casualties. Thenceforth the second iron becomes a dangling, sharp-edged terror, skittishly curvetting about both boat and whale, entangling the lines, or cutting them, and making a prodigious sensation in all directions. Nor, in general, is it possible to secure it again until the whale is fairly captured and a corpse. Imagine four boats on one unusually strong, knowing whale, and eight or ten loose second irons dancing at once about him. All these particulars are faithfully narrated here, as they will not fail to elucidate several most important, however intricate passages, in scenes hereafter to be painted.

Stubb’s whale had been killed far from the ship; in a flat calm we made a tandem of three boats and, with eighteen men, thirty-six arms, and one hundred and eighty thumbs and fingers, slowly towed the inert, heaving bulk toward the Pequod. Three dim lights in the main-rigging guided us; drawing nearer we saw Ahab drop more lanterns, issue the usual orders for securing it for the night, hand his light to a seaman, and vanish to his cabin. Though hot in the chase, he now showed some obscure impatience or despair, as if the dead body only reminded him that Moby Dick yet lived, and a thousand other whales were nothing to his monomaniac end. Tied by the head to the stern and by the tail to the bows, the black hull of whale and ship lay close, yoked like colossal bullocks—one reclining, one standing.

A word for the flukes: the surest and most reliable hold is the tail; but as, from its greater density and death’s flexibility, it sinks low, you cannot with the hand get at it from the boat to put the chain round it. The difficulty is overcome by a small line with a float and a mid-weight, led so the float rises on the far side; having girdled the mass, the chain follows and is locked fast round the smallest part, hard by the lobes.

If Ahab grew still, Stubb—flush with conquest—bustled; the staid Starbuck even yielded him the deck. One small, helping cause of all this liveliness was soon made manifest. Stubb was a high liver; he was somewhat intemperately fond of the whale as a flavorish thing to his palate. “A steak, a steak, ere I sleep! You, Daggoo! overboard you go, and cut me one from his small!” Here be it known, that though these wild fishermen do not, as a general thing, and according to the great military maxim, make the enemy defray the current expenses of the war (at least before realizing the proceeds of the voyage), yet now and then you find some of these Nantucketers who have a genuine relish for that particular part of the Sperm Whale designated by Stubb; comprising the tapering extremity of the body.

About midnight that steak was cut and cooked; and lighted by two lanterns of sperm oil, Stubb stoutly stood up to his spermaceti supper at the capstan-head, as if that capstan were a sideboard. Nor was Stubb the only banqueter on whale’s flesh that night. Mingling their mumblings with his own mastications, thousands on thousands of sharks, swarming round the dead leviathan, smackingly feasted on its fatness. The few sleepers below in their bunks were often startled by the sharp slapping of their tails against the hull; peering over the side you could just see them wallowing in the sullen, black waters, turning on their backs as they scooped out huge globular pieces, leaving hollows like a carpenter’s countersink.

Though amid all the smoking horror and diabolism of a sea-fight, sharks will gaze up like hungry dogs round a carving-table; though, were the whole affair turned upside down, it would still be a shocking sharkish business enough; and though they are the invariable outriders of slave-ships—yet never will you find them in such countless numbers, and gayer spirits, than around a dead sperm whale moored by night to a whaleship. If you have never seen that sight, suspend your decision about the propriety of devil-worship, and the expediency of conciliating the devil.

But Stubb heeded not the mumblings so nigh him. “Cook, cook!—where’s that old Fleece?” he cried, widening his legs for a surer base, and stabbing his fork into the dish as with a lance. The old black, roused from his warm hammock at a most unseasonable hour, came shambling from his galley—this old Fleece, this old Ebony—shuffling and limping, assisting his step with his tongs made of straightened iron hoops; in obedience to the word of command, he came to a dead stop opposite Stubb’s sideboard, bowed his arched back lower, and sideways inclined his head to bring his best ear into play.

“Cook,” said Stubb, rapidly lifting a rather reddish morsel, “don’t you think this steak is rather overdone? Don’t I always say that to be good, a whale-steak must be tough? See those sharks—prefer it tough and rare! Go talk to ’em: welcome to help themselves civilly, and in moderation—but they must keep quiet. Blast me, if I can hear my own voice. Away, cook—and preach to ’em!” Sullenly taking the lantern, Fleece leaned over the bulwarks and began: “Fellow-critters: I’se ordered here to say dat you must stop dat dam noise. Massa Stubb say you can fill your dam bellies up to de hatchings; but—” “Cook!”—a slap—“you mustn’t swear when you’re preaching.” “Well, den—Belubed fellow-critters:” Stubb approved—“Right! coax ’em to it”—and Fleece went on with a flourish of his tongs, but was collared again: “Talk to ’em gentlemanly.” Once more the sermon proceeded.

“Your woraciousness, fellow-critters, I don’t blame ye so much for; dat is natur, and can’t be helped; but to gobern dat wicked natur, dat is de pint. You is sharks, sartin; but if you gobern de shark in you, why den you be angel; for all angel is not’ing more dan de shark well goberned. Now, look here, bred’ren, just try wonst to be cibil, a helping yourselbs from dat whale. Don’t be tearin’ de blubber out your neighbour’s mout. Is not one shark dood right as toder to dat whale? And, by Gor, none on you has de right to dat whale; dat whale belong to some one else. I know some o’ you has berry brig mout, brigger dan oders; but den de brig mouts sometimes has de small bellies; so dat de brigness of de mout is not to swaller wid, but to bit off de blubber for de small fry ob sharks, dat can’t get into de scrouge to help demselves.” “Well done, old Fleece!” cried Stubb, “that’s Christianity; go on.” “No use goin’ on; de dam willains will keep a scougin’ and slappin’ each oder, Massa Stubb; dey don’t hear one word; no use a-preachin’ to such dam g’uttons as you call ’em, till dare bellies is full, and dare bellies is bottomless; and when dey do get ’em full, dey wont hear you den; for den dey sink in de sea, go fast to sleep on de coral, and can’t hear not’ing at all, no more, for eber and eber.” “Give the benediction, Fleece.” He lifted both hands over the fishy mob and cried: “Cussed fellow-critters! Kick up de damndest row as ever you can; fill your dam’ bellies ’till dey bust—and den die.”

“Now, cook—attend.” Stubb plied his fork, then cross-questioned him on age and birthplace; with sudden gravity: “Do you belong to the church?”—“Passed one once in Cape-Down.” “And where do you expect to go to, cook?” He mumbled of bed soon; Stubb held him: “Avast! heave to! I mean when you die.” “When dis old brack man dies,” said the negro slowly, changing his whole air and demeanor, “he hisself won’t go nowhere; but some bressed angel will come and fetch him.” “Fetch him? How? In a coach and four, as they fetched Elijah? And fetch him where?” “Up dere,” said Fleece, holding his tongs straight over his head. “So, then, you expect to go up into our main-top, do you, cook? But don’t you know the higher you climb, the colder it gets? Perhaps you expect to get into heaven by crawling through the lubber’s hole; but, no, no—regular way, round by the rigging; ticklish business, but must be done. Drop your tongs, cook, and hear my orders.”

“Well—for the future, when you cook another whale-steak for my private table here, the capstan, hold the steak in one hand, and show a live coal to it with the other; that done, dish it. To-morrow, when we are cutting in, stand by for the tips of his fins—put them in pickle. Have the ends of the flukes soused.” As Fleece limped off: “Cutlets for supper to-morrow in the mid-watch!—Avast! Whale-balls for breakfast—don’t forget.” And old Fleece, vanishing to his hammock, muttered: “Wish, by gor! whale eat him, ’stead of him eat whale. I’m bressed if he ain’t more of shark dan Massa Shark hisself.”

That mortal man should feed upon the creature that feeds his lamp, and, like Stubb, eat him by his own light—outlandish, yet historical.

It is upon record that France once prized the Right Whale’s tongue; that in Henry VIIIth’s time a court cook was rewarded for a sauce for barbacued porpoises; and porpoises to this day are made into well-spiced balls.

The fact is, that among his hunters at least, the whale would by all hands be considered a noble dish, were there not so much of him; but when you come to sit down before a meat-pie nearly one hundred feet long, it takes away your appetite. Only the most unprejudiced of men like Stubb, nowadays partake of cooked whales; but the Esquimaux are not so fastidious. We all know how they live upon whales, and have rare old vintages of prime old train oil. Zogranda, one of their most famous doctors, recommends strips of blubber for infants, as being exceedingly juicy and nourishing.

Certain Englishmen, long ago accidentally left in Greenland, lived for several months on the mouldy scraps of whales left ashore after trying out the blubber; among the Dutch whalemen these scraps are called “fritters”; which, indeed, they greatly resemble, being brown and crisp, and smelling something like old Amsterdam housewives’ dough-nuts or oly-cooks.

But what further depreciates the whale as a civilized dish is his exceeding richness—the great prize ox of the sea, too fat to be delicately good; the spermaceti itself, how bland and creamy that is; like the transparent, half-jellied, white meat of a cocoanut in the third month of its growth, yet far too rich to supply a substitute for butter—nevertheless, in the long try-watches the seamen dip their ship-biscuit into the huge oil-pots and let them fry there awhile.

In the case of a small Sperm Whale the brains—two plump, whitish lobes—are mixed with flour and cooked into a mess somewhat resembling calves’ head; which is quite a dish among some epicures. The head looks a sort of reproachfully at him, with an “Et tu Brute!” expression.

It is not, perhaps, entirely because the whale is so excessively unctuous that landsmen seem to regard the eating of him with abhorrence; that appears, in some way, from the consideration before mentioned: that a man should eat a newly murdered thing of the sea, and eat it too by its own light. But no doubt the first man that ever murdered an ox was regarded as a murderer; perhaps he was hung; and if he had been put on his trial by oxen, he certainly would have been; and he certainly deserved it if any murderer does. Go to the meat-market of a Saturday night and see the crowds of live bipeds staring up at the long rows of dead quadrupeds. Does not that sight take a tooth out of the cannibal’s jaw? Cannibals? who is not a cannibal? I tell you it will be more tolerable for the Fejee that salted down a lean missionary in his cellar against a coming famine; it will be more tolerable for that provident Fejee, I say, in the day of judgment, than for thee, civilized and enlightened gourmand, who nailest geese to the ground and feastest on their bloated livers in thy paté-de-foie-gras.

But Stubb, he eats the whale by its own light, does he? and that is adding insult to injury, is it? Look at your knife-handle, there, my civilized and enlightened gourmand dining off that roast beef, what is that handle made of?—what but the bones of the brother of the very ox you are eating? And what do you pick your teeth with, after devouring that fat goose? With a feather of the same fowl. And with what quill did the Secretary of the Society for the Suppression of Cruelty to Ganders formally indite his circulars? It is only within the last month or two that that society passed a resolution to patronize nothing but steel pens.

Commonly, when a Sperm Whale is brought alongside late at night, all sail is taken in; the helm lashed a’lee; and every one sent below till daylight, save the anchor-watches—two and two for an hour in rotation.

But sometimes, especially upon the Line, such incalculable hosts of sharks gather round the moored carcase that, were he left so for six hours, little more than the skeleton would be visible by morning. In most other parts of the ocean, however, their wondrous voracity can be at times considerably diminished by vigorously stirring them up with sharp whaling-spades—a procedure which, in some instances, only seems to tickle them into still greater activity. But it was not thus in the present case with the Pequod’s sharks; though, to be sure, any man unaccustomed to such sights, to have looked over her side that night, would have almost thought the whole round sea was one huge cheese, and those sharks the maggots in it.

Nevertheless, upon Stubb setting the anchor-watch after his supper was concluded; and when, accordingly, Queequeg and a forecastle seaman came on deck, no small excitement was created among the sharks; for, immediately suspending the cutting stages over the side, and lowering three lanterns so that they cast long gleams of light over the turbid sea, these two mariners, darting their long whaling-spades, kept up an incessant murdering of the sharks, by striking the keen steel deep into their skulls, seemingly their only vital part. But in the foamy confusion of their mixed and struggling hosts, the marksmen could not always hit their mark; and this brought about new revelations of the incredible ferocity of the foe. They viciously snapped, not only at each other’s disembowelments, but like flexible bows, bent round, and bit their own; till those entrails seemed swallowed over and over again by the same mouth, to be oppositely voided by the gaping wound. Nor was this all. It was unsafe to meddle with the corpses and ghosts of these creatures. A sort of generic or Pantheistic vitality seemed to lurk in their very joints and bones, after what might be called the individual life had departed. Killed and hoisted on deck for the sake of his skin, one of these sharks almost took poor Queequeg’s hand off, when he tried to shut down the dead lid of his murderous jaw.

“Queequeg no care what god made him shark,” said the savage, agonizingly lifting his hand up and down; “wedder Fejee god or Nantucket god; but de god wat made shark must be one dam Ingin.”

It was a Saturday night, and such a Sabbath as followed! Ex officio professors of Sabbath breaking are all whalemen. The ivory Pequod seemed a shamble; every sailor a butcher. You would have thought we were offering up ten thousand red oxen to the sea gods. The enormous cutting tackles—among other ponderous things comprising a cluster of blocks generally painted green, and which no single man can possibly lift—this vast bunch of grapes was swayed up to the main-top and lashed to the lower mast-head. Their hawser-like fall led to the windlass; the lower block swung over the whale, and the blubber-hook was bent. From stages, Starbuck and Stubb cut a hole above the side-fin, scarved a semicircle, and set the hook. Then, to a wild chorus, all hands heaved. The ship careened; every bolt in her starts like the nail-heads of an old house in frosty weather; she trembles, quivers, and nods her frighted mast-heads to the sky. A swift snap; a great swash; and the triumphant tackle rose, dragging the semicircular end of the first strip.

As the orange’s rind spirals from the fruit, so the blubber peels in one uniform strip along the scarf; the blanket-piece rises till it grazes the main-top and sways blood-dripping as if let down from the sky—and every one present must dodge its swing, else it may box his ears and pitch him headlong overboard. A boarding-sword slices a low hole; the second tackle hooks; then, with a few lunging cuts, the swordsman severs it in twain—short lower part still fast, the long blanket-piece clear for lowering. While one tackle peels and hoists, the other slackens, and down goes the strip through the main hatch to the blubber-room, where nimble hands coil it like a great live mass of plaited serpents. So the work proceeds: tackles alternating; whale and windlass heaving; heavers singing; mates scarfing; ship straining—and all hands swearing occasionally, by way of assuaging the general friction.

I have given no small attention to that not unvexed subject, the skin of the whale. I have had controversies about it with experienced whalemen afloat, and learned naturalists ashore.

What and where is the skin of the whale? From the unmarred body you can scrape an infinitely thin, transparent substance—isinglass-like, soft as satin until dried; I have used such shreds for markers in my whale-books; it is pleasant to read about whales through their own spectacles, as you may say. But that is rather the skin of the skin, so to speak. The only dense enveloping layer you can raise is the blubber: firm, elastic, compact, eight to fifteen inches thick. Assuming the blubber for skin, then, when a great Sperm yields a hundred barrels of oil, which is but three-fourths of its expressed substance, conceive the enormity of the mass whose mere integument pours such a lake.

In life, the Sperm’s visible surface is marvellously graven—crossed with numberless straight marks like the finest Italian line-engraving. Those marks are not upon the isinglass above mentioned, but seem seen through it, as if engraved upon the body itself; and, to the quick eye, they afford the ground for far other delineations. These are hieroglyphical; that is, if you call those mysterious cyphers on the walls of pyramids hieroglyphics, then that is the proper word to use in the present connexion. By my retentive memory of the hieroglyphics upon one Sperm Whale in particular, I was much struck with a plate representing the old Indian characters chiselled on the famous hieroglyphic palisades on the banks of the Upper Mississippi. Like those mystic rocks, too, the mystic-marked whale remains undecipherable. Besides, in some parts rude random scratches efface the lines—hostile contacts, I deem, among the large, full-grown bulls; New England rocks scored by ice may resemble him there.

Blanket-pieces they call the long strips—aptly; for he is wrapt in his blubber as in a blanket or Indian poncho. By this cosy surtout he keeps comfortable in all weathers, in all seas. Other fish—cold-blooded, lungless refrigerators—may bask under the lee of an iceberg; but the whale, like man, has warm blood and lungs: freeze his blood, and he dies. How wonderful is it then—except after explanation—that this great monster, to whom corporeal warmth is as indispensable as it is to man, should be found at home, immersed to his lips for life in those Arctic waters! where seamen fallen overboard have been found, months afterwards, frozen into the fields of ice, as a fly is found glued in amber. More surprising still, the blood of a Polar whale is warmer than that of a Borneo negro.

It does seem to me, that herein we see the rare virtue of a strong individual vitality, of thick walls, of interior spaciousness. Oh, man! admire and model thyself after the whale! Do thou, too, remain warm among ice. Do thou, too, live in this world without being of it. Be cool at the equator; keep thy blood fluid at the Pole. Like the great dome of St. Peter’s, and like the great whale, retain, O man! in all seasons a temperature of thine own.

But how easy and how hopeless to teach these fine things! Of erections, how few are domed like St. Peter’s! of creatures, how few vast as the whale!

“Haul in the chains! Let the carcase go astern!” The peeled white body of the beheaded whale flashes like a marble sepulchre; still colossal, it floats away, the water torn by insatiate sharks and the air vexed with screaming fowls, whose beaks are like so many insulting poniards in the whale. The white headless phantom floats further and further; at every rod what seem square roods of sharks and cubic roods of fowls augment the murderous din. For hours and hours from the almost stationary ship that hideous sight is seen. Beneath the unclouded and mild azure sky, upon the fair face of the pleasant sea, wafted by the joyous breezes, that great mass of death floats on and on, till lost in infinite perspectives.

There’s a most doleful and most mocking funeral! The sea-vultures all in pious mourning, the air-sharks all punctiliously in black or speckled. In life but few of them would have helped the whale, I ween, if peradventure he had needed it; but upon the banquet of his funeral they most piously do pounce. Oh, horrible vultureism of earth! from which not the mightiest whale is free.

Nor is this the end. Desecrated as the body is, a vengeful ghost survives and hovers over it to scare. Espied by some timid man-of-war or blundering discovery-vessel from afar, the distance obscuring the swarming fowls yet still showing the white mass floating in the sun and the white spray heaving high against it; straightway the whale’s unharming corpse, with trembling fingers is set down in the log—_shoals, rocks, and breakers hereabouts: beware!_ And for years afterwards, perhaps, ships shun the place; leaping over it as silly sheep leap over a vacuum, because their leader originally leaped there when a stick was held. There’s your law of precedents; there’s your utility of traditions; there’s the story of your obstinate survival of old beliefs never bottomed on the earth, and now not even hovering in the air! There’s orthodoxy!

Thus, while in life the great whale’s body may have been a real terror to his foes, in his death his ghost becomes a powerless panic to a world.

Are you a believer in ghosts, my friend? There are other ghosts than the Cock-Lane one, and far deeper men than Doctor Johnson who believe in them.

Before stripping, the leviathan was beheaded. Consider that the whale has nothing that can properly be called a neck; on the contrary, where his head and body seem to join, there, in that very place, is the thickest part of him. Do you not marvel, then, at Stubb’s boast, that he demanded but ten minutes to behead a sperm whale? When first severed, the head is dropped astern and held there by a cable till the body is stripped; but with a full grown leviathan this is impossible to hoist on deck, for the sperm whale’s head embraces nearly one third of his entire bulk, and completely to suspend such a burden as that, even by the immense tackles of a whaler, this were as vain a thing as to attempt weighing a Dutch barn in jewellers’ scales. The head was hoisted against the ship’s side—about half out of the sea—and there that blood-dripping mass hung to the Pequod’s waist like the giant Holofernes’s from the girdle of Judith. The seamen went below to their dinner. An intense copper calm, like a universal yellow lotus, was more and more unfolding its noiseless measureless leaves upon the sea.

Ahab came alone; took a few turns; then, in the main-chains, set Stubb’s spade crutch-wise and leaned over the black, hooded mass. It seemed the Sphynx’s head in a desert. “Speak, thou vast and venerable head,” he muttered, “which, though ungarnished with a beard, yet here and there lookest hoary with mosses; speak, mighty head, and tell us the secret thing that is in thee. Of all divers, thou hast dived the deepest. That head upon which the upper sun now gleams, has moved amid this world’s foundations. Where unrecorded names and navies rust, and untold hopes and anchors rot; where in her murderous hold this frigate earth is ballasted with bones of millions of the drowned; there, in that awful water-land, there was thy most familiar home. Thou hast been where bell or diver never went; hast slept by many a sailor’s side, where sleepless mothers would give their lives to lay them down. Thou saw’st the locked lovers when leaping from their flaming ship; heart to heart they sank beneath the exulting wave; true to each other, when heaven seemed false to them. Thou saw’st the murdered mate when tossed by pirates from the midnight deck; for hours he fell into the deeper midnight of the insatiate maw; and his murderers still sailed on unharmed—while swift lightnings shivered the neighboring ship that would have borne a righteous husband to outstretched, longing arms. O head! thou hast seen enough to split the planets and make an infidel of Abraham, and not one syllable is thine!”

“Sail ho!” cried a triumphant voice from the main-mast-head.

“Aye? Well, now, that’s cheering,” cried Ahab, suddenly erecting himself, while whole thunder-clouds swept aside from his brow. “That lively cry upon this deadly calm might almost convert a better man.—Where away?”

“Three points on the starboard bow, sir, and bringing down her breeze to us!”

“Better and better, man. Would now St. Paul would come along that way, and to my breezelessness bring his breeze! O Nature, and O soul of man! how far beyond all utterance are your linked analogies! not the smallest atom stirs or lives on matter, but has its cunning duplicate in mind.”

Hand in hand, ship and breeze blew on; soon the Pequod rocked, and the stranger proved a whaleship. Too far to windward, she shot by, so signals were set; she answered—the Jeroboam of Nantucket. She ranged abeam and lowered a boat; but as Starbuck rigged the side-ladder, the captain in the stern waved it off. A malignant epidemic raged aboard; though himself untainted and half a rifle-shot off, Captain Mayhew, adhering to shore-quarantine punctilio, would not come aboard. His boat kept a few yards off, now shoved ahead by a rolling wave, now dropping back.

At an oar sat a small, short, youngish man, freckled, redundant yellow hair, in a long-skirted, cabalistic coat; delirium fanatic in his eyes. “That’s he!” cried Stubb. Gabriel, as the Town-Ho had told, had been a great prophet among the Neskyeuna Shakers, descending from heaven through a trap-door with the seventh vial. Thence to Nantucket; where, with that cunning peculiar to craziness, he assumed a steady, common-sense exterior, and offered himself as a green-hand candidate for the Jeroboam’s whaling voyage; and once off soundings declared himself the archangel Gabriel, and commanded the captain to jump overboard. He published his manifesto, whereby he set himself forth as the deliverer of the isles of the sea and vicar-general of all Oceanica. The unflinching earnestness with which he declared these things;—the dark, daring play of his sleepless, excited imagination, and all the preternatural terrors of real delirium, united to invest this Gabriel in the minds of the majority of the ignorant crew with an atmosphere of sacredness; they feared and protected him; the captain could not land him. And since the epidemic had broken out, he carried a higher hand than ever; declaring that the plague, as he called it, was at his sole command; nor should it be stayed but according to his good pleasure. The poor devils cringed, sometimes rendering him homage as to a god. Such things may seem incredible; but, however wondrous, they are true; and not so striking is the fanatic’s measureless self-deception as his measureless power of deceiving and bedevilling so many others.

“I fear not thy epidemic,” Ahab called. “Come on board.” Gabriel sprang up: “Think of the fevers! Beware the horrible plague!” A headlong wave shot the boat ahead, drowning Mayhew’s words. When she drifted back Ahab hailed, “Hast thou seen the White Whale?”—“Think of thy whale-boat, stoven and sunk! Beware the horrible tail!” Again a wave tore them on.

When the sea’s caprice abated, Mayhew began the dark story of Moby Dick—Gabriel breaking in whenever the name arose. Not long from home, the Jeroboam had been warned of the White Whale’s havoc. Gabriel denounced any attack, pronouncing him the Shaker God incarnate. Yet, a year or two later, when Moby Dick was fairly sighted, Macey, the chief mate, burned to engage. The captain let him go; five men manned his boat. Gabriel, at the main-royal, tossed his arm and hurled prophecies of doom. Macey stood in the bow, reckless, essaying his lance, when a broad white shadow rose; its quick fanning took the oarsmen’s breath. Next instant the mate, so full of furious life, was smitten bodily into the air, and fell fifty yards off. Not a chip of the boat was harmed, nor a hair of any head; but the mate for ever sank. It is well to parenthesize that this kind of fatality is frequent: sometimes only the man is struck; sometimes the bow or thigh-board goes; strangest, recovered bodies often show no mark—stark dead.

“The vial! the vial!” shrieked Gabriel; he called the crew from further hunting; and the terrible event clothed him with added influence, as if he had fore-announced it specifically. He became a nameless terror aboard.

Mayhew ending, he asked Ahab if he intended to hunt the White Whale. “Aye,” said Ahab. Gabriel sprang up, pointing down: “Think of the blasphemer—dead, and down there!—beware of the blasphemer’s end!” Ahab turned aside. “Captain, my letter-bag—there is a letter for one of thy officers,” he said; and Starbuck fetched a sorely tumbled, mould-spotted missive, damp from a dark locker. Of such a letter, Death himself might well have been the post-boy. Ahab spelled it out: “Mr. Harry Macey, Ship Jeroboam.”—“It’s Macey—and he’s dead!” sighed Mayhew. “From his wife—let me have it.” “Nay, keep it thyself,” cried Gabriel to Ahab; “thou art soon going that way.” “Curses throttle thee!” yelled Ahab. Starbuck split the end of a long spade-pole to hold the letter clear of contagion; Ahab reached it toward the boat. The oarsmen, expectant, ceased rowing; the boat drifted astern; and, as if by magic, the letter ranged along Gabriel’s eager hand. He clutched, impaled it on a boat-knife, and sent it, thus loaded, back to the Pequod. It fell at Ahab’s feet. Gabriel screamed to give way, and the mutinous boat shot from our side.

The men, returning to the whale’s jacket, muttered many a strange hint of this wild affair, while the hoisted head jogged and the cut blubber bled.

In cutting-in there is much scampering—everything at once. We must go back a little. Upon first breaking ground in the whale’s back, Queequeg, as harpooneer, descended to insert the blubber-hook. Often he must stay till the flensing is done, the whale lying mostly submerged, the worked-on parts alone awash. So, ten feet below the deck, half on the whale and half in the sea as the vast mass revolved like a tread-mill, Queequeg floundered—in Highland costume of shirt and socks—while I, his bowsman, held him from the ship’s steep side. You have seen Italian organ-boys holding a dancing-ape by a long cord. Just so, from the ship’s steep side, did I hold Queequeg down there in the sea, by what is technically called in the fishery a monkey-rope, attached to a strong strip of canvas belted round his waist—and, Stubb’s improvement, made fast at the other end to my own.

It was a humorously perilous wedding. For better or worse we were wedded; should Queequeg sink, usage and honor forbade cutting the cord, but dragged me after. So an elongated Siamese ligature united us. Queequeg was my own inseparable twin brother; nor could I any way get rid of the dangerous liabilities which the hempen bond entailed. So strongly and metaphysically did I conceive of my situation then, that while earnestly watching his motions, I seemed to perceive that my own individuality was merged in a joint stock company of two; that my free will had received a mortal wound; and that another’s mistake or misfortune might plunge innocent me into unmerited disaster and death. Therefore, I saw here a sort of interregnum in Providence. And yet further pondering—while I jerked him now and then from between the whale and ship, which would threaten to jam him—I saw that this was the precise situation of every mortal that breathes; only, in most cases, he has this Siamese connexion with a plurality of other mortals. If your banker breaks, you snap; if your apothecary sends poison in your pills, you die. Handle the monkey-rope heedfully as I would, sometimes he jerked so that I came near sliding overboard; and do what I would, I had but one end of it.

Unappalled by last night’s massacre, sharks swarmed like bees, freshly allured by the pent blood now flowing. Right among them was Queequeg, pushing them aside with his feet; the miscellaneously carnivorous shark, drawn by such prey, will seldom touch a man. Nevertheless, wise to look sharp: besides my jerks, Tashtego and Daggoo, from the stages above, flourished keen spades over his head, slaughtering what they could reach—disinterested and indiscreet; half-hidden in blood-muddled water, their steel came nearer a leg than a tail. Poor Queequeg, iron hook in hand, strained and gasped, and prayed to Yojo.

Well, well, my dear comrade and twin-brother, thought I, as I drew in and then slacked off the rope to every swell of the sea—what matters it, after all? Are you not the precious image of each and all of us men in this whaling world? That unsounded ocean you gasp in, is Life; those sharks, your foes; those spades, your friends; and what between sharks and spades you are in a sad pickle and peril, poor lad. But courage! there is good cheer in store for you, Queequeg. For now, as with blue lips and blood-shot eyes the exhausted savage at last climbs up the chains and stands dripping and trembling, the steward advances and hands him—what? Some hot Cognac? No! a cup of tepid ginger and water.

“Ginger? Do I smell ginger?” suspiciously asked Stubb, coming near. “Yes, this must be ginger,” peering into the as yet untasted cup. Then standing as if incredulous for a while, he calmly walked towards the astonished steward slowly saying, “Ginger? ginger? and will you have the goodness to tell me, Mr. Dough-Boy, where lies the virtue of ginger? Ginger! is ginger the sort of fuel you use, Dough-boy, to kindle a fire in this shivering cannibal? Ginger!—what the devil is ginger? Sea-coal? firewood?—lucifer matches?—tinder?—gunpowder?—what the devil is ginger, I say, that you offer this cup to our poor Queequeg here.” There was, Stubb averred, some sneaking Temperance Society in it. He carried the kannakin to Starbuck, who owned it “poor stuff enough.” “It was Aunt Charity,” cried Dough-Boy; “she bade me never give the harpooneers any spirits, only this ginger-jub.” “Ginger-jub! you gingerly rascal!—but I hope I do no wrong, Mr. Starbuck; the captain’s orders—grog for the harpooneer on a whale.” “Enough,” said Starbuck; “only don’t hit him—go down with him, and get what thou wantest thyself.” Stubb returned with a dark flask for Queequeg, and Aunt Charity’s tea-caddy—for the waves.

All this while the prodigious head of a Sperm Whale hung to starboard; we must let it hang, praying the tackles hold, for other matters press. Drifting into yellow brit water—token of Right Whales—Stubb’s and Flask’s boats gave chase to tall spouts to leeward. Fast to a whale and towed perilously near the hull—“Cut, cut!”—they paid out line, pulled ahead; winning but a few feet, a swift tremor ran like lightning along the keel as the strained line, scraping beneath the ship, suddenly rose to view under her bows, snapping and quivering, its drippings flying like bits of broken glass. The fagged whale altered and went round the stern, towing both boats in a full circuit, while sharks quit the Sperm head for the new blood and drank like Israelites at the smitten rock. Thickening spout; a frightful roll; he turned upon his back a corpse.

Making fast to the flukes and preparing to tow, the headsmen talked. Stubb, disgusted with “so ignoble a leviathan,” wondered what Ahab wanted with the lump. Flask told the charm: a ship with a Sperm head starboard and a Right head larboard can never capsize. “I don’t know,” he added, “but I heard that gamboge ghost of a Fedallah saying so, and he seems to know all about ships’ charms. But I sometimes think he’ll charm the ship to no good at last. I don’t half like that chap, Stubb.” “Sink him! I never look at him,” cried Stubb; “Flask, I take that Fedallah to be the devil in disguise. The reason why you don’t see his tail is because he tucks it up out of sight; he carries it coiled away in his pocket, I guess; blast him! he’s always wanting oakum to stuff into the toes of his boots.” “What’s the old man have so much to do with him for?” “Striking up a swap or a bargain, I suppose.” “Bargain?—about what?” “Why, do ye see, the old man is hard bent after that White Whale, and the devil there is trying to come round him, and get him to swap away his silver watch, or his soul, or something of that sort, and then he’ll surrender Moby Dick.” “Pooh! Stubb, you are skylarking; how can Fedallah do that?” “I don’t know, Flask; but the devil is a curious chap, and a wicked one. Why, they say as how he once went sauntering into the old flag-ship, switching his tail devilish easy and gentlemanlike, asked for John, and—by the Lord—gave him the Asiatic cholera afore he got through with him.” “How old do you suppose Fedallah is, Stubb?” “Do you see that mainmast there?”—pointing to the ship—“well, that’s the figure one; now take all the hoops in the Pequod’s hold, and string along in a row with that mast, for oughts, do you see; well, that wouldn’t begin to be Fedallah’s age.” They bandied sailor’s theology—duck the devil; and if he ducks you, give him two black eyes; the governor won’t clap him in double-darbies, having signed a bond to roast the kidnappees he brings. “Do you suppose Fedallah wants to kidnap Captain Ahab?” “Do I suppose it? You’ll know it before long, Flask. But I am going now to keep a sharp look-out on him; and if I see anything very suspicious going on, I’ll just take him by the nape of his neck, and say—Look here, Beelzebub, you don’t do it; and if he makes any fuss, by the Lord I’ll make a grab into his pocket for his tail, take it to the capstan, and give him such a wrenching and heaving, that his tail will come short off at the stump—do you see; and then, I rather guess when he finds himself docked in that queer fashion, he’ll sneak off without the poor satisfaction of feeling his tail between his legs.” “And what will you do with the tail, Stubb?” “Do with it? Sell it for an ox whip when we get home;—what else?” “Now, do you mean what you say, and have been saying all along, Stubb?” “Mean or not mean, here we are at the ship.”

The boats were here hailed, to tow the whale on the larboard side where fluke-chains and other necessaries were already prepared for securing him. “Didn’t I tell you so?” said Flask; “yes, you’ll soon see this right whale’s head hoisted up opposite that parmacetti’s.” In good time it proved true; the Pequod, steeply leaned over by the first head, now, by the counterpoise of both, regained an even keel—sorely strained, though. “So, when on one side you hoist in Locke’s head, you go over that way; but now, on the other side, hoist in Kant’s and you come back again; but in very poor plight. Thus, some minds for ever keep trimming boat. Oh, ye foolish! throw all these thunder-heads overboard, and then you will float light and right.”

No customary stripping had yet been done; the carcases of both whales had dropped astern, and the head-laden ship looked a mule with overburdening panniers. Meantime, Fedallah, calmly eyeing the Right Whale’s head, ever and anon glanced from the deep wrinkles there to the lines in his own hand; and Ahab so chanced to stand that the Parsee occupied his shadow; while, if the Parsee’s shadow was there at all it seemed only to blend with, and lengthen Ahab’s. Laplandish speculations flew among the toilers at these passing things.

“Here, now, are two great whales, laying their heads together; let us join them, and lay together our own.” The Sperm and the Right—folio leviathans, the only whales regularly hunted—present, in their heads, extremes. The Sperm’s shows a certain mathematical symmetry and pervading dignity—grey-headed at the summit, tokening age and experience.

Let us now note what is least dissimilar—eye and ear. Far back, low near the jaw’s angle, a lashless eye, colt-small for such magnitude. From its sideway set, the whale can never see an object exactly ahead or astern. “If your bitterest foe were walking straight towards you, with dagger uplifted in broad day, you would not be able to see him, any more than if he were stealing upon you from behind.” In a word, “you would have two backs, so to speak; but, at the same time, also, two fronts (side fronts): for what is it that makes the front of a man—what, indeed, but his eyes?” Effectually divided by many cubic feet of head, each eye imparts a wholly separate picture—profound darkness and nothingness all between. Man may, in effect, be said to look out on the world from a sentry-box with two joined sashes for his window. But with the whale, these two sashes are separately inserted, making two distinct windows, but sadly impairing the view. This peculiarity is to be borne in mind in the fishery—and remembered in scenes to come.

A curious and most puzzling question arises here. So long as a man’s eyes are open in the light, seeing is involuntary; yet, though he may take in an undiscriminating sweep, he cannot attentively examine two things at the same instant; separate them and surround each with darkness, and, in bringing your mind to bear on one, “the other will be utterly excluded from your contemporary consciousness.” How is it, then, with the whale? True, both his eyes, in themselves, must simultaneously act; but is his brain so much more comprehensive and combining than man’s, that, at the same moment of time, he can attentively examine two distinct prospects, one on one side of him, and the other in an exactly opposite direction? If he can, then is it as marvellous as “if a man were able simultaneously to go through the demonstrations of two distinct problems in Euclid.”

It may be but an idle whim, but it has always seemed to me that the extraordinary vacillations of movement displayed by some whales when beset by three or four boats—their timidity and queer frights—indirectly proceed from the helpless perplexity of volition in which their divided and diametrically opposite powers of vision must involve them.

The ear is as curious: no external leaf; a minute hole a quill can hardly enter, lodged a little behind the eye. In the Sperm an external opening; in the Right, evenly covered by a membrane—imperceptible without. Is it not curious, that so vast a being should see the world through so small an eye, and hear the thunder through an ear smaller than a hare’s? But if his eyes were broad as the lens of Herschel’s great telescope, and his ears capacious as the porches of cathedrals, would that make him any longer of sight, or sharper of hearing? Not at all.—Why then do you try to “enlarge” your mind? Subtilize it.

Cant over the sperm whale’s head and peer down the mouth—its really beautiful and chaste-looking floor to ceiling papered with a glistening white membrane, glossy as bridal satins. Then behold the portentous lower jaw, long narrow lid of an immense snuff-box; pried overhead, its teeth make a terrific portcullis; and, fathoms down, some sulky whale floats with his prodigious jaw hanging straight for all the world like a ship’s jib-boom. This whale is not dead; he is only dispirited; out of sorts; hypochondriac; and so supine that the hinges of his jaw have relaxed, leaving him there in that ungainly plight, a reproach to all his tribe.

In most cases this lower jaw—being easily unhinged by a practised hand—is hoisted on deck to extract the ivory teeth. With a long, weary hoist it is dragged aboard; and, when the proper time comes—some few days after the other work—Queequeg, Daggoo, and Tashtego, being all accomplished dentists, are set to drawing teeth. With a keen cutting-spade, Queequeg lances the gums; then the jaw is lashed down to ringbolts, and a tackle being rigged from aloft, they drag out these teeth, as Michigan oxen drag stumps of old oaks out of wild wood lands. There are generally forty-two; in old whales much worn, but undecayed. The jaw is afterwards sawn into slabs, and piled away like joists.

Cross the deck to the Right Whale’s head. If the Sperm’s front is a rounded Roman chariot, the Right’s bears an inelegant resemblance to a gigantic galliot-toed shoe; an old Dutch voyager likened it to a shoemaker’s last—ample enough to lodge that nursery old woman and all her brood. Nearer, the head shifts aspect: stand atop; the two F-shaped spoutholes make it an enormous bass-viol; fix on the green barnacled “crown” or “bonnet” with live crabs, and he is a diademed king—though a sulky one. Look at that hanging lower lip! what a huge sulk and pout is there! a sulk and pout, by carpenter’s measurement, about twenty feet long and five feet deep; a sulk and pout that will yield you some 500 gallons of oil and more.

A pity, now, that this whale should be hare-lipped—about a foot across. Probably the mother during an important interval was sailing down the Peruvian coast, when earthquakes caused the beach to gape. Over this lip, as over a slippery threshold, we now slide into the mouth. Were I at Mackinaw, I should take this to be the inside of an Indian wigwam. Good Lord! is this the road that Jonah went? The roof is about twelve feet high, and runs to a pretty sharp angle, as if there were a regular ridge-pole there; while these arched, hairy sides present those wondrous, half-vertical, scimetar-shaped slats—say three hundred on a side—depending from the crown-bone: Venetian blinds whose hairy fringes strain the seas of brit. Marks and hollows in the central blinds serve some to count age, as oaks by rings. Old travelers called them whiskers, hogs’ bristles, fins; and, indeed, the ladies’ busks draw their stiffening from the same bone. In Queen Anne’s time the bone was in its glory, the farthingale all the fashion; and as those ancient dames moved about gaily, though in the jaws of the whale, as you may say; even so, in a shower, with the like thoughtlessness, do we nowadays fly under the same jaws for protection; the umbrella being a tent spread over the same bone.

Now forget blinds and whiskers; standing within among colonnades of bone, would you not think you were inside of the great Haarlem organ, and gazing upon its thousand pipes? For a carpet to the organ we have a rug of the softest Turkey—the tongue—fat and tender; this one, at a glance, a six-barreler.

To sum up: no great well of sperm here; no ivory teeth; no long slender mandible; while in the Sperm there are none of these blinds; no huge lower lip; and scarcely any tongue. Two external spout-holes to the Right; one only to the Sperm. Look your last while they lie together: one will soon sink unrecorded. The Sperm’s broad brow wears prairie-like placidity, speculative indifference as to death; the Right’s amazing lip, pressed to the vessel’s side, speaks of enormous practical resolution in facing death. This Right Whale I take to have been a Stoic; the Sperm Whale, a Platonian, who might have taken up Spinoza in his latter years.

Ere quitting, for the nonce, the Sperm Whale’s head, I would have you, as a sensible physiologist, simply—particularly remark its front aspect, in all its compacted collectedness. I would have you investigate it now with the sole view of forming to yourself some unexaggerated, intelligent estimate of whatever battering-ram power may be lodged there. Here is a vital point; for you must either satisfactorily settle this matter with yourself, or for ever remain an infidel as to one of the most appalling, but not the less true events, perhaps anywhere to be found in all recorded history. You observe that in the ordinary swimming position of the Sperm Whale, the front of his head presents an almost wholly vertical plane; you observe that the lower part of that front slopes considerably backwards, furnishing a retreat for the long socket of the boom-like lower jaw; you observe that the mouth is entirely under the head, much as though your own were under your chin. Moreover you observe that the whale has no external nose; and that what nose he has—his spout-hole—is on the top of his head; you observe that his eyes and ears are set far back at the sides, nearly one third of his entire length from the front. Wherefore, you must now have perceived that the front of the Sperm Whale’s head is a dead, blind wall, without a single organ or tender prominence whatsoever. Furthermore, you are now to consider that only in the extreme, lower, backward sloping part of that front is there the slightest vestige of bone; and not till near twenty feet from the forehead do you come to the full cranial development. So that this whole enormous boneless mass is as one wad. In some previous place I have described to you how the blubber wraps the body of the whale, as the rind wraps an orange. Just so with the head; but with this difference: about the head this envelope, though not so thick, is of a boneless toughness, inestimable by any man who has not handled it. The severest pointed harpoon, the sharpest lance, impotently rebounds from it; it is as though the forehead were paved with horses’ hoofs. I do not think that any sensation lurks in it.

Bethink yourself also of another thing. When two large, loaded Indiamen crowd and crush in the docks, the sailors do not interpose iron or wood, but hold there a large, round wad of tow and cork, enveloped in the thickest and toughest of ox-hide, which bravely and uninjured takes the jam that would have snapped oak and iron. By itself this illustrates the obvious fact I drive at. Supplementary to this, it has hypothetically occurred to me, that as ordinary fish possess a swimming bladder capable of distension or contraction, and as the Sperm Whale, as far as I know, has no such provision; considering the elasticity of his envelope, the unique interior of his head, and the otherwise inexplicable manner in which he now depresses it beneath the surface, and anon swims with it high elevated; those mystical lung-celled honeycombs there may possibly have some hitherto unsuspected connexion with the outer air, to be susceptible to atmospheric distension and contraction. If this be so, fancy the irresistibleness of that might, to which the most impalpable and destructive of all elements contributes. Now, mark. Unerringly impelling this dead, impregnable, uninjurable wall, and this most buoyant thing within; there swims behind it all a mass of tremendous life, only to be adequately estimated as piled wood is—by the cord; and all obedient to one volition, as the smallest insect.

So that when I shall hereafter detail his more inconsiderable braining feats, be ready to abide by this: that though the Sperm Whale stove a passage through the Isthmus of Darien, and mixed the Atlantic with the Pacific, you would not elevate one hair of your eye-brow. For unless you own the whale, you are but a provincial and sentimentalist in Truth. But clear Truth is a thing for salamander giants only to encounter; how small the chances for the provincials then? What befell the weakling youth lifting the dread goddess’s veil at Lais?

Now comes the Baling of the Case. Regard the Sperm Whale’s head as a solid oblong; sideways divide it into two quoins (Quoin is not a Euclidean term; it belongs to the pure nautical mathematics. I know not that it has been defined before): the lower, bone—cranium and jaws; the upper, an unctuous mass, wholly free of bone, its broad forward end forming the apparent vertical forehead. Horizontally subdivide that upper quoin at mid-forehead: the lower subpart is the junk—an immense honeycomb of oil, ten thousand infiltrated cells crossing and recrossing with tough elastic white fibres; the upper subpart is the Case—“the great Heidelburgh Tun of the Sperm Whale.” As that famous tierce is mystically carved, so the whale’s vast plaited forehead makes emblematical devices upon his tun; and as Heidelburgh brimmed with the wines of the Rhenish valleys, so this tun holds the most precious of his oily vintages—spermaceti in its absolutely pure, limpid, and odoriferous state. In life perfectly fluid, after death it concretes, sending forth delicate crystalline shoots like the first thin ice. A large Case yields about five hundred gallons; much is spilled and lost in the ticklish business of securing what you can.

I know not with what fine and costly material the Heidelburgh Tun was coated within, but in superlative richness that coating could not possibly have compared with the silken pearl-coloured membrane, like the lining of a fine pelisse, forming the inner surface of the Sperm Whale’s case. The Case embraces the entire length of the head’s top; if a whale be eighty feet, then more than twenty-six feet is the tun’s depth when hoisted lengthwise against a ship’s side. In decapitating, the cutting-tool is brought close to where entrance is afterwards forced into the spermaceti magazine; heed is needful, lest a careless, untimely stroke invade the sanctuary and wastingly let out the treasure. It is this decapitated end that is at last elevated out of water and retained there by the enormous cutting tackles—a wilderness of ropes in that quarter.

Thus much being said, attend now, I pray you, to that marvellous and—in this particular instance—almost fatal operation whereby the Sperm Whale’s great Heidelburgh Tun is tapped.

Nimble as a cat, Tashtego ran out upon the overhanging mainyard-arm above the hoisted Tun, swung a light whip’s end to deck, and hand-over-hand dropped to the summit of the head. There—still high elevated above the rest of the company, to whom he cries—he seems some Turkish Muezzin calling the good people to prayers from the top of a tower. Spade in hand, he sought the place to break in. A stout, iron-bound bucket was bent to the whip; with a long pole he guided it down into the Case. Up it came, bubbling like a dairy-maid’s pail of new milk; lowered, emptied to a tub; remounted; and so round till the deep cistern would yield no more—twenty feet of pole gone.

Then, all at once, a queer accident. Whether Tashtego in heedlessness loosed his one-handed hold on the great cabled tackles; or the footing was oozy; or the Evil One so willed, without stating his particular reasons; how it was exactly, there is no telling now; as the eightieth or ninetieth bucket came suckingly up—my God! poor Tashtego—like a twin reciprocating bucket dropped head-foremost down into the great Tun, and, with a horrible oily gurgling, went clean out of sight! “Man overboard!” cried Daggoo. Foot in the bucket, he shot aloft to the head. The before lifeless head now heaved and throbbed, as if that moment seized with some momentous idea; whereas it was only the poor Indian unconsciously revealing by those struggles the perilous depth to which he had sunk. A sharp crack—the unspeakable horror: one of the two enormous hooks tore out; the mass swung; the ship reeled as if smitten by an iceberg; the one remaining hook strained to part.

“Come down!” they yelled to Daggoo; but clinging to the heavy tackles, having cleared the foul, he rammed down the bucket to the collapsed well that Tash might grasp it. “Avast! How will that help him?” cried Stubb. Then a voice like the bursting of a rocket: “Stand clear of the tackle!” Thunder-boom—the enormous mass dropped like Niagara’s Table-Rock; the suddenly relieved hull rolled far to her copper; Daggoo swung through spray; and Tashtego sank. A naked figure with a boarding-sword flashed over the bulwarks—Queequeg dived.

Moments, ripples, no sign. “Ha! ha!” cried Daggoo from aloft; an arm thrust upright from the blue, as from grass over a grave. “Both! both!” Soon Queequeg struck out with one hand, the other clutching the Indian’s long hair. Tashtego was long in coming to; Queequeg looked not brisk.

The rescue? Diving after the descending head, Queequeg side-lunged with his keen sword near its bottom, scuttling a large hole; thrusting his long arm inwards and upwards, he hauled out Tash by the head. He averred a leg first offered; knowing that was not as it ought to be, and might occasion trouble, he thrust it back, and by a dexterous toss wrought a somerset, so the next trial came forth the good old way—head foremost.

And thus, through the courage and great skill in obstetrics of Queequeg, the deliverance, or rather, delivery of Tashtego, was successfully accomplished, in the teeth, too, of the most untoward and apparently hopeless impediments; which is a lesson not to be forgotten. Midwifery should be taught in the same course with fencing and boxing, riding and rowing. I know this queer adventure of the Gay-Header’s will seem incredible to some landsmen, though they may have seen or heard of one’s falling into a cistern ashore. But, peradventure, it may be sagaciously urged, how is this? We thought the tissued, infiltrated head was the lightest and most corky part about him; and yet thou makest it sink. Not at all, but I have ye; for when poor Tash fell in, the case had been nearly emptied of its lighter contents, leaving little but the dense, double-welded wall—much heavier than sea-water—so that a lump of it sinks like lead; the other parts of the head remaining undetached made it go very slowly, affording Queequeg a fair chance for performing his agile obstetrics on the run—yes, a running delivery. Now, had Tashtego perished in that head, it had been a very precious perishing; smothered in the very whitest and daintiest of fragrant spermaceti; coffined, hearsed, and tombed in the secret inner chamber and sanctum sanctorum of the whale. Only one sweeter end can readily be recalled—the delicious death of an Ohio honey-hunter, who, seeking honey in the crotch of a hollow tree, found such exceeding store that leaning too far over, it sucked him in, so that he died embalmed. How many, think ye, have likewise fallen into Plato’s honey head, and sweetly perished there?

Physiognomy and phrenology have not scanned this Leviathan’s lines; such an enterprise would seem almost as hopeful as for Lavater to have scrutinized the wrinkles on the Rock of Gibraltar, or for Gall to have mounted a ladder and manipulated the Dome of the Pantheon. I try all things; I achieve what I can. The Sperm Whale has no proper nose, whose absence should mar the countenance. Dash the nose from Phidias’s marble Jove, and what a sorry remainder! Nevertheless, Leviathan’s stately magnitude makes that same deficiency—hideous in the sculptured Jove—no blemish at all. Nay, it is an added grandeur. A nose to the whale would have been impertinent. As on your physiognomical voyage you sail round his vast head in your jolly-boat, your noble conceptions of him are never insulted by the reflection that he has a nose to be pulled. A pestilent conceit, which so often will insist upon obtruding even when beholding the mightiest royal beadle on his throne.

Perhaps the most imposing view is the full front. This aspect is sublime. In man a fine brow is like the East when troubled with the morning; in bull or elephant, grand; human or animal, the mystical brow is as that great golden seal affixed by the German emperors to their decrees. It signifies—“God: done this day by my hand.” But in most creatures, nay in man himself, very often the brow is but a mere strip of alpine land lying along the snow line. Few are the foreheads which like Shakespeare’s or Melancthon’s rise so high, and descend so low, that the eyes themselves seem clear, eternal, tideless mountain lakes; and all above them in the forehead’s wrinkles, you seem to track the antlered thoughts descending there to drink, as the Highland hunters track the snow prints of the deer. In the great Sperm Whale this god-like dignity of brow is so immensely amplified that, gazing full-front, you feel the Deity and dread powers more forcibly than in any living object. “Nothing but that one broad firmament of a forehead, pleated with riddles; dumbly lowering with the doom of boats, and ships, and men.”

Genius? He has written no book, spoken no speech. It is declared in his doing nothing particular to prove it; declared in his pyramidical silence. Had the young Orient deified the tongueless crocodile, much more had it enthroned this tongueless lord. Be sure, exalted to Jove’s high seat, the great Sperm Whale shall lord it. But there is no Champollion to decipher the Egypt of every man’s and every being’s face. Physiognomy, like every other human science, is but a passing fable. How may unlettered Ishmael hope to read the awful Chaldee of the Sperm Whale’s brow? I but put that brow before you. Read it if you can.

If physiognomically the Sperm Whale be a Sphinx, to the phrenologist his brain is that circle impossible to square. Unhinge the jaw; the skull’s side shows a moderately inclined plane; in life it is angularly filled up and almost squared by the enormous superincumbent junk and sperm. At the high end the skull forms a crater to bed part of that mass; beneath its long floor—in another cavity seldom exceeding ten inches—reposes the mere handful of brain, at least twenty feet from the apparent forehead, hidden behind vast outworks like the innermost citadel within the amplified fortifications of Quebec. So like a choice casket is it secreted in him, that I have known some whalemen who peremptorily deny that the Sperm Whale has any other brain than that palpable semblance of one formed by the cubic-yards of his sperm magazine. Lying in strange folds, courses, and convolutions, to their apprehensions, it seems more in keeping with the idea of his general might to regard that mystic part of him as the seat of his intelligence. It is plain, then, that phrenologically the head, in the creature’s living intact state, is an entire delusion; the whale, like all things that are mighty, wears a false brow to the common world.

If you unload his skull of its spermy heaps and take a rear view of the high end, you will be struck with its resemblance to a human skull reversed; scaled to man’s size, you might confound it on a plate; remarking summit-depressions, you would say—This man had no self-esteem, and no veneration. And by those negations, considered along with the affirmative fact of his prodigious bulk and power, you can best form to yourself the truest, though not the most exhilarating conception of what the most exalted potency is.

Consider the spine: vertebræ like a strung necklace of dwarfed skulls—a German conceit of undeveloped skulls. A foreign friend once pointed it out to me, in the skeleton of a foe he had slain, and with the vertebræ of which he was inlaying, in a sort of basso-relievo, the beaked prow of his canoe. Now, I consider that the phrenologists have omitted an important thing in not pushing their investigations from the cerebellum through the spinal canal. For I believe that much of a man’s character will be found betokened in his backbone. “I would rather feel your spine than your skull, whoever you are.” I rejoice in my spine, as in the firm audacious staff of that flag which I fling half out to the world. Apply this spinal branch of phrenology to the Sperm Whale: the cranial cavity is continuous with the first vertebra; the spinal canal, at its origin, is of nearly equal girth with the brain for many feet; it is filled with the same strangely fibrous substance, directly communicating with the brain. Thus the comparative smallness of brain proper is more than compensated by the magnitude of the spinal cord. Assume, then, a spinal phrenology: the august hump rises over one of the larger vertebræ, its outer convex mould. From that relative site, call it the organ of firmness or indomitableness. And that he is indomitable, you will yet have reason to know.

The predestinated day arrived, and we duly met the ship Jungfrau, Derick De Deer, master, of Bremen; once the greatest whaling people, the Dutch and Germans are now among the least; yet here, at wide intervals, you still occasionally meet their flag in the Pacific. For some reason the Jungfrau was eager to pay her respects. While yet a distance off, she rounded to, dropped a boat, and her captain came bow-forward, impatient as a man who would be at once both host and petitioner.

“What has he in his hand there?” cried Starbuck. “Impossible!—a lamp-feeder!”

“Not that,” said Stubb; “no, no, it’s a coffee-pot, Mr. Starbuck; he’s coming off to make us our coffee, is the Yarman; don’t you see that big tin can there alongside of him?—that’s his boiling water. Oh! he’s all right, is the Yarman.”

“Go along with you,” cried Flask, “it’s a lamp-feeder and an oil-can. He’s out of oil, and has come a-begging.”

However curious to see an oil-ship borrowing oil on the whale-ground, yet such things happen; and in the present case Captain Derick De Deer did indubitably flourish a lamp-feeder. As he mounted the deck, Ahab abruptly accosted him; but in his broken lingo the German soon evinced his complete ignorance of the White Whale; immediately turned the talk to his lamp-feeder and oil can, to his dark hammocks, his last drop of Bremen oil being gone, and not a single flying-fish yet captured; concluding by hinting that his ship was what we call clean—well named Jungfrau, the Virgin. His necessities supplied, he departed; but had not gained his ship’s side when whales were raised from the mast-heads of both vessels; and so eager was he that, without pausing to put aboard even his oil-can and lamp-feeder, he slewed round and made after the leviathan lamp-feeders.

The game rose to leeward; the Jungfrau’s four boats had the start of ours. There were eight whales, abreast, going before the wind; in their wide wake, many fathoms astern, swam a huge humped old bull, yellowed as by jaundice, his spout short and choking, his wake dashed where back waters met; now and then he rolled and exposed the unnatural stump of his starboard fin.

“Who’s got some paregoric?” said Stubb. “He has the stomach-ache, I’m afraid. Lord, think of having half an acre of stomach-ache! Adverse winds are holding mad Christmas in him, boys. It’s the first foul wind I ever knew to blow from astern; but look, did ever whale yaw so before? it must be, he’s lost his tiller.”

As an overladen Indiaman bearing down the Hindostan coast with a deck load of frightened horses, careens, buries, rolls, and wallows on her way; so did this old whale heave his aged bulk, and now and then partly turning over on his cumbrous rib-ends, expose the cause of his devious wake in the unnatural stump of his starboard fin.

“Only wait a bit, old chap, and I’ll give ye a sling for that wounded arm,” cried cruel Flask to the whale-line.

“Mind he don’t sling thee with it,” cried Starbuck. “Give way, or the German will have him.”

With one intent all the combined rival boats were pointed for this one fish. Derick still led; he even shook his lamp-feeder in derision.

“The ungracious and ungrateful dog!” cried Starbuck; “he mocks and dares me with the very poor-box I filled for him not five minutes ago!”—then in his old intense whisper—“Give way, greyhounds! Dog to it!”

“I tell ye what it is, men”—cried Stubb to his crew—“it’s against my religion to get mad; but I’d like to eat that villainous Yarman—Pull—won’t ye? Are ye going to let that rascal beat ye? Do ye love brandy? A hogshead of brandy, then, to the best man. Come, why don’t some of ye burst a blood-vessel? Who’s that been dropping an anchor overboard—we don’t budge an inch—we’re becalmed. Halloo, here’s grass growing in the boat’s bottom—and by the Lord, the mast there’s budding. This won’t do, boys. Look at that Yarman! The short and long of it is, men, will ye spit fire or not?”

“Oh! see the suds he makes!” cried Flask, dancing up and down—“What a hump—Oh, do pile on the beef—lays like a log! Oh! my lads, do spring—slap-jacks and quahogs for supper—baked clams and muffins—oh, do, do, spring,—he’s a hundred barreller—don’t lose him now—don’t oh, don’t!—see that Yarman—Oh, won’t ye pull for your duff, my lads—such a sog! such a sogger! Don’t ye love sperm? There goes three thousand dollars, men!—a bank!—a whole bank! The bank of England!—Oh, do, do, do!—What’s that Yarman about now?”

At this moment Derick, in unmannerly spite, pitched overboard his lamp-feeder and oil-can to foul us and spur himself. “Pull now, men, like fifty thousand line-of-battle-ship loads of red-haired devils,” cried Stubb. “What d’ye say, Tashtego?”

“I say, pull like god-dam,” cried the Indian.

Fiercely, but evenly incited by the taunts of the German, the Pequod’s three boats now began ranging almost abreast; and, so disposed, momentarily neared him. In that fine, loose, chivalrous attitude of the headsman when drawing near to his prey, the three mates stood up proudly, occasionally backing the after oarsman with an exhilarating cry of, “There she slides, now! Hurrah for the white-ash breeze! Down with the Yarman! Sail over him!”

But so decided a start had Derick, that spite of all their gallantry, he would have proved the victor, had not a crab caught his midship oarsman’s blade. While Derick stormed and his boat nearly capsized, Starbuck, Stubb, and Flask slanted up on his quarter; an instant more and all four boats lay diagonically in the whale’s immediate wake, the foaming swell stretching from them on both sides.

It was a terrific, most pitiable, and maddening sight. The whale was now going head out, his one poor fin beating his side in an agony; now to this hand, now to that, he yawed in his faltering flight; and still at every billow he spasmodically sank, or sideways rolled up his one beating fin. So have I seen a bird with clipped wing making affrighted broken circles in the air, vainly striving to escape the piratical hawks.
