But the bird has a voice; the fear of this vast dumb brute was chained in him; he had no voice, save that choking respiration through his spiracle; while still, in his amazing bulk, portcullis jaw, and omnipotent tail, there was enough to appal the stoutest who so pitied.

Derick risked a long dart; but no sooner did his harpooneer rise than Queequeg, Tashtego, and Daggoo sprang to their feet, and in a diagonal row simultaneously pointed their barbs; “Blinding vapors of foam and white-fire!”—their three Nantucket irons entered the whale, the three boats bumped the German’s aside; Derick and his baffled harpooneer were spilled and sailed over.

“Don’t be afraid, my butter-boxes,” cried Stubb as he shot by; “ye’ll be picked up presently—I saw some sharks astern—St. Bernard’s dogs, you know. Hurrah! Every keel a sunbeam!—Here we go like three tin kettles at the tail of a mad cougar! This puts me in mind of fastening to an elephant in a tilbury on a plain—makes the wheel-spokes fly, boys, when you fasten to him that way; and there’s danger of being pitched out too, when you strike a hill. Hurrah! this is the way a fellow feels when he’s going to Davy Jones—all a rush down an endless inclined plane! Hurrah! this whale carries the everlasting mail!”

The monster’s rush was brief; he sounded tumultuously. The three lines flew round the loggerheads, gouging deep grooves; the bows were almost awash, the sterns tilting high. Holding on thus often torments Leviathan into rising to meet the lance; yet the longer he stays below, the more he is exhausted by the vast pressure of the sea.

As the three boats lay there on that gently rolling noon-blue, gazing down, not eight inches of perpendicular rope were visible at the bows. Seems it credible that by three such thin threads the great Leviathan was suspended like the big weight to an eight day clock? Suspended? and to what? To three bits of board. Is this the creature of whom it was once so triumphantly said—“Canst thou fill his skin with barbed irons? or his head with fish-spears? The sword of him that layeth at him cannot hold, the spear, the dart, nor the habergeon: he esteemeth iron as straw; the arrow cannot make him flee; darts are counted as stubble; he laugheth at the shaking of a spear!” This the creature? this he? Oh! that unfulfilments should follow the prophets. For with the strength of a thousand thighs in his tail, he had run his head under the mountains of the sea, to hide him from the Pequod’s fish-spears!

In that sloping afternoon sunlight, the shadows that the three boats sent down beneath the surface must have been long enough and broad enough to shade half Xerxes’ army. Who can tell how appalling to the wounded whale must have been such huge phantoms flitting over his head!

“Stand by, men; he stirs,” cried Starbuck, as the lines vibrated, conducting up, as by magnetic wires, the life and death throbs of the whale, so that every oarsman felt them in his seat. The next moment, relieved in great part from the downward strain, the boats gave a sudden bounce upwards.

“Haul in! Haul in!” cried Starbuck again; “he’s rising.” The coils came in hand over hand, and soon the whale broke water within two ship’s lengths, his motions denoting extreme exhaustion. In land beasts, certain valves in the veins shut off the blood at a wound; not so with the whale, whose non-valvular blood makes a deadly drain at a mere puncture; yet so vast is the quantity, he will bleed and bleed, like a river in drought whose source is far-off hills.

As the boats now more closely surrounded him, the whole upper part of his form, with much ordinarily submerged, was revealed. His eyes, or rather the places where his eyes had been, were beheld; from those points now protruded blind bulbs, horribly pitiable to see. But pity there was none. For all his old age, and his one arm, and his blind eyes, he must die the death and be murdered, to light the gay bridals and other merry-makings of men, and to illuminate the solemn churches that preach unconditional inoffensiveness by all to all. Still rolling in his blood, at last he partially disclosed a strangely discolored bunch low on the flank.

“A nice spot,” cried Flask, “just let me prick him there once.”

“Avast!” cried Starbuck; “there’s no need of that!”—but humane Starbuck was too late. An ulcerous jet shot from the cruel wound; goaded beyond sufferance, spouting thick blood, he blindly darted at the craft, bespattering crews with gore, capsizing Flask’s boat, marring bows—his death stroke. Spent by loss of blood, he rolled away; impotently flapped with his stumped fin; over and over slowly revolved like a waning world; turned up the white secrets of his belly; lay like a log, and died. As when by unseen hands the water is gradually drawn off from some mighty fountain, and with half-stifled melancholy gurglings the spray-column lowers and lowers to the ground—so the last long dying spout of the whale.

The body showed signs of sinking with all its treasures unrifled. By Starbuck’s orders, lines were secured at different points, each boat a buoy, till the ship came up. He was fluke-chained fast; cutting in, we found the corroded length of a buried harpoon in the bunch, and, stranger yet, a lance-head of stone. What other marvels that cabinet held none can say; for suddenly the ship was dragged sideways—the body’s tendency to sink immensely increasing. Starbuck hung on till the Pequod herself would have capsized if still locking arms with the corpse; but when the command came to break clear, the strain on the timber-heads made it impossible to cast off. Everything was aslant; the ship groaned; ivory inlayings started. Handspikes and crows were vain.

“Hold on, won’t ye?” cried Stubb to the body. “Run one of ye for a prayer book and a pen-knife, and cut the big chains.”

“Knife? Aye, aye,” cried Queequeg; seizing the carpenter’s heavy hatchet, he leaned out a porthole and slashed at the fluke-chains. A few strokes full of sparks—and with a terrific snap every fastening went adrift; the ship righted; the carcase sank.

This occasional sinking of the newly-killed Sperm Whale is curious. Not the meagre old only go down; brawny, buoyant heroes sometimes sink. After hours or days, gases generate; he swells and rises—a sort of animal balloon. On New Zealand grounds, when a Right Whale gives token of sinking, they buoy him for the resurrection.

Not long after the sinking, a cry from our mast-heads announced the Jungfrau again lowering; the only spout in sight was a Fin-Back’s—uncapturable for his incredible speed, yet whose spout the unskilful mistake for the Sperm’s. Derick and all his host went valiantly after that unnearable brute; the Virgin crowding all sail, vanished to leeward in hopeful chase. Oh! many are the Fin-Backs, and many are the Dericks, my friend.

There are some enterprises in which a careful disorderliness is the true method. The deeper I dive into whaling’s spring-heads, the more I am impressed with its honorableness and antiquity; demi-gods and prophets have shed distinction upon it, and I am transported to belong, though subordinately, to so emblazoned a fraternity.

The gallant Perseus, a son of Jupiter, was the first whaleman; and to our calling’s eternal honor, the first whale attacked was struck not for sordid lamp-feeders, but to succor the distressed. Every one knows Andromeda—tied to a rock, and as Leviathan was bearing her off, Perseus harpooned the monster and married the maid; slain, too, at the very first dart. Doubt not the Arkite story; in ancient Joppa a vast whale skeleton long stood, held to be the identical bones of Perseus’ fish. What seems most singular is this: it was from Joppa that Jonah set sail.

Akin is St. George and the Dragon. I maintain the dragon was a whale; whales and dragons are jumbled in old chronicles; “Thou art as a lion of the waters, and as a dragon of the sea,” saith Ezekiel. Besides, it would much subtract from the glory had the saint but met a crawling reptile of the land; Any man may kill a snake, but only a Perseus, a St. George, a Coffin, have the heart in them to march boldly up to a whale. Let not the modern paintings mislead: though griffin-shaped and the saint on horseback, the beast might have wallowed on the beach; the saint’s steed been a sea-horse; and artists then knew not the whale’s true form. In fact, placed before the strict and piercing truth, this whole story will fare like that fish, flesh, and fowl idol of the Philistines, Dagon by name; who being planted before the ark of Israel, his horse’s head and both the palms of his hands fell off from him, and only the stump or fishy part of him remained. Thus one of our noble stamp, even a whaleman, is England’s tutelary guardian; and by rights we Nantucketers should be of the most noble order of St. George; let not those knights disdain a Nantucketer, since even in our woollen frocks and tarred trowsers we are better entitled to their decoration than they.

Whether to admit Hercules among us or not, concerning this I long remained dubious: for though according to the Greek mythologies, that antique Crockett and Kit Carson—that brawny doer of rejoicing good deeds, was swallowed down and thrown up by a whale; still, whether that strictly makes a whaleman of him, that might be mooted. It nowhere appears that he ever actually harpooned his fish, unless, indeed, from the inside. Nevertheless, he may be deemed a sort of involuntary whaleman; at any rate the whale caught him, if he did not the whale. I claim him for one of our clan. By best contradictory authorities, the Grecian story is derived from the Hebrew Jonah; and vice versâ; if I claim the demi-god, why not the prophet?

Nor heroes and saints alone. Our grand master is still to be named; for like royal kings of old times, we find the head waters of our fraternity in nothing short of the great gods themselves. That wondrous oriental story is now to be rehearsed from the Shaster, which gives us the dread Vishnoo, one of the three persons in the godhead of the Hindoos; gives us this divine Vishnoo himself for our Lord. When Brahma, or the God of Gods, saith the Shaster, resolved to recreate the world after one of its periodical dissolutions, he gave birth to Vishnoo, to preside over the work; but the Vedas, or mystical books, whose perusal would seem to have been indispensable to Vishnoo before beginning the creation, and which therefore must have contained something in the shape of practical hints to young architects, these Vedas were lying at the bottom of the waters; so Vishnoo became incarnate in a whale, and sounding down in him to the uttermost depths, rescued the sacred volumes. Was not this Vishnoo a whaleman, then?

Perseus, St. George, Hercules, Jonah, and Vishnoo! there’s a member-roll for you! What club but the whaleman’s can head off like that?

Some Nantucketers distrust Jonah’s story; but as with some sceptical Greeks and Romans who doubted Hercules and the whale, and Arion and the dolphin, their doubtings made those traditions no whit the less facts.

One old Sag-Harbor whaleman argued from a quaint Bible plate that Jonah’s whale had two spouts—a Right Whale trait, whose swallow is so small “a penny roll would choke him.” Bishop Jebb answers beforehand: Jonah need not be tombed in the belly, but temporarily lodged in some part of the mouth; the Right Whale’s mouth would accommodate a couple of whist-tables and all the players.

Sag-Harbor urged gastric juices; but a German exegetist supposes Jonah took refuge in a floating dead whale—as French soldiers in Russia turned dead horses into tents. Others divine he swam to a vessel with a whale for a figure-head; and some opined the “whale” meant a life-preserver—an inflated bag. Poor Sag-Harbor, therefore, seems worsted all round. But he had still another reason for his want of faith. It was this, if I remember right:

Jonah was swallowed in the Mediterranean, and after three days was vomited within three days’ journey of Nineveh, on the Tigris—very much more than three days’ march from the nearest Mediterranean shore. How is that? But was there no other way for the whale to land the prophet within that short distance of Nineveh? Yes. He might have carried him round by the way of the Cape of Good Hope. Such a supposition involves the complete circumnavigation of Africa in three days; and the Tigris is too shallow for whales. Besides, this idea of Jonah’s weathering the Cape of Good Hope at so early a day would wrest the honor of the discovery of that great headland from Bartholomew Diaz, its reputed discoverer, and so make modern history a liar. But all these foolish arguments of old Sag-Harbor only evinced his foolish pride of reason—a thing still more reprehensible in him, seeing that he had but little learning except what he had picked up from the sun and the sea. I say it only shows his foolish, impious pride, and abominable, devilish rebellion against the reverend clergy. For a Portuguese Catholic priest advanced this very idea of Jonah’s going to Nineveh via the Cape of Good Hope as a signal magnification of the general miracle; and so it was. Besides, to this day the highly enlightened Turks devoutly believe; and an old English traveller speaks of a Turkish Mosque built in Jonah’s honor, with a miraculous lamp that burnt without oil.

To make them run easily and swiftly, the axles of carriages are anointed; and for much the same purpose, some whalers perform an analogous operation upon their boat; they grease the bottom. Nor is it to be doubted that as such a procedure can do no harm, it may possibly be of no contemptible advantage; considering that oil and water are hostile; that oil is a sliding thing, and that the object in view is to make the boat slide bravely. Queequeg believed strongly in such anointings, and one morning crawled under the hanging craft, rubbing in the unctuousness as though diligently seeking to insure a crop of hair from the craft’s bald keel. He seemed to be working in obedience to some particular presentiment. Nor did it remain unwarranted by the event.

Towards noon whales were raised; but so soon as the ship sailed down to them, they turned and fled with swift precipitancy; a disordered flight, as of Cleopatra’s barges from Actium. Nevertheless, the boats pursued, and Stubb’s was foremost. Tashtego planted one iron; but the stricken whale, without at all sounding, still continued his horizontal flight, with added fleetness. It became imperative to lance the flying whale, or be content to lose him; yet to haul the boat up to his flank was impossible.

Of all the wondrous devices and dexterities, the sleights of hand and countless subtleties, to which the veteran whaleman is so often forced, none exceed that fine manœuvre with the lance called pitchpoling. Its grand fact and feature is the wonderful distance to which the long lance is accurately darted from a violently rocking boat, under extreme headway; steel and wood included, the spear is some ten or twelve feet in length, slighter than the harpoon, of light pine, and furnished with a warp by which it is hauled back.

Look now at Stubb; a man who from his humorous, deliberate coolness and equanimity in the direst emergencies, was specially qualified to excel in pitchpoling. Look at him; he stands upright in the tossed bow of the flying boat; wrapt in fleecy foam, the towing whale is forty feet ahead. Handling the long lance lightly, glancing twice or thrice along its length to see if it be exactly straight, Stubb whistlingly gathers up the coil of the warp in one hand, so as to secure its free end in his grasp, leaving the rest unobstructed. Then holding the lance full before his waistband’s middle, he levels it at the whale; when, covering him with it, he steadily depresses the butt-end in his hand, thereby elevating the point till the weapon stands fairly balanced upon his palm, fifteen feet in the air. He minds you somewhat of a juggler, balancing a long staff on his chin. Next moment with a rapid, nameless impulse, in a superb lofty arch the bright steel spans the foaming distance, and quivers in the life spot of the whale. Instead of sparkling water, he now spouts red blood.

“That drove the spigot out of him!” cried Stubb. “’Tis July’s immortal Fourth; all fountains must run wine today! Would now, it were old Orleans whiskey, or old Ohio, or unspeakable old Monongahela! Then, Tashtego, lad, I’d have ye hold a canakin to the jet, and we’d drink round it! Yea, verily, hearts alive, we’d brew choice punch in the spread of his spout-hole there, and from that live punch-bowl quaff the living stuff.”

Again and again to such gamesome talk, the dexterous dart is repeated, the spear returning to its master like a greyhound held in skilful leash. The agonized whale goes into his flurry; the tow-line is slackened, and the pitchpoler dropping astern, folds his hands, and mutely watches the monster die.

That for six thousand years—and no one knows how many ages before—the great whales should have been spouting all over the sea, and sprinkling and mistifying the gardens of the deep; and that for centuries thousands of hunters have watched at the very fountain; and yet, down to this blessed minute (fifteen and a quarter minutes past one o’clock P.M., December 16th, 1851), it should remain a problem whether these spoutings are water or vapor—this is noteworthy.

Whales have regular lungs; the Sperm’s windpipe has no connexion with the mouth; he breathes through his spiracle atop the head. Anomalous as it seems, the whale does thus live by intervals, his full hour and more at the bottom, without drawing a breath. Between ribs and spine he carries a Cretan labyrinth of vermicelli-like vessels, distended with oxygenated blood when he quits the surface—his surplus stock of vitality.

Hence that obstinacy in having his spoutings out. Unmolested, if he stays his set time and jets his set number, he will repeat them to a minute on rising. Alarm him after a few breaths, he will be dodging up to make good his allowance; not till they are told will he go down for his term. How obvious, too, that this necessity for rising exposes him to all the fatal hazards of the chase; for not by hook or by net could this vast leviathan be caught a thousand fathoms beneath the sunlight. Not so much thy skill, then, O hunter, as the great necessities that strike the victory to thee!

It has been said that the whale only breathes through his spout-hole; if it could truthfully be added that his spouts are mixed with water, then I opine we should be furnished with the reason why his sense of smell seems obliterated in him; for the only thing about him that at all answers to his nose is that identical spout-hole. But what does he want of them? No roses, no violets, no Cologne-water in the sea.

As the spouting canal is locked against water, and opens to the windpipe alone, the whale has no voice—unless you say that when he strangely rumbles, he talks through his nose. But then again, what has the whale to say? Seldom have I known any profound being that had anything to say to this world, unless forced to stammer for a living.

But why pester one with all this reasoning on the subject? You have seen him spout; then declare what the spout is; can you not tell water from air? My dear sir, in this world it is not so easy to settle these plain things. I have ever found your plain things the knottiest of all. And as for this whale spout, you might almost stand in it, and yet be undecided as to what it is precisely.

The central body is hidden in snowy mist; close by, the sea is all commotion; the drops you think you see may be mere condensation, or those few lodged in the countersunk fissure of the blow-hole, for even in calm the whale carries a small basin of water on his head. Nor is it prudent to be over-curious. Among whalemen, the spout is deemed poisonous; they try to evade it. You cannot go with your pitcher to this fountain and fill it, and bring it away. For even when coming into slight contact with the outer, vapory shreds of the jet, your skin will feverishly smart, from the acridness of the thing so touching it. And I know one, who coming into still closer contact with the spout, whether with some scientific object in view, or otherwise, I cannot say, the skin peeled off from his cheek and arm. And if spouted fairly into your eyes, it will blind you. Best let this deadly fountain alone.

Still we may hypothesize. Mine is this: the spout is nothing but mist. And I am impelled to it by considerations touching the whale’s inherent dignity and sublimity—never found on soundings, nor near shores; both ponderous and profound. From the heads of all ponderous profound beings—Plato, Pyrrho, the Devil, Jupiter, Dante—there goes up a certain semi-visible steam while thinking deep thoughts. While composing a treatise on Eternity, I placed a mirror before me and saw a curious worming and undulation in the atmosphere over my head; the invariable moisture of my hair, after six cups of hot tea in my thin-shingled attic, of an August noon, seems further argument.

How nobly it raises our conceit of the mighty, misty monster to behold him solemnly sailing a calm tropical sea, his vast, mild head overhung by a canopy of vapor, engendered by his incommunicable contemplations, and that vapor—as you will sometimes see it—glorified by a rainbow, as if Heaven had put its seal upon his thoughts. For, d’ye see, rainbows do not visit the clear air; they only irradiate vapor. And through all the thick mists of my doubts, divine intuitions now and then shoot, enkindling my fog with a heavenly ray. Doubts of all things earthly, and intuitions of some things heavenly; this combination makes neither believer nor infidel, but makes a man who regards them both with equal eye.

Other poets have warbled the praises of the soft eye of the antelope, and the lovely plumage of the bird that never alights; less celestial, I celebrate a tail.

Reckon the largest Sperm Whale’s tail from where the trunk tapers to a man’s girth; its upper surface alone covers fifty square feet. The compact round root expands into two broad, flat palms or flukes, shoaling to less than an inch; at the crotch they slightly overlap, then recede like wings, leaving a vacancy. In no living thing are lines of beauty more exquisitely defined than in their crescentic borders.

Seemingly a dense web of welded sinews, it is composed of three strata: upper and lower fibres long and horizontal; the middle short, running crosswise, like the thin Roman tile-courses that give masonry its strength. Moreover, the whole bulk is knit with warp and woof of muscular fibres running down and blending into the flukes, so that in the tail the measureless force of the whale is concentrated to a point. Could annihilation be to matter, this were the thing to do it.

Nor does this amazing strength tend to cripple the graceful flexion of its motions; where infantileness of ease undulates through a Titanism of power. On the contrary, those motions derive their most appalling beauty from it. Real strength never impairs beauty, but often bestows it; and in everything imposingly beautiful, strength has much to do with the magic. Take away the tied tendons that all over seem bursting from the marble in the carved Hercules, and its charm would be gone. As devout Eckerman lifted the sheet from the naked corpse of Goethe, he was overwhelmed with the massive chest of the man, that seemed as a Roman triumphal arch. When Angelo paints even God the Father in human form, mark what robustness is there. And whatever they may reveal of the divine love in the Son, the soft, curled, hermaphroditical Italian pictures, in which his idea has been most successfully embodied—so destitute as they are of all brawniness—hint nothing of any power, but the mere negative, feminine one of submission and endurance, the peculiar practical virtues of his teachings. Such is the subtle elasticity of the organ I treat of, that whether wielded in sport, or in earnest, or in anger, whatever be the mood it be in, its flexions are invariably marked by exceeding grace.

Five great motions are peculiar to it. First, propulsion: being horizontal, it never wriggles—wriggling is a sign of inferiority; scroll-wise coiled beneath, then sprung back, it gives that darting leap; side-fins only steer. Second, as a mace in battle: in striking at a boat he curves away, the blow inflicted by recoil; in unobstructed air the stroke is irresistible—your salvation, elude it; through water it cracks a rib, dashes a plank or two. Third, sweeping: there seems a delicacy of touch concentrated here, a maidenly gentleness, the palms moving side to side; feel but a sailor’s whisker—woe to whiskers and all. Fourth, lobtailing: unbent from dignity, kitten-like he plays; broad palms flirt high, then smite—the thunderous concussion resounds for miles; you would think a great gun had been discharged; and if you noticed the light wreath of vapor from the spiracle at his other extremity, you would think that that was the smoke from the touch-hole. Fifth, peaking flukes: about to plunge, his entire flukes with thirty feet of body are tossed erect, vibrating a moment, then shoot from sight. Excepting the sublime breach, this is perhaps the grandest sight in nature—Out of the bottomless profundities the gigantic tail seems spasmodically snatching at the highest heaven. So in dreams, have I seen majestic Satan thrusting forth his tormented colossal claw from the flame Baltic of Hell. In gazing at such scenes, it is all in all what mood you are in; if in the Dantean, the devils will occur to you; if in that of Isaiah, the archangels. Standing once at sunrise, I saw a herd all heading east, flukes peaked and for a moment vibrating in concert—never in Persia, home of the fire-worshippers, was such an embodiment of adoration. As Ptolemy testified of the elephant, I then testified of the whale, pronouncing him the most devout of beings.

Let no chance comparison place tail and elephant’s trunk on equality. As the mightiest elephant is but a terrier to Leviathan, so his trunk is but the stalk of a lily to these flukes; the direst trunk-blow is a fan’s tap beside the crush of the ponderous flukes, which have hurled boats and crews into the air.

The more I consider this mighty tail, the more I deplore my inability to express it; at times its gestures, which would grace a human hand, remain inexplicable. In herds, remarkable are these mystic motions; hunters have declared them akin to Free-Mason signs; that the whale, by these methods, intelligently conversed. Dissect him how I may, I but go skin-deep; I know him not, and never will. If I know not even the tail, how understand his head?—much more, how comprehend his face, when face he has none? Thou shalt see my back parts, my tail, he seems to say, but my face shall not be seen. But I cannot completely make out his back parts; and hint what he will about his face, I say again he has no face.

South-eastward the long peninsula of Malacca runs, and from it in file stretch Sumatra, Java, Bally, and Timor—a vast green rampart, pierced here and there by sally-ports for ships and whales; those narrow Straits of Sunda divide Sumatra from Java, buttressed by bold green Java Head like the central gateway to some walled empire: and considering the inexhaustible wealth of spices, and silks, and jewels, and gold, and ivory, with which the thousand islands of that oriental sea are enriched, it seems a significant provision of nature, that such treasures, by the very formation of the land, should at least bear the appearance, however ineffectual, of being guarded from the all-grasping western world. No fortresses frown there; the Orientals do not demand lowered topsails, yet they do not renounce more solid tribute.

With a fair wind the Pequod drew nigh these straits; Ahab purposed thence to the Javan sea, then northward by Sperm-Whale waters, inshore by the Philippines, and so to Japan for the great season; and after circling the known grounds, to descend upon the Line, where, foiled elsewhere, he firmly counted upon giving battle to Moby Dick. But how now? in this zoned quest, does Ahab touch no land? does his crew drink air? Surely, he will stop for water. Nay. For a long time, now, the circus-running sun has raced within his fiery ring, and needs no sustenance but what’s in himself. So Ahab. Mark this, too, in the whaler. While other hulls are loaded down with alien stuff, to be transferred to foreign wharves; the world-wandering whale-ship carries no cargo but herself and crew, their weapons and their wants. She is ballasted with utilities; not altogether with unusable pig-lead and kentledge. She carries years’ water in her—clear old prime Nantucket water; so that though other ships touch a score of ports, the world-wandering whaler may not sight one grain of soil. Tell them a new flood had come—“Well, boys, here’s the ark!”

Many Sperm Whales had been captured off Java; yet though the palmy cliffs loomed to starboard and the air smelt of fresh cinnamon, not a jet was descried—till at last, hailed from aloft, a spectacle of singular magnificence saluted us. Of late, hunted over all oceans, Sperm Whales now often herd in immense caravans; thus you may sail for weeks without a spout, and then be suddenly saluted by what seems thousands on thousands.

Broad on both bows, two or three miles off, in a vast semicircle, a continuous chain of jets up-played and sparkled; not the Right Whale’s split boughs, but the Sperm Whale’s single forward-slanting curled bush of mist. Rising on a sea-hill, we beheld that host of vapory spouts, through a bluish haze, like a thousand cheerful chimneys of some dense metropolis descried on a balmy autumnal morning. As armies hastening through a mountain defile, so did this fleet seem hurrying the Straits; their wings contracting, they swam on in a solid crescentic centre.

Crowding all sail, we pressed after; and who could tell whether, in that congregated caravan, Moby Dick himself might not temporarily be swimming, like the worshipped white-elephant in the coronation procession of the Siamese! So with stun-sail piled on stun-sail, we sailed along, driving these leviathans before us; when, of a sudden, the voice of Tashtego was heard, loudly directing attention to something in our wake. Corresponding to the crescent in our van, we beheld another astern—detached white vapors hovering like spouts that did not quite come and go. Ahab levelled his glass, wheeled upon his pivot—“Aloft there, rig whips and buckets to wet the sails;—Malays, sir, and after us!”

But the swift Pequod, herself in hot chase, found these tawny philanthropists but riding-whips and rowels. Pacing to and fro, Ahab in one turn beheld the monsters he chased; in the other, the bloodthirsty pirates chasing him; and glancing on the green walls of the watery defile—the gate to his vengeance—he was both chasing and being chased to his deadly end. When these conceits had passed, his brow was left gaunt and ribbed, like a black sand beach gnawn by stormy tide.

The crew troubled little; steadily dropping the pirates astern, we shot by Cockatoo Point and emerged upon broad waters. Still driving in the whales’ wake, at length they seemed abating; we neared them; the wind dying, word was passed to spring to the boats. But no sooner did the herd, notified by some wonderful instinct of the keels after them, rally and form in close battalions, their spouts like flashing lines of stacked bayonets, than they moved on with redoubled velocity.

Stripped to shirts and drawers we sprang to the white-ash. After hours’ pulling we were for renouncing the chase, when a general pausing commotion showed them gallied. The compact columns broke into rout—like King Porus’ elephants in the Indian battle with Alexander, they seemed going mad with consternation—expanding in vast irregular circles, aimlessly swimming, their short thick spoutings betraying panic; some, paralysed, floated like dismantled water-logged ships. Though banding together in tens of thousands, the lion-maned buffaloes of the West have fled before a solitary horseman. Witness, too, all human beings, how when herded together in the sheepfold of a theatre’s pit, they will, at the slightest alarm of fire, rush helter-skelter for the outlets, crowding, trampling, jamming, and remorselessly dashing each other to death. Best, therefore, withhold any amazement at the strangely gallied whales before us, for there is no folly of the beasts of the earth which is not infinitely outdone by the madness of men.

The herd, though much in motion, neither advanced nor retreated, but as a whole remained; the boats separated for lone whales on the outskirts. In three minutes Queequeg’s harpoon flew; the fish, running with us like light, steered straight for the heart of the herd. Thus dragged deeper and deeper into the frantic shoal, we bade adieu to circumspect life and only existed in a delirious throb. Our boat, mobbed by to-and-fro rushing monsters, was like a ship among ice-isles in tempest. Not daunted, Queequeg steered manfully; Starbuck stood in the bows, lance in hand, pricking aside whatever he could reach; the oarsmen shouted—“Hard down with your tail, there!”

All whaleboats carry druggs—crossed wooden blocks with a long line looped to a harpoon—to “wing” more whales than can be killed at once. Two we darted; the fettered whales ran off, cramped like malefactors with chain and ball. On flinging the third, the block caught under a seat, tore it out, and wounded both sides of the boat; drawers and shirts stuffed in stayed the leaks.

Advancing inward, our whale’s way diminished; the dire disorders waned. When the harpoon drew out and the tow-ward whale vanished, with his tapering momentum we glided into the innermost heart of the shoal—as if from a mountain torrent we had slid into a serene valley lake. This central expanse presented the sleek—a smooth satin-like surface; we were in the enchanted calm said to lurk at every commotion’s heart. In the distracted distance the outer circles went round and round, pods shoulder to shoulder, as if a Titanic circus-rider might arch the middle backs. Hemmed by a living wall, we must watch for a breach. Small tame cows and calves—the women and children—came snuffling like household dogs, touching our gunwales; Queequeg patted their foreheads; Starbuck scratched their backs with his lance, yet refrained from darting.

Far beneath this wondrous surface another world appeared: nursing mothers and those large with young floated suspended. The lake, as I have hinted, was to a considerable depth exceedingly transparent; and as human infants while suckling will calmly and fixedly gaze away from the breast, as if leading two different lives at the time; and while yet drawing mortal nourishment, be still spiritually feasting upon some unearthly reminiscence;—even so did the young of these whales seem looking up towards us, but not at us, as if we were but a bit of Gulfweed in their new-born sight. One little fellow, hardly a day old, some fourteen feet long, six round, frisked; his body seemed scarce yet recovered from that irksome position it had so lately occupied in the maternal reticule; where, tail to head, and all ready for the final spring, the unborn whale lies bent like a Tartar’s bow. The delicate side-fins, and the palms of his flukes, still freshly retained the plaited crumpled appearance of a baby’s ears newly arrived from foreign parts. “Line! line!” cried Queequeg; “him fast!”—and Starbuck saw long coils of Madame Leviathan’s umbilical cord, tethering the cub to its dam. Some of the subtlest secrets of the seas seemed divulged to us in this enchanted pond. We saw young Leviathan amours in the deep. The milk is very sweet and rich; it has been tasted by man; it might do well with strawberries.

And though surrounded by circle upon circle of affrights, these creatures at the centre freely and fearlessly indulged in all peaceful concernments; yea, serenely revelled in dalliance and delight. But even so, amid the tornadoed Atlantic of my being, do I myself still for ever centrally disport in mute calm; and while ponderous planets of unwaning woe revolve round me, deep down and deep inland there I still bathe me in eternal mildness of joy.

Meanwhile, frantic spectacles afar showed the other boats drugging on the frontier. At last there came one more appalling sight. A whale, hamstrung at the tail-tendon by a cutting-spade and broken away, had entangled himself in the harpoon-line; the spade, working loose, was tossed by his flailing tail, wounding and murdering his own comrades.

Recalled from fright, the herd on our lake’s margin crowded; the sleek heaved; nurseries vanished; the central circles thickened; a low hum advanced; then like the block-ice of the Hudson breaking up, the entire host came tumbling upon their inner centre, to pile in one common mountain. Instantly Starbuck took the stern—“Oars! Oars!—gripe your oars, and clutch your souls, now!—Shove him off, you Queequeg—the whale there!—prick him!—hit him! Stand up—stay so! Spring, men—pull; never mind their backs—scrape them!”

Jammed between two vast bulks like a narrow Dardanelles, we shot by desperate endeavor into a fleeting opening, watched for another, and, after many hair-breadth escapes, at last glided into what had been an outer circle, now crossed by random whales all making for one centre—Queequeg’s hat whisked clean from his head by a sudden pair of flukes.

Riotous commotion resolved itself to system; clumped in one dense body, they renewed their onward flight with augmented fleetness. Further pursuit was useless. The boats lingered to pick up drugged whales and a waifed fish of Flask’s. Yet the result proved that sagacious saying—the more whales the less fish. Of all the drugged whales only one was captured; the rest escaped for the time, to be taken, as will hereafter be seen, by some other craft than the Pequod.

Though immense herds are sometimes met, small detached bands are often observed—schools—embracing from twenty to fifty. Two sorts: those almost entirely of females, and those mustering none but young vigorous males—forty-barrel-bulls.

In cavalier attendance on the females, you invariably see one full-grown male, not old; upon alarm, he gallantly falls in the rear and covers the flight of his ladies. A luxurious Ottoman, swimming with the solaces of a harem, he is of the largest proportions, while his concubines, even at full growth, are not more than a third his bulk—comparatively delicate, indeed; I dare say, not to exceed half a dozen yards round the waist. Nevertheless, it cannot be denied, that upon the whole they are hereditarily entitled to en bon point. They promenade the Equator, cheat summer in northern seas, then anticipate coolness in Oriental waters.

If any unwarrantably pert young Leviathan presume to draw close to a lady, with what prodigious fury the Bashaw assails and chases him! Though, do what the Bashaw will, he cannot keep the most notorious Lothario out of his bed; for, alas! all fish bed in common. But if the invader betake himself away at the first rush, then the lord insinuates his vast bulk among them again and revels there awhile—like pious Solomon devoutly worshipping among his thousand concubines. And, like certain omnivorous roving lovers that might be named, my Lord Whale has no taste for the nursery, however much for the bower; and so, being a great traveller, he leaves his anonymous babies all over the world; every baby an exotic.

As youth declines and dumps increase; as reflection lends her pauses; in short, as a general lassitude overtakes the sated Turk; then a love of ease and virtue supplants the love for maidens; our Ottoman enters upon the impotent, repentant, admonitory stage of life, forswears, disbands the harem, and, grown to an exemplary, sulky old soul, goes alone among the meridians and parallels saying his prayers, warning each young Leviathan from amorous errors. Now, as the harem is called a school, so the lord is the schoolmaster—satirically so; after going to school, he preaches the folly of it. The title would seem derived from the school itself; yet some have surmised it came of Vidocq’s memoirs and what sort of country-schoolmaster that famous Frenchman was, and the occult lessons he inculcated.

The same secludedness marks all ancient Sperm Whales; almost universally a lone whale proves an aged one—like venerable moss-bearded Daniel Boone, he will have no one near him but Nature herself; and her he takes to wife in the wilderness of waters, and the best of wives she is, though she keeps so many moody secrets.

The forty-barrel-bull schools are larger and a contrast: like a mob of collegians, full of fight, fun, and wickedness, tumbling round the world at a rollicking rate; the most dangerous to encounter, save the wondrous grizzled whales that fight like fiends. Struck, a bull is deserted by all his comrades; but strike a member of the harem, and her companions swim around her with every token of concern, lingering so near and so long as themselves to fall a prey.

The waif and waif-poles necessitate some account of the whale fishery’s laws.

Though no nation has written codes (save Holland of 1695), the American fishermen have been their own legislators and lawyers, providing a system which for terse comprehensiveness surpasses Justinian’s Pandects and the By-laws of the Chinese Society for the Suppression of Meddling with other People’s Business. Yes; these laws might be engraven on a Queen Anne’s farthing:

I. A Fast-Fish belongs to the party fast to it.

II. A Loose-Fish is fair game for anybody who can soonest catch it.

But what plays the mischief with this masterly code is its admirable brevity, which necessitates a vast volume of commentaries to expound it. Alive or dead a fish is technically fast when it is connected with an occupied ship or boat by any controllable medium; likewise when it bears a waif under a plainly evinced ability and intention to take it alongside. These are scientific commentaries; but the commentaries of the whalemen themselves sometimes consist in hard words and harder knocks—the Coke-upon-Littleton of the fist.

There was a curious English whale-trover case. After a hard chase and harpooning, the plaintiffs, through peril of their lives, forsook lines and even their boat; the defendants came up, struck, killed, seized, and appropriated—retaining line, harpoons, and boat still attached. Mr. Erskine for the defendants; Lord Ellenborough judge. Erskine illustrated by a crim. con.: the husband who had once harpooned the lady and abandoned her; she became a Loose-Fish; the subsequent gentleman re-harpooning had the property, harpoon and all. The learned judge awarded the boat to the plaintiffs (abandoned to save life), but the whale, harpoons, and line to the defendants: the fish was a Loose-Fish at capture; and when the fish made off with the gear it acquired a property in it; whoever afterwards took the fish had right to them.

Ploughed to the primary rock, these twin laws are the fundamentals of all human jurisprudence; for notwithstanding its complicated tracery of sculpture, the Temple of the Law, like the Temple of the Philistines, has but two props to stand on. Is it not a saying in every one’s mouth, Possession is half of the law: that is, regardless of how the thing came into possession? But often possession is the whole of the law.

What are the sinews and souls of Russian serfs and Republican slaves but Fast-Fish? What to the rapacious landlord is the widow’s last mite but a Fast-Fish? What is yonder undetected villain’s marble mansion with a door-plate for a waif; what is that but a Fast-Fish? What is the ruinous discount which Mordecai, the broker, gets from poor Woebegone, the bankrupt, on a loan to keep Woebegone’s family from starvation; what is that ruinous discount but a Fast-Fish? What is the Archbishop of Savesoul’s income of £100,000 seized from the scant bread and cheese of hundreds of thousands of broken-backed laborers (all sure of heaven without any of Savesoul’s help) what is that globular £100,000 but a Fast-Fish? What are the Duke of Dunder’s hereditary towns and hamlets but Fast-Fish? What to that redoubted harpooneer, John Bull, is poor Ireland, but a Fast-Fish? What to that apostolic lancer, Brother Jonathan, is Texas but a Fast-Fish? And concerning all these, is not Possession the whole of the law?

If the doctrine of Fast-Fish be widely applicable, the kindred doctrine of Loose-Fish is still more so—internationally and universally. What was America in 1492 but a Loose-Fish, in which Columbus struck the Spanish standard by way of waifing it for his royal master and mistress? What was Poland to the Czar? What Greece to the Turk? What India to England? What at last will Mexico be to the United States? All Loose-Fish.

What are the Rights of Man and the Liberties of the World but Loose-Fish? What all men’s minds and opinions but Loose-Fish? What is the principle of religious belief in them but a Loose-Fish? What to the ostentatious smuggling verbalists are the thoughts of thinkers but Loose-Fish? What is the great globe itself but a Loose-Fish? And what are you, reader, but a Loose-Fish and a Fast-Fish, too?

“De balena vero sufficit, si rex habeat caput, et regina caudam.”—Bracton. That is: of all whales captured on England’s coast, the King, as Honorary Grand Harpooneer, must have the head, and the Queen the tail—a division much like halving an apple; no remainder. Being still in force, and anomalous to Fast- and Loose-Fish, it is here noted.

Certain mariners of the Cinque Ports killed and beached a fine whale. Now the Cinque Ports are under the jurisdiction of a sort of beadle, called a Lord Warden. By some writers this office is called a sinecure. But not so. Because the Lord Warden is busily employed at times in fobbing his perquisites; which are his chiefly by virtue of that same fobbing of them. Now when these poor sun-burnt mariners, bare-footed, and with their trowsers rolled high up on their eely legs, had wearily hauled their fat fish high and dry, promising themselves £150 from the precious oil and bone; and in fantasy sipping their tea and ale upon the strength of their shares; up steps a very learned gentleman, with a copy of Blackstone under his arm; and laying it upon the whale’s head, he says—“Hands off! this fish, my masters, is a Fast-Fish. I seize it as the Lord Warden’s.” Upon this the poor mariners in their respectful consternation—so truly English—knowing not what to say, fall to vigorously scratching their heads all round; meanwhile ruefully glancing from the whale to the stranger. “Please, sir, who is the Lord Warden?”—“The Duke.” “But the Duke had nothing to do with taking this fish.”—“It is his.” “We have been at great trouble, peril, expense—are all to go to the Duke’s benefit?”—“It is his.” “Is the Duke so very poor?”—“It is his.” “I thought to relieve my old bed-ridden mother by part of my share of this whale.”—“It is his.” “Won’t the Duke be content with a quarter or a half?”—“It is his.” In a word, the whale was seized and sold; his Grace the Duke of Wellington received the money. Thinking the case rather hard, an honest clergyman wrote begging consideration for the mariners. His Grace replied he had already done so, received the money, and would be obliged if the reverend gentleman would decline meddling with other people’s business. Is this the still militant old man, standing at the corners of the three kingdoms, on all hands coercing alms of beggars?

Plowdon gives the reason: the whale belongs to King and Queen “because of its superior excellence.” But why should the King have the head, and the Queen the tail? William Prynne, in “Queen-Gold,” says, “Ye tail is ye Queen’s, that ye Queen’s wardrobe may be supplied with ye whalebone.” A sagacious lawyer’s sad mistake; the black limber bone is in the head, not the tail. Is the Queen a mermaid, to be presented with a tail? Allegory may lurk.

Two royal fish the law styles—the whale and the sturgeon—both royal property under certain limitations. By inference, the sturgeon must be divided as the whale; the King receiving the highly dense elastic head—congeniality symbolical, perhaps. There is a reason in all things, even in law.

“In vain it was to rake for Ambergriese in the paunch of this Leviathan, insufferable fetor denying not inquiry.”—Sir T. Browne. A week or two after the last lowering, as we sailed over a sleepy, vapory, mid-day sea, the many noses on the Pequod’s deck proved more vigilant than the three pairs of eyes aloft. A peculiar, not very pleasant smell was smelt. “Some of those drugged whales we tickled the other day,” said Stubb; “I thought they would keel up.”

The vapors slid aside; a ship lay becalmed; and by the eddying vulture sea-fowl, it was plain the whale alongside was blasted—died and floated unappropriated. It may well be conceived what an unsavory odor such a mass must exhale; worse than an Assyrian city in the plague, when the living are incompetent to bury the departed. So intolerable indeed is it regarded by some, that no cupidity could persuade them to moor alongside of it. Yet are there those who will still do it; notwithstanding the fact that the oil obtained from such subjects is of a very inferior quality, and by no means of the nature of attar-of-rose. Coming nearer, the Frenchman had a second fish—one of those dyspeptic whales that die prodigiously indigested; almost bankrupt of oil, yet not to be turned up by a knowing fisherman.

Stubb vowed he recognised his cutting spade-pole entangled round one whale’s tail. “There’s a Crappo content with our leavings—the drugged whale—and scraping dry bones of the other. Poor devil! Pass a hat; make him a present of a little oil. For what oil he’ll get from that drugged whale there, wouldn’t be fit to burn in a jail; no, not in a condemned cell. And as for the other whale, why, I’ll agree to get more oil by chopping up and trying out these three masts of ours, than he’ll get from that bundle of bones. Though—” and he sniffed—“maybe there’s ambergris. Yes, I’m for it.”

Stubb called his crew and pulled for the stranger. Drawing across her bow, he perceived that, in accordance with fanciful French taste, the upper part of her stem-piece was carved in the likeness of a huge drooping stalk, was painted green, and for thorns had copper spikes projecting from it here and there; the whole terminating in a symmetrical folded bulb of a bright red colour. Upon her head boards, in large gilt letters, he read “Bouton de Rose,”—Rose-button, or Rose-bud; and this was the romantic name of this aromatic ship. “A wooden rose-bud, eh?” cried Stubb, hand to nose; “how like all creation it smells!”

“Bouton-de-Rose, ahoy! any of you that speak English?” “Yes,” rejoined a Guernsey-man—the chief-mate. “Have ye seen the White Whale—Moby Dick?” “Never heard of such a whale. Cachalot Blanche?—no.” Stubb trumpeted back to Ahab—“No, Sir! No!”—and returned.

The Guernsey-man, in the chains with a cutting-spade, had slung his nose in a bag. “Broke it?” said Stubb. “I wish it was broken, or I had none! What are you holding yours for?” “It’s a wax nose; I have to hold it on. Air rather gardenny; throw us a bunch of posies, Bouton-de-Rose.” The sailors in tasselled red caps worked slow and talked fast, noses like jib-booms; some ran aloft for air; others puffed pipes or held tarred oakum to their nostrils. From the round-house a fiery face thrust out—the tormented surgeon, yelling remonstrances, self-immured to avoid the pest.

The Guernsey-man detested his captain, a delicate, moustached gentleman in red velvet vest with watch-seals; a Cologne manufacturer on his first voyage; a conceited ignoramus, said the mate. Sounding him, Stubb perceived no suspicion of ambergris; so they concocted a plan—Guernsey, under cover of interpreter, to tell the Captain what he pleased as from Stubb; Stubb to utter any nonsense uppermost.

Introduced, Stubb eyed the vest and seals. “Tell him he looks babyish to me.” The Guernsey-man, in French, said instead that only yesterday a ship’s captain, chief-mate, and six sailors had died of a fever from a blasted whale. “Now,” whispered the mate. “Tell him,” said Stubb, “he’s no more fit to command a whale-ship than a St. Jago monkey. In fact, tell him from me he’s a baboon.” The interpreter said: “The other whale, the dried one, is far more deadly; as we value our lives, cut loose.” Instantly the Captain ran forward and ordered his crew to cast loose all cables and chains.

“What now?”—“Tell him I’ve diddled him.” The interpreter: “He is happy to have been of any service.” The Captain, grateful, invited Stubb below to a bottle of Bordeaux. “Thank him; tell him it’s against my principles to drink with the man I’ve diddled. If Monsieur would live another day to drink, best drop all four boats and pull the ship away; it’s so calm they won’t drift.” Stubb over the side again, offered his long tow-line and would help by towing the lighter whale off. While the French boats towed their ship one way, Stubb benevolently towed the whale the other, slacking an unusually long line. A breeze sprang; he feigned to cast off; the Frenchman increased his distance; the Pequod slid between him and Stubb’s whale. Stubb pulled to the floating body, hailed his ship, and set to reaping the fruit of his cunning.

With sharp boat-spade he excavated behind the side fin, as if digging a sea-cellar; ribs rang like Roman tiles in fat loam; his boat’s crew were all in high excitement, eagerly helping their chief, and looking as anxious as gold-hunters. And all the time numberless fowls were diving and screaming around them. The horrible nosegay increased—when suddenly, from the plague’s heart, there stole a faint perfume, flowing through the bad smells without blending, as one river within another. “I have it, I have it,” cried Stubb, striking—“a purse! a purse!” He thrust both hands and drew out handfuls like ripe Windsor soap, or rich mottled old cheese; unctuous; easily dented; a hue between yellow and ash. Ambergris—worth a guinea an ounce. Some six handfuls were obtained; more lost to sea—when impatient Ahab’s loud command bade Stubb desist and come on board, else the ship would bid them good-bye.

Now this ambergris is a very curious substance, and so important as an article of commerce, that in 1791 a certain Nantucket-born Captain Coffin was examined at the bar of the English House of Commons on that subject. Though the word ambergris is but the French compound for grey amber, yet the two substances are quite distinct. Amber is hard, transparent, brittle, odorless; found on coasts and in some inland soils; for mouth-pieces, beads, ornaments. Ambergris is soft, waxy, highly fragrant and spicy; used in perfumery, in pastiles and pomatum; the Turks use it in cooking; some drop a few grains into claret to flavor it.

Who would think, then, that such fine ladies and gentlemen should regale themselves with an essence found in the inglorious bowels of a sick whale! Yet so it is. By some, ambergris is cause; by others, effect, of the whale’s dyspepsia. How to cure such a dyspepsia it were hard to say, unless by administering three or four boat loads of Brandreth’s pills, and then running out of harm’s way, as laborers do in blasting rocks.

Certain hard, round, bony plates were found in this ambergris; Stubb took them for sailors’ trowsers buttons; they proved pieces of small squid bones embalmed.

That incorruption should be found in the heart of such decay—is this nothing? Bethink thee of St. Paul to the Corinthians—sown in dishonor, raised in glory; remember Paracelsus on what maketh the best musk; forget not that Cologne-water, in rudimental stages, is of all ill savors the worst.

I should like to conclude the chapter with the above appeal, but cannot, owing to my anxiety to repel a charge often made against whalemen. One charge to repel. It is slanderously hinted that all whales always smell bad. The stigma is traceable to the first Greenland whalemen in London, who, not trying out at sea as the Southern ships do, but thrusting fresh blubber through cask bung-holes, unload in the dock a savor like excavating an old city grave-yard for the foundations of a Lying-in Hospital. Add Schmerenburgh or Smeerenberg on Greenland’s coast—the latter name used by the learned Fogo Von Slack, in his great work on Smells, a text-book on that subject—a place for trying out the Dutch fleet’s blubber; when in full operation, no pleasant savor.

But all this is different with a South Sea Sperm Whaler; in a four years’ voyage, after filling her hold, she perhaps consumes not fifty days boiling; and casked, the oil is nearly scentless. The truth is, living or dead, if but decently treated, whales as a species are by no means of ill odor; nor can whalemen be recognised, as the people of the middle ages affected to detect a Jew in the company, by the nose. Indeed, the motion of a Sperm Whale’s flukes above water dispenses a perfume, as when a musk-scented lady rustles her dress in a warm parlor. What then liken the Sperm Whale to for fragrance, considering his magnitude? To that famous elephant, with jewelled tusks, redolent with myrrh, led out of an Indian town to do honor to Alexander the Great.

It was but some few days after encountering the Frenchman, that a most significant event befell the most insignificant of the Pequod’s crew; an event most lamentable; and which ended in providing the sometimes madly merry and predestinated craft with a living and ever accompanying prophecy of whatever shattered sequel might prove her own. Little Pip, the tambourine boy—over tender-hearted, loving life and its peaceable securities—had unaccountably been entrapped into the panic-striking business.

Some few hands are reserved as ship-keepers; so it was with the little negro Pippin by nick-name, Pip by abbreviation. In outer aspect he matched hapless Dough-Boy like a black pony to a white; but while Dough-Boy was dull and torpid, Pip, though over tender-hearted, was at bottom very bright, with that pleasant, genial, jolly brightness peculiar to his tribe. Nor smile so, while I write that this little black was brilliant, for even blackness has its brilliancy. But Pip loved life and all life’s peaceable securities; so that his brightness was sadly blurred; though what was thus temporarily subdued in him was destined to be luridly illumined by strange wild fires. So, as when the cunning jeweller, laying a pure diamond against a gloomy ground and lighting it by unnatural gases, brings out infernal effulgences, till the evil-blazing gem looks like some crown-jewel stolen from the King of Hell. But let us to the story.

Stubb’s after-oarsman having sprained his hand, Pip was put in his place. The first lowering he only trembled and escaped close contact with the whale; the second, when the struck fish gave its customary rap right under his seat, he leaped overboard, and the slack line, sweeping his chest, entangled him; the whale started; and presto! poor Pip came all foaming up to the chocks, dragged by the strangling line round chest and neck. Tashtego snatched the boat-knife—“Cut?” Meantime Pip’s blue, choked face plainly looked, Do, for God’s sake! All passed in a flash. In less than half a minute, this entire thing happened. “Damn him, cut!” roared Stubb; the whale was lost, and Pip was saved.

So soon as he recovered himself, the poor little negro was assailed by yells and execrations from the crew. Tranquilly permitting these irregular cursings to evaporate, Stubb then in a plain, business-like, but still half humorous manner, cursed Pip officially; and that done, unofficially gave him much wholesome advice. The substance was, Never jump from a boat, Pip, except—but all the rest was indefinite, as the soundest advice ever is. Now, in general, _Stick to the boat_, is your true motto in whaling; but cases will sometimes happen when _Leap from the boat_, is still better. Moreover, as if perceiving at last that if he should give undiluted conscientious advice to Pip, he would be leaving him too wide a margin to jump in for the future; Stubb suddenly dropped all advice, and concluded with a peremptory command: “Stick to the boat, Pip, or by the Lord, I won’t pick you up if you jump; mind that. We can’t afford to lose whales by the likes of you; a whale would sell for thirty times what you would, Pip, in Alabama.” Hereby perhaps Stubb indirectly hinted, that though man loved his fellow, yet man is a money-making animal, which propensity too often interferes with his benevolence.

But we are all in the hands of the Gods; and Pip jumped again. This time he did not breast out the line; and when the whale ran, Pip was left astern, like a hurried traveller’s trunk. A bright, blue day; the horizon hammered thin as gold-beater’s skin; bobbing there, Pip’s ebon head showed like a head of cloves. No boat-knife was lifted; Stubb’s inexorable back was turned; the whale was winged; and in three minutes a mile of shoreless ocean widened round Pip’s miserable horizon. Out from the centre of the sea, poor Pip turned his crisp, curling, black head to the sun, another lonely castaway, though the loftiest and the brightest.

Now, in calm weather, to swim in the open ocean is as easy to the practised swimmer as to ride in a spring-carriage ashore. But the awful lonesomeness is intolerable. The intense concentration of self in the middle of such a heartless immensity, my God! who can tell it?

But had Stubb really abandoned the poor little negro to his fate? No; he did not mean to, at least. There were boats in his wake, and he supposed they would quickly pick Pip up; but those boats, suddenly spying whales, turned and gave chase; and Stubb’s boat was now so far away that Pip’s ringed horizon expanded miserably. By the merest chance the ship itself at last rescued him; but from that hour the little negro went about the deck an idiot—so, at least, they said he was. The sea had jeeringly kept his finite body up, but drowned the infinite of his soul. Not drowned entirely; rather carried down alive to wondrous depths, where strange shapes of the unwarped primal world glided before his passive eyes; and the miser-merman, Wisdom, revealed his hoarded heaps; and among the joyous, heartless, ever-juvenile eternities, Pip saw the multitudinous, God-omnipresent, coral insects that out of the firmament of waters heaved the colossal orbs. He saw God’s foot upon the treadle of the loom, and spoke it; therefore they called him mad. So man’s insanity is heaven’s sense; and wandering from all mortal reason, man comes at last to that celestial thought which, to reason, is absurd and frantic; and weal or woe, feels then uncompromised, indifferent as his God.

For the rest, blame not Stubb too hardly; the thing is common in that fishery; and in the sequel of the narrative, it will be seen what like abandonment befell myself.

Stubb’s whale was brought to the Pequod’s side; the cutting and hoisting done. The sperm cooled and crystallized so that, sitting cross-legged before a great bath of it, I found soft lumps rolling in the liquid part. Our business was to squeeze them back to fluid. A sweet and unctuous duty! Such a clearer! such a sweetener! such a softener! such a delicious molifier! After but a few minutes, my fingers felt like eels, serpentine and spiralising.

Under a blue tranquil sky; the ship under indolent sail, gliding serenely; my hands bathing among those gentle globules; as they broke to my touch and discharged all their opulence, like fully ripe grapes their wine; as I snuffed that uncontaminated aroma,—literally and truly, like the smell of spring violets; I declare to you, that for the time I lived as in a musky meadow; I forgot all about our horrible oath; in that inexpressible sperm, I washed my hands and my heart of it; I almost began to credit the old Paracelsan superstition that sperm is of rare virtue in allaying the heat of anger; while bathing in that bath, I felt divinely free from all ill-will, or petulance, or malice, of any sort whatsoever.

Squeeze! squeeze! squeeze! all the morning long; I squeezed that sperm till I almost melted into it; till a strange sort of insanity came over me; and I unwittingly squeezed my co-laborers’ hands, mistaking them for the gentle globules. Such an abounding, affectionate, friendly, loving feeling did this avocation beget; that at last I was continually squeezing their hands, and looking up into their eyes sentimentally; as much as to say,—Oh! my dear fellow beings, why should we longer cherish any social acerbities, or know the slightest ill-humor or envy! Come; let us squeeze hands all round; nay, let us all squeeze ourselves into each other; let us squeeze ourselves universally into the very milk and sperm of kindness.

Would that I could keep squeezing that sperm for ever! For now, since by many prolonged, repeated experiences, I have perceived that in all cases man must eventually lower, or at least shift, his conceit of attainable felicity; not placing it anywhere in the intellect or the fancy; but in the wife, the heart, the bed, the table, the saddle, the fireside, the country; now that I have perceived all this, I am ready to squeeze case eternally. In thoughts of the visions of the night, I saw long rows of angels in paradise, each with his hands in a jar of spermaceti.

Now, while discoursing of sperm, it behooves to speak of other things akin to it, in the business of preparing the sperm whale for the try-works. White-horse; plum-pudding—of an exceedingly rich, mottled tint, with a bestreaked snowy and golden ground, dotted with spots of the deepest crimson and purple; it is plums of rubies, in pictures of citron. Spite of reason, it is hard to keep yourself from eating it. I confess, that once I stole behind the foremast to try it; it tasted something as I should conceive a royal cutlet from the thigh of Louis le Gros might have tasted, supposing him to have been killed the first day after the venison season, and that particular venison season contemporary with an unusually fine vintage of the vineyards of Champagne. Slobgollion—an ineffably oozy, stringy affair after much squeezing; gurry—the right-whalemen’s dark glue; and the whalemen’s nippers—leathern squilgees of tail—all pass through the blubber-room, where, by lantern’s gloom, the pike-and-gaffman and the shoeless spade-man hew the sheets—a scene where toes are scarce among veterans.

Had you stepped aboard the Pequod at a certain juncture of this post-mortemizing, and strolled forward nigh the windlass, you had scanned, lying lengthwise in the lee scuppers, a strange, enigmatical object. Not the wondrous cistern in the whale’s huge head; not the prodigy of his unhinged lower jaw; not the miracle of his symmetrical tail; none of these would so surprise you, as half a glimpse of that unaccountable cone,—longer than a Kentuckian is tall, nigh a foot in diameter at the base, and jet-black as Yojo, the ebony idol of Queequeg. And an idol, indeed, it is; or, rather, in old times, its likeness was. Such an idol as that found in the secret groves of Queen Maachah in Judea; and for worshipping which, King Asa, her son, did depose her, and destroyed the idol, and burnt it for an abomination at the brook Kedron, as darkly set forth in the 15th chapter of the First Book of Kings.

Look at the sailor called the mincer, who, with two allies, backs the grandissimus, as the mariners call it, and staggers off with it; laid upon the forecastle, he cylindrically removes its dark pelt, as an African hunter the pelt of a boa; then turns it inside out like a pantaloon-leg, gives it a stretching till near doubled, and hangs it in the rigging to dry. Taken down, he lops a length, cuts two slits for arm-holes at the broad end, and lengthwise slips himself bodily into it. The mincer now stands invested in the full canonicals of his calling. Immemorial to all his order, this investiture alone will adequately protect him, while employed in the peculiar functions of his office.

That office consists in mincing the horse-pieces of blubber for the pots; performed at a wooden horse against the bulwarks, with a tub beneath, into which the slices drop, fast as the sheets from a rapt orator’s desk. Arrayed in decent black; occupying a conspicuous pulpit; intent on bible leaves—what a candidate for an archbishopric; what a lad for a Pope, were this mincer!

“Bible leaves! Bible leaves!”—so cry the mates, bidding him cut as thin as may be, that boiling may be sped, the yield increased, and, it may be, the quality refined.

Outwardly, an American whaler is marked by her try-works—brick and mortar joining oak and hemp, as if a brick-kiln were shipped to sea. Between fore and main, upon strong timbers, rises the mass, cased with wood; remove the battened hatch, and within two vast try-pots shine like silver punch-bowls when polished with soapstone and sand. Beneath, the furnaces’ iron mouths open.

About nine at night, the Pequod’s try-works were first started. Stubb oversaw. First wood, then the whale’s own crisp scraps fed the flames. Like a plethoric burning martyr, the whale supplies his own fuel and burns by his own body; would that he consumed his own smoke! for his smoke is horrible to inhale, and inhale it you must, and not only that, but you must live in it for the time. It has an unspeakable, wild, Hindoo odor about it, such as may lurk in the vicinity of funereal pyres. It smells like the left wing of the day of judgment; it is an argument for the pit.

By midnight all was in full operation; clear of the carcass; sail made; wind freshening; the ocean’s darkness intense; but licked up by fierce flames forking from the sooty flues, emblazoning the rigging like Greek fire. The hatch removed made a broad hearth: there the pagan harpooneers, stokers of the whale-ship, with pronged poles pitched hissing blubber into the scalding pots; the oil leaped to each pitch; the watch lounged by the windlass, their tawny faces, matted beards, barbaric teeth in the capricious glow. As they narrated to each other their unholy adventures, their tales of terror told in words of mirth; as their uncivilized laughter forked upwards out of them, like the flames from the furnace; as to and fro, in their front, the harpooneers wildly gesticulated with their huge pronged forks; as the wind howled on, and the sea leaped, and the ship groaned and dived, and yet steadfastly shot her red hell further and further into the blackness of the sea and the night, and scornfully champed the white bone in her mouth, and viciously spat round her on all sides; then the rushing Pequod, freighted with savages, and laden with fire, and burning a corpse, and plunging into that blackness of darkness, seemed the material counterpart of her monomaniac commander’s soul.

So seemed it to me at the helm; long hours I silently guided the way of this fire-ship. Wrapped, for that interval, in darkness myself, I the better saw the redness, the madness, the ghastliness of others. The continual sight of the fiend shapes before me, capering half in smoke and half in fire, these at last begat kindred visions in my soul, so soon as I began to yield to that unaccountable drowsiness which ever would come over me at a midnight helm. Starting from a brief standing sleep, I felt something fatally wrong. The tiller smote my side; the sails began to shake; yet no compass glimmered before me—only jet gloom and ghastly flashes. Uppermost, that what I stood on rushed from all havens astern. Convulsively I grasped the tiller, crazily conceiving it inverted—till, lo! I had in sleep turned my body, fronting the stern, my back to prow and compass. I faced about in the instant, and saved the ship from flying up into the wind.

Look not too long in the face of the fire, O man! Never dream with thy hand on the helm! Turn not thy back to the compass; accept the first hint of the hitching tiller; believe not the artificial fire, when its redness makes all things ghastly. To-morrow, in the natural sun, the skies will be bright; those who glared like devils in the forking flames, the morn will show in far other, at least gentler, relief; the glorious, golden, glad sun, the only true lamp—all others but liars!

Nevertheless, the sun hides not Virginia’s Dismal Swamp, nor Rome’s Campagna, nor Sahara, nor the ocean—dark side of the earth. So, therefore, that mortal man who hath more of joy than sorrow in him, that mortal man cannot be true—not true, or undeveloped. With books the same. The truest of all men was the Man of Sorrows, and the truest of all books is Solomon’s, and Ecclesiastes is the fine hammered steel of woe. “All is vanity.” ALL. This wilful world hath not got hold of unchristian Solomon’s wisdom yet. But he who dodges hospitals and jails, and walks fast crossing graveyards, and would rather talk of operas than hell; calls Cowper, Young, Pascal, Rousseau, poor devils all of sick men; and throughout a care-free lifetime swears by Rabelais as passing wise, and therefore jolly;—not that man is fitted to sit down on tomb-stones, and break the green damp mould with unfathomably wondrous Solomon.

But even Solomon, he says, “the man that wandereth out of the way of understanding shall remain” (i.e., even while living) “in the congregation of the dead.” Give not thyself up, then, to fire, lest it invert thee, deaden thee; as for the time it did me. There is a wisdom that is woe; but there is a woe that is madness. And there is a Catskill eagle in some souls, that can alike dive into the blackest gorges, and soar out again into the sunny spaces; and even if he for ever flies within the gorge, that gorge is in the mountains; so that even in his lowest swoop the mountain eagle is still higher than other birds upon the plain, even though they soar.

Had you descended from the Pequod’s try-works to the forecastle, you would have thought yourself in some illuminated shrine of canonized kings and counsellors: they lay in their triangular vaults, each mariner a chiselled muteness; a score of lamps flashing upon his hooded eyes.

In merchantmen, oil for the sailor is more scarce than the milk of queens. To dress in the dark, and eat in the dark, and stumble in darkness to his pallet—this is his usual lot. But the whaleman, as he seeks the food of light, so he lives in light. He makes his berth an Aladdin’s lamp, and lays him down in it; so that in the pitchiest night the ship’s black hull still houses an illumination.

See with what entire freedom the whaleman takes his handful of lamps—often but old bottles and vials, though—to the copper cooler at the try-works, and replenishes them there, as mugs of ale at a vat. He burns, too, the purest of oil, in its unmanufactured and, therefore, unvitiated state—a fluid unknown to solar, lunar, or astral contrivances ashore—sweet as early grass butter in April. He hunts for his oil, to be sure of its freshness and genuineness, even as the traveller on the prairie hunts up his own supper of game.

Already has it been related how the great leviathan is afar off descried from the mast-head; how he is chased over the watery moors, and slaughtered in the valleys of the deep; how he is then towed alongside and beheaded; and how (on the principle which entitled the headsman of old to the garments in which the beheaded was killed) his great padded surtout becomes the property of his executioner; how, in due time, he is condemned to the pots, and, like Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego, his spermaceti, oil, and bone pass unscathed through the fire;—but now it remains to conclude the last chapter of this part of the description by rehearsing—singing, if I may—the romantic proceeding of decanting off his oil into the casks and striking them down into the hold, where once again leviathan returns to his native profundities, sliding along beneath the surface as before; but, alas! never more to rise and blow.

While still warm, the oil, like hot punch, is received into the six-barrel casks; and while, perhaps, the ship is pitching and rolling this way and that in the midnight sea, the enormous casks are slewed round and headed over, end for end, and sometimes perilously scoot across the slippery deck, like so many land-slides, till at last man-handled and stayed in their course; and all round the hoops, rap, rap, go as many hammers as can play upon them, for now, _ex officio_, every sailor is a cooper.

At length, when the last pint is casked, and all is cool, then the great hatchways are unsealed, the bowels of the ship are thrown open, and down go the casks to their final rest in the sea. This done, the hatches are replaced, and hermetically closed, like a closet walled up.

In the sperm fishery, this is perhaps one of the most remarkable incidents in all the business of whaling. One day the planks stream with freshets of blood and oil; on the sacred quarter-deck enormous masses of the whale’s head are profanely piled; great rusty casks lie about, as in a brewery yard; the smoke from the try-works has besooted all the bulwarks; the mariners go about suffused with unctuousness; the entire ship seems great leviathan himself; while on all hands the din is deafening.

But a day or two after, you look about you in this self-same ship; and were it not for the tell-tale boats and try-works, you would all but swear you trod some silent merchant vessel, with a most scrupulously neat commander. The unmanufactured sperm oil possesses a singularly cleansing virtue; besides, from the ashes a potent lye is readily made. Hands go diligently with buckets and rags and restore the bulwarks; the great hatch is scrubbed and placed upon the try-works, completely hiding the pots; every cask is out of sight; all tackles are coiled in unseen nooks; and when this conscientious duty is concluded, the crew themselves proceed to their own ablutions; shift from top to toe; and finally issue to the immaculate deck, fresh and all aglow, as bridegrooms new-leaped from out the daintiest Holland.

Now, with elated step, they pace in twos and threes, and humorously discourse of parlors, sofas, carpets, and fine cambrics; propose to mat the deck; object not to taking tea by moonlight on the piazza of the forecastle. To hint to such musked mariners of oil, and bone, and blubber, were little short of audacity. They know not the thing you distantly allude to. Away, and bring us napkins!

But mark: aloft there, at the three mast-heads, stand three men intent on spying out more whales, which, if caught, infallibly will again soil the old oaken furniture, and drop at least one small grease-spot somewhere.
